By addressing benchmark construction, provenance disclosure, and automated overlap auditing, we aim to guide future research toward verifiable claims of progress.

</details>


### [27] [GeoSEAN: Explainable Country-Level Image Geolocation for ASEAN Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12284)
*Muhamad Syukron,Danish Rafie Ekaputra,Tintrim Dwi Ary Widhianingsih*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper presents an explainable country-level image geolocation pipeline for 11 ASEAN countries, using an MLP classifier that achieves 85.91% accuracy/F1, with post-hoc explainability via CLIP attention rollout, YOLO object detection, and EBPG metrics to analyze visual evidence behind predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image geolocation is challenging in regions where countries share similar visual characteristics (urban, roadside, architectural, environmental). Existing models focus on coordinate-level prediction or classification performance but provide limited insight into how visual evidence contributes to location predictions.

Method: (1) Collected 4,850 images from GeoGuessr, Google Images, and street-level imagery across 11 ASEAN countries. (2) Evaluated three approaches: CLIP zero-shot classification, LightGBM classifier, and MLP classifier. (3) Best-performing MLP classifier predictions were analyzed post-hoc using CLIP attention rollout, YOLO object detection, and Energy-Based Pointing Game (EBPG) overlap metrics for explainability.

Result: MLP classifier achieved best test performance with 85.91% accuracy and F1 score. Object-level analysis revealed that frequently detected objects are not necessarily associated with the highest attention density, indicating object frequency and attention-based visual evidence capture different scene aspects.

Conclusion: The proposed model supports accurate regional image geolocation while enabling object-level inspection of the visual cues underlying its predictions.

Abstract: Image geolocation aims to infer the geographic origin of an image from visual content alone. However, this task remains challenging in regions where countries share similar urban, roadside, architectural, and environmental characteristics. Many existing geolocation models focus on coordinate level prediction or classification performance while providing limited insight into how visual evidence contributes to location predictions. This study presents an explainable country level image geolocation pipeline for 11 ASEAN countries. First, we collected 4,850 images from GeoGuessr style sources, Google Images, and additional street level imagery. We then evaluated three approaches on this dataset: CLIP zero shot classification, a LightGBM classifier, and an MLP classifier. The MLP achieved the best test performance, attaining an accuracy and F1 score of 85.91%. For explainability, predictions generated by the MLP classifier were analyzed post hoc using CLIP attention rollout, YOLO26 object detection on the original images, and Energy Based Pointing Game (EBPG) overlap metrics. Object level analysis indicates that frequently detected objects are not necessarily associated with the highest attention density, suggesting that object frequency and attention based visual evidence capture different aspects of a scene. These results demonstrate that the proposed model can support accurate regional image geolocation while enabling object level inspection of the visual cues underlying its predictions.

</details>


### [28] [Semantic-Edge Response Decoding of SAM3 for Zero-Shot Crack Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12292)
*Shipeng Liu,Zhanping Song,Liang Zhao,Dengfeng Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SERD improves zero-shot crack segmentation by decoding internal semantic responses from SAM3's decoder rather than its final masks, using a lightweight edge prior and global threshold, achieving 61.14% Crack IoU (+4.63 over SAM3) without fine-tuning or annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing high-performance crack segmentation methods require pixel-level annotations and training. While vision foundation models enable zero-shot deployment, their final mask proposals perform poorly on thin, fragmented, and low-contrast cracks due to suppression, truncation, or over-expansion of crack evidence.

Method: SERD interprets internal language-conditioned semantic responses from SAM3's decoder as a dense crack-likelihood field, calibrates them with a lightweight edge prior, and generates crack masks using a unified global threshold—all without annotations or fine-tuning.

Result: On six public datasets, SERD achieves 61.14% average Crack IoU, outperforming native SAM3 by 4.63 points and surpassing other zero-shot/open-vocabulary methods. Gains primarily stem from decoding internal semantic responses, with edge calibration improving structural recovery and false-positive control.

Conclusion: For thin and non-compact targets like cracks, internal continuous responses from foundation model decoders offer a more transferable interface than final mask outputs, suggesting a paradigm shift for zero-shot segmentation of fine-grained structures.

Abstract: Crack segmentation is essential for infrastructure inspection and structural health assessment, but existing high-performance methods typically require task-specific pixel-level annotations and training. Text-promptable vision foundation models enable zero-shot deployment, yet their final mask proposals are poorly suited to thin, fragmented, and low-contrast cracks, whose evidence may be suppressed, truncated, or over-expanded during mask generation. We find that language-conditioned semantic responses within the SAM3 decoder preserve more continuous and complete crack evidence than its final masks. Based on this observation, we propose Semantic-Edge Response Decoding (SERD), which interprets internal responses as a dense crack-likelihood field, calibrates them with a lightweight edge prior, and generates crack masks using a unified global threshold, without annotation or fine-tuning. Experiments on six public datasets show that SERD consistently improves over native SAM3 and outperforms the compared zero-shot and open-vocabulary segmentation methods, achieving an average Crack IoU of 61.14\%, 4.63 points higher than SAM3. Further analyses show that most gains arise from directly decoding internal semantic responses, while edge calibration improves structural recovery and false-positive control without increasing end-to-end inference overhead. These results suggest that, for thin and non-compact targets, internal continuous responses can provide a more transferable interface than the final masks of foundation models. Code is available at: https://github.com/xauat-liushipeng/SERD

</details>


### [29] [Adaptive Cross-Modal Fusion with Sparse Attention for Pedestrian Crossing Intention Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12293)
*Md Mahfuzur Rahman,Pengzhan Zhou,A F M Abdun Noor,Md Imam Ahasan,Kah Ong Michael Goh,S. M. Hasan Mahmud,Md Mustafizur Rahman,Kaixin Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ADAPT is a multimodal transformer framework for pedestrian crossing intention prediction that jointly models visual (RGB, depth, semantic maps) and kinematic (speed, bounding boxes, skeleton pose) data using specialized attention and Mamba-based modules, achieving state-of-the-art results on JAAD and PIE benchmarks with 17.23 ms inference per sample.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pedestrian crossing prediction methods rely on single-modal inputs or dense multimodal fusion that inadequately captures complementary visual-kinematic information while introducing redundant inter-modal interactions, motivating a more efficient and selective multimodal framework.

Method: ADAPT uses five specialized modules: (1) a weight-shared Swin Transformer V2 backbone for visual feature extraction from four aligned modalities (RGB, local depth, global semantic map, global depth); (2) Cross-Modality Guided Attention for hierarchical visual fusion; (3) a Mamba-based Motion Feature Encoding module for efficient temporal modeling; (4) a Sparse Cross-Modal Attention module for selective inter-modal interaction; and (5) a Vision Transformer-based Temporal Feature Fusion module for sequence-level prediction.

Result: On JAAD: AUC 0.73 (JAADbeh) and 0.85 (JAADall). On PIE: accuracy 0.92 and AUC 0.90. Inference time: 17.23 ms per sample. Outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods with low computational complexity.

Conclusion: ADAPT achieves an effective balance between predictive accuracy and real-time deployment efficiency, making it suitable for intelligent transportation and autonomous driving applications.

Abstract: Predicting pedestrian crossing intention is a safety-critical task for autonomous driving, yet existing approaches often rely on single-modal inputs or dense multimodal fusion strategies that inadequately capture complementary visual and kinematic information while introducing redundant inter-modal interactions. We propose ADAPT (Adaptive Domain-Aware Pedestrian Crossing Transformer), a multimodal framework that jointly models local and global visual context together with temporal motion dynamics for accurate pedestrian crossing intention prediction. ADAPT processes four spatially aligned visual modalities, including RGB images, local depth maps, global semantic maps, and global depth maps, together with ego-vehicle speed, pedestrian bounding boxes, and skeleton pose information through five specialized modules: a weight-shared Swin Transformer V2 backbone for visual feature extraction, a Cross-Modality Guided Attention module for hierarchical visual fusion, a Mamba-based Motion Feature Encoding module for efficient temporal modeling, a Sparse Cross-Modal Attention module that selectively preserves the most informative inter-modal interactions, and a Vision Transformer-based Temporal Feature Fusion module for sequence-level prediction. Extensive experiments on the JAAD and PIE benchmark datasets demonstrate that ADAPT consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods while maintaining low computational complexity. On JAAD, the proposed method achieves an AUC of 0.73 on JAADbeh and 0.85 on JAADall, while on PIE it achieves an accuracy of 0.92 and an AUC of 0.90. Furthermore, ADAPT performs inference in only 17.23 ms per sample, offering an effective balance between predictive accuracy and real-time deployment efficiency for intelligent transportation and autonomous driving applications.

</details>


### [30] [MobileSAM2: Lightweight Segment Anything for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12297)
*Kai Jiang,Jiaxing Huang,Jingyi Zhang,Weiying Xie,Yunsong Li,Yufei Wang,Aoran Xiao,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes HyperKD, a hypergraph-based knowledge distillation method to compress the heavy SAM2 video/image segmentation model into a mobile-friendly lightweight model called MobileSAM2, validated across benchmarks and embodied AI tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SAM2 achieves strong segmentation performance but is too heavy for resource-constrained devices like mobile phones and laptops. There is a need to make SAM2 more mobile-friendly while retaining its comprehensive and generalizable knowledge.

Method: HyperKD introduces hypergraph-based knowledge distillation with two components: Temporal HyperKD models and transfers generalizable temporal knowledge, and Granularity HyperKD captures comprehensive multi-granularity knowledge from SAM2. These hypergraphs are aligned with a lightweight student model. MobileSAM2 searches for optimal architectures during model size reduction using HyperKD.

Result: Extensive experiments validate MobileSAM2 across multiple benchmarks, demonstrating promising generalization performance on embodied AI tasks.

Conclusion: HyperKD effectively distills SAM2's knowledge into a lightweight model, enabling segment anything on mobile devices with good efficiency-effectiveness balance.

Abstract: The recent large video foundation model, SAM2, enables segment anything in both images and videos, serving as a powerful base model for various applications. However, many of such use cases require to operate on resource-constrained devices like mobile phones and laptops. In this work, we aim to make SAM2 more mobile-friendly by distilling the heavyweight SAM2 into a lightweight model, facilitating segment anything in both images and videos on mobile devices. To this end, we propose Hypergraphical Knowledge Distill (HyperKD), which introduces the idea of hypergraph into knowledge distillation, aiming to effectively model and transfer SAM2's generalizable and comprehensive knowledge. HyperKD consists of Temporal HyperKD and Granularity HyperKD that construct hypergraphs to explicitly model and extract the generalizable temporal knowledge and the comprehensive multi-granularity knowledge from SAM2 respectively, which are then distilled into the lightweight student model by aligning it with the constructed hypergraphs. Besides, we present MobileSAM2, a new family of lightweight SAM2 that balances efficiency and effectiveness via searching the best model architectures with HyperKD during model size reduction. Extensive experiments validate MobileSAM2 across multiple benchmarks and show promising generalization performance on embodied AI tasks.

</details>


### [31] [What Does a Temporal Benchmark Score Measure? Decomposing Channel Use in Video VLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12304)
*Farrukh Rahman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Temporal VQA benchmarks conflate two questions: whether a question is truly temporal (task question) and whether models answer using visual order vs. positional encoding (channel question). The authors propose reversal-drop, a label-free screen to isolate the channel question, and show models like Molmo2 (position-dominant) and Qwen3-VL (visual-sequence-dominant) behave fundamentally differently despite similar scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current temporal VQA benchmarks fail to distinguish between two conflated aspects of temporal understanding: whether a question actually requires temporal reasoning (task question) and whether a model extracts temporal information from visual content or from positional encodings like RoPE (channel question). Aggregate scores mask these distinct failure modes.

Method: The authors introduce reversal-drop, a label-free diagnostic that measures accuracy loss when the visual sequence is reversed while RoPE remains forward-facing. For tasks with deterministic label transformations under reversal (or paired reverse labels), this distinguishes models that follow reversed visual content from those merely disrupted by the conflict. Activation patching is used to verify the property is internal.

Result: Molmo2 is position-dominant (reads forward event order from positional encodings despite reversed visuals), while Qwen3-VL is visual-sequence-dominant (answers based on the reversed visual content it sees). This split holds across two benchmarks and multiple temporal tasks at two scales. Activation patching confirms this is a real internal property.

Conclusion: The two channels (position and visual-sequence) fail on opposite inputs, making models with similar aggregate scores non-interchangeable. Aggregate temporal scores are insufficient for understanding model robustness; reversal-drop reveals hidden failure modes that benchmarks currently miss.

Abstract: A score on a temporal video question answering benchmark is meant to measure that a model has temporal understanding, but it conflates two questions. 1. The task question: is the question even temporal, does it need several frames and their order? and 2. The channel question, when it does, does the model recover the order from the pixels, or read it off the positional encoding (RoPE)? Most of a temporal score answers neither, a single frame and answer priors often carry it. The field's validity checks, frame-shuffle sensitivity and the accuracy gained from the full video, speak only to the task question. We contribute a label-free screen for the channel question, the reversal-drop: the accuracy lost when the visual sequence is reversed while RoPE remains forward. It can be applied to compatible temporal benchmarks without new annotations. Paired reverse labels, or tasks whose labels transform deterministically under reversal, distinguish models that follow reversed content from those merely disrupted by the conflict. Molmo2 answers the forward event reading order off positions, while Qwen3-VL answers the reversed event it actually sees, reading visual order (comparatively). We call them position-dominant and visual-sequence-dominant. The split holds across two benchmarks and several temporal tasks at two scales, and activation patching shows it is a real internal property, not an artifact of the conflict. The distinction matters, the two channels fail on opposite inputs so two models with similar score are not interchangable, i.e. an aggregate score does not reflect potential failure modes.

</details>


### [32] [DM-KG: A Novel Method for Boosting Spatial Cognition of Vision-Language Models in Street View Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12319)
*Xinyue Xu,Zheng Zhang,Kunyang Ma,Ge Zhu,Lianshuai Cao,Lei Wang,Zixuan Li,Yi Cheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DM-KG proposes a Direction-Metric Knowledge Graph framework that extracts directional and metric spatial relationships from street view imagery via panoptic segmentation and depth estimation, then injects this structured knowledge as explicit geometric priors into VLMs, reducing distance estimation MAE by 31.1% and direction judgment angular error by 65.8% on spatial QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLMs suffer from "spatial semantic hallucinations" in perceiving object locations, distances, and directions in real-world street view scenes, and such errors are difficult to trace and calibrate, posing a critical bottleneck for geospatial deployment.

Method: The framework integrates panoptic segmentation with metric depth estimation to compute entity-level 3D spatial coordinates, encodes clock azimuths and Euclidean distances of entity pairs into a JSON-formatted knowledge graph, and injects this graph into VLMs as an explicit geometric prior to guide spatial reasoning.

Result: On public spatial QA benchmarks, DM-KG reduces MAE in distance estimation by 31.1% and mean angular error in direction judgment by 65.8%, while maintaining a high QA success rate.

Conclusion: DM-KG establishes a complete, augmented reasoning pipeline that significantly improves spatial cognitive capabilities of VLMs in street view scenarios, providing a flexible, generalized, and interpretable framework for geographic visual question answering in open environments.

Abstract: As vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed in geospatial question answering and visual scene understanding, improving their spatial cognition capability on street view imagery for complex logical reasoning has emerged as a key research priority. However, existing VLMs frequently suffer from "spatial semantic hallucinations" when perceiving object locations, distances, and directions in real-world street view scenes. Furthermore, such errors are often recalcitrant to tracing and calibration, posing a critical bottleneck for their practical deployment in geospatial tasks. To address this pressing challenge, this study proposes DM-KG (Direction-Metric Knowledge Graph), a structurally grounded spatial representation framework for street view imagery. By explicitly extracting directional and metric relationships between entities from a single 2D image, this framework enhances the spatial reasoning accuracy of VLMs through a structured knowledge graph. Specifically, we integrate panoptic segmentation with metric depth estimation to robustly compute entity-level 3D spatial coordinates. Subsequently, we encode the clock azimuths and Euclidean distances of entity pairs into a JSON-formatted knowledge graph, which is injected into the VLM as an explicit geometric prior to guide spatial reasoning. Experimental results on public spatial question-answering (QA) benchmarks demonstrate that DM-KG reduces the mean absolute error (MAE) in distance estimation by 31.1% and the mean angular error in direction judgment by 65.8%, while simultaneously maintaining a high QA success rate. By establishing a complete, augmented reasoning pipeline, this research significantly improves the spatial cognitive capabilities of VLMs in street view scenarios, thereby providing a flexible, generalized, and interpretable framework for geographic visual question answering (GeoVQA) in open environments.

</details>


### [33] [ProtoPointNet: Prototype-Based Interpretable Classification of 3D Dental Point Clouds with Verifiable Spatial Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12335)
*George V. Jose,Thao Liang Chiam,Toby Hughes,Dilan Patel,Alan Brook,Lyle J. Palmer,Nikhil Cherian Kurian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ProtoPointNet is a prototype-based network for dental occlusion classification from 3D intraoral point clouds, achieving mean macro-F1 of 0.724 and AUROC of 0.825 on the Bits2Bites dataset, with prototypes activating on anatomically plausible regions like molars and incisors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prototype-based networks offer interpretable classification but have not been applied to 3D point clouds or clinical surface-pair reasoning (e.g., dental occlusion), where transparency is critical for clinical trust.

Method: ProtoPointNet uses a 14D point descriptor (local geometry, curvature, inter-arch displacement/clearance), a shared multi-task backbone, and axis-specific prototype heads for five occlusion classification tasks. Training employs auxiliary supervision and encoder-freeze hand-off to address limited clinical data.

Result: Bits2Bites: mean macro-F1 0.724, AUROC 0.825; best on vertical (F1 0.828) and sagittal-left (F1 0.807). Prototype activations localize to anatomically relevant regions (posterior molars for cross-bite, anterior incisors for bite-depth).

Conclusion: ProtoPointNet demonstrates that prototype-based reasoning is a transparent, spatially grounded alternative to black-box 3D classifiers for dental surface-pair analysis, supporting clinical interpretability.

Abstract: Prototype-based networks provide inherently interpretable classification by linking predictions to learned exemplars, but their use in 3D point clouds and clinical surface-pair reasoning remains limited. We introduce ProtoPointNet, a prototype-based model for dental occlusion classification from registered upper--lower intraoral arch pairs. Each point is encoded by a 14-dimensional descriptor combining local surface geometry, curvature, and explicit inter-arch displacement and clearance, exposing occlusal relationships to prototype matching. A shared multi-task point-cloud backbone learns axis-specific prototype heads for sagittal-left, sagittal-right, vertical, transverse, and midline classification. To support limited clinical data, we train prototypes from scratch using auxiliary supervision and encoder-freeze hand-off. On Bits2Bites, ProtoPointNet achieves mean test macro-F1 of 0.724 and AUROC of 0.825, with strongest performance on vertical (F1 0.828) and sagittal-left classification (F1 0.807). Projected prototype activations localise to anatomically plausible regions, including posterior molars and premolars for cross-bite evidence and anterior incisors for bite-depth evidence. These results support prototype-based reasoning as a transparent, spatially grounded alternative to black-box 3D classifiers for dental surface-pair analysis.

</details>


### [34] [Filtering-out poor-quality images for data preparation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12352)
*Roopdeep Kaur,Gour Karmakar,Muhammad Imran*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel approach that filters out poor-quality images using an image quality assessment metric and an optimal threshold, rather than denoising images, to improve deep learning model performance. The method achieves 93.8% accuracy on traffic sign recognition and 84.9% on object recognition datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing noise reduction techniques (median, Gaussian, bilateral filters, CNNs) have limitations: they work only for specific noise types, require appropriate training datasets, and need image resizing. There is a research gap for a more general solution that handles various environmental and camera impacts.

Method: Instead of denoising images, the proposed approach filters out poor-quality images using an image quality assessment metric and an optimum threshold, while ensuring a sufficient number of images remain for training deep learning models.

Result: The approach achieves 93.8% average recognition accuracy on traffic sign recognition datasets and 84.9% on object recognition datasets, outperforming state-of-the-art approaches on both real and simulated traffic and object recognition data.

Conclusion: The proposed quality-filtering approach demonstrates performance supremacy over existing denoising techniques and shows strong potential for real-life applications such as autonomous vehicles.

Abstract: Filtering noise is a fundamental part of data preparation that enhances image quality for applications such as object segmentation, detection, and recognition. Various noise reduction techniques are proposed in the literature, including the use of median, Gaussian, and bilateral filters. Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have gained popularity in image denoising owing to their ability to extract complex patterns and features from data. CNNs are highly adaptable, making them effective tools for various image-denoising tasks. One drawback of CNN-based techniques is that they require an appropriate training dataset and all images to be resized. Another notable drawback of all these filtering techniques is that they work for certain types of environmental and camera noises. To bridge this research gap, in this paper, for the first time, instead of denoising, we propose an approach that filters out poor-quality images for various environmental and camera impacts. In our approach, quality is assessed using an image quality assessment metric and an optimum threshold is used to filter out poor-quality images. We also ensure that a sufficient number of images remain to develop the deep learning (DL) model. The results produced using real and simulated traffic and object recognition data demonstrate the performance supremacy of the proposed approach compared with the state-of-the-art approaches. The average recognition accuracy for our proposed approach is 93.8% for the traffic sign recognition dataset and 84.9% for the object recognition dataset. This indicates our model's potential for real-life applications such as autonomous vehicles.

</details>


### [35] [ACID: Adaptive Caching for vIDeo generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12358)
*Om Agrawal,Saurabh Agarwal,Aditya Akella*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Existing dynamic caching methods for video diffusion models use a fixed threshold τ to control the speed-quality tradeoff. ACID is a lightweight, training-free wrapper that dynamically switches between low and high thresholds by monitoring the rate of change of the drift signal, identifying critical timesteps where drift changes rapidly. It expands the Pareto frontier of visual quality vs. inference speed across multiple caching methods and models, achieving up to 2.16x speedup over no-caching and 38% additional speedup over fixed-threshold baselines with negligible quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge and overcome the apparent fundamental tradeoff between speed and quality in caching-based video diffusion acceleration, which arises from holding a fixed threshold constant throughout the entire denoising process.

Method: ACID monitors the rate of change of the existing drift signal from any dynamic caching method, dynamically switching between a low threshold (at critical timesteps where drift changes rapidly) and a high threshold (elsewhere). It is training-free, signal-agnostic, and modular, plugging into existing methods like TeaCache, EasyCache, and DiCache without modifying their core mechanisms.

Result: Up to 2.16x speedup over no-caching baseline, up to 38% additional speedup over fixed-threshold baseline, with negligible quality degradation (<0.3 dB PSNR, <0.01 SSIM, <0.01 LPIPS). Consistent expansion of the Pareto frontier across three caching methods and three video diffusion models (HunyuanVideo, Wan 2.1, CogVideoX).

Conclusion: The speed-quality tradeoff in dynamic caching is not fundamental but an artifact of fixed thresholds. Adaptive thresholding that identifies critical timesteps enables substantial speed gains with minimal quality loss, and ACID provides a general, plug-and-play solution across different caching methods and models.

Abstract: Video diffusion models produce high-quality generations but remain slow at inference due to their sequential denoising procedure. Caching-based acceleration methods address this by reusing intermediate model outputs: leading dynamic approaches such as TeaCache, EasyCache, and DiCache accumulate a drift signal and skip expensive model evaluations when accumulated drift stays below a fixed threshold τ. This threshold controls an apparent tradeoff - raising it yields faster generation at the cost of visual quality, while lowering it preserves quality but sacrifices speed. We show this tradeoff is not fundamental; it is an artifact of holding τ constant throughout denoising. We identify the existence of critical steps - timesteps where the drift signal changes rapidly - and show that applying a low threshold selectively at these steps while caching aggressively elsewhere recovers most of the quality of conservative caching at substantially higher inference speeds. Building on this insight, we propose ACID, a lightweight, training-free wrapper that monitors the rate of change of each method's existing drift signal to dynamically switch between a low and a high threshold. ACID is signal-agnostic and modular: it requires no retraining and plugs directly into existing dynamic caching methods without modifying their core mechanisms. Evaluated across three caching methods (TeaCache, EasyCache, DiCache) and three open-source video diffusion models (HunyuanVideo, Wan 2.1, CogVideoX), ACID consistently expands the Pareto frontier of visual quality versus inference speed beyond what any fixed threshold achieves. In particular, on TeaCache and HunyuanVideo, ACID achieves up to 2.16x speedup over the no-caching baseline, and up to 38% additional speedup over the conservative fixed-threshold baseline with negligible (<0.3 dB PSNR, <0.01 SSIM, <0.01 LPIPS) quality degradation.

</details>


### [36] [Implicit 4D Gaussian Splatting for Fast Motion with Large Inter-Frame Displacements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12362)
*Seung-gyeom Kim,Areum Kim,Yongjae Yoo,Sukmin Yun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPIN-4DGS introduces a spatiotemporal position implicit network for 4D Gaussian Splatting that learns Gaussian attributes from explicit spatiotemporal positions via a lightweight feed-forward network, enabling robust reconstruction under fast motion with large inter-frame displacements, outperforming baselines like D3DGS on challenging sports scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 4DGS methods fail under fast motion with large inter-frame displacements, where Gaussian attributes are poorly learned and fast-moving objects are lost from reconstruction.

Method: SPIN-4DGS learns Gaussian attributes from explicitly collected spatiotemporal positions (rather than modeling temporal displacements) using a lightweight feed-forward network trained under rasterization-based reconstruction loss, enabling shared representations across Gaussians for spatiotemporal consistency.

Result: SPIN-4DGS achieves higher fidelity under large displacements with clear improvements in PSNR and SSIM on challenging sports scenes from the CMU Panoptic dataset, notably outperforming D3DGS by +1.83 PSNR on the Basketball scene.

Conclusion: SPIN-4DGS enables stable high-quality Gaussian splatting under challenging fast motions by learning shared spatiotemporal representations, avoiding the memory overhead of explicit attribute optimization across all spatiotemporal positions.

Abstract: Recent 4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) methods often fail under fast motion with large inter-frame displacements, where Gaussian attributes are poorly learned during training, and fast-moving objects are often lost from the reconstruction. In this work, we introduce Spatiotemporal Position Implicit Network for 4DGS, coined SPIN-4DGS, which learns Gaussian attributes from explicitly collected spatiotemporal positions rather than modeling temporal displacements, thereby enabling more faithful splatting under fast motions with large inter-frame displacements. To avoid the heavy memory overhead of explicitly optimizing attributes across all spatiotemporal positions, we instead predict them with a lightweight feed-forward network trained under a rasterization-based reconstruction loss. Consequently, SPIN-4DGS learns shared representations across Gaussians, effectively capturing spatiotemporal consistency and enabling stable high-quality Gaussian splatting even under challenging motions. Across extensive experiments, SPIN-4DGS consistently achieves higher fidelity under large displacements, with clear improvements in PSNR and SSIM on challenging sports scenes from the CMU Panoptic dataset. For example, SPIN-4DGS notably outperforms the strongest baseline, D3DGS, by achieving +1.83 higher PSNR on the Basketball scene.

</details>


### [37] [Lost in Visual Translation: A VLM-Assisted Perceptual-Semantic Coherence Framework for EEG-to-Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12364)
*Sukriti Tiwari,BHVSP Subrahmanyam,Nidhi Goyal,Sai Amrit Patnaik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper identifies failures of existing metrics (SSIM, LPIPS, CLIP) for evaluating EEG-to-image reconstructions, then proposes BCS (BCI-Coherence Score)—a VLM-based evaluator that separately measures tolerant perceptual and semantic alignment, validated with high human agreement (Cohen's kappa = 0.882).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard image quality metrics (SSIM, LPIPS, CLIP) poorly evaluate EEG-to-image reconstructions because they penalize blurry but semantically recoverable outputs or reward plausible but incorrect ones. A BCI-specific evaluation framework is needed.

Method: Analyzed 6,855 ground-truth/reconstruction pairs from four datasets using semantic probes, caption harshness, blind-spot rates, and controlled degradations. Proposed a VLM-based evaluation framework (four VLMs) using structured questions to produce T-PAS (perceptual) and T-SAS (semantic) scores, distilled into the BCS metric.

Result: BCS achieves T-PAS MAE of 0.079 (r=0.700) and T-SAS MAE of 0.082 (r=0.850). Human validation yields Cohen's kappa = 0.882 ± 0.174 and Krippendorff's alpha = 0.882.

Conclusion: Perceptual-semantic recoverability is a more appropriate evaluation paradigm for EEG-to-image reconstruction than generic visual similarity. BCS offers a compact, human-aligned evaluator for BCI research.

Abstract: EEG-to-image evaluation should distinguish visual fidelity from recoverable meaning. Yet EEG-derived reconstructions are blurry, distorted, and low-detail, causing SSIM, LPIPS, and CLIP to penalize semantically recoverable outputs or reward plausible but incorrect ones. We analyze 6,855 ground-truth/reconstruction pairs from ATM, ENIGMA, BrainVis, and DreamDiffusion using semantic probes, caption harshness and blind-spot rates, and controlled degradations. Pixel metrics show near-zero correlation with semantic consistency, while representation metrics conflate perceptual and semantic errors. We therefore introduce a BCI-aware framework in which four VLMs assess image pairs through structured questions, producing Tolerant Perceptual Alignment Scores (T-PAS) and Tolerant Semantic Alignment Scores (T-SAS). Their consensus is distilled into the BCI-Coherence Score (BCS), a compact evaluator achieving a T-PAS MAE of 0.079 (r = 0.700) and a T-SAS MAE of 0.082 (r = 0.850) on our data. Human validation shows highly reliable joint coherence judgments, with Cohen's kappa = 0.882 +/- 0.174 and Krippendorff's alpha = 0.882, supporting perceptual-semantic recoverability over generic visual similarity. Code and resources are available at https://sukt03.github.io/BCS/.

</details>


### [38] [UMSS: Towards Unsupervised Multi-modal Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12372)
*Haitian Zhang,Thai Duy Nguyen,Xiangyuan Wang,Mohan Liu,Lin Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniM2 is a novel framework for Unsupervised Multimodal Semantic Segmentation (UMSS) that leverages DINOv3 and Cross Modal Correspondence Synergy (CMCS) with a Cross Modal Harmonizer (CMH) to learn a unified latent space from multimodal data without labels, achieving 6.4% and 9.8% mIoU improvements on NYU Depth v2 and MFNet respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal semantic segmentation is hindered by the high cost of human annotations, and existing unsupervised methods fail when naively extended to multimodal data due to fusion degradation—the inability to reconcile heterogeneous structural patterns from different sensors without explicit supervision.

Method: A framework called UniM2 built on DINOv3, with two key components: (1) Cross Modal Correspondence Synergy (CMCS) for learning a unified latent space that extracts intrinsic shared semantic cues without label guidance, and (2) Cross Modal Harmonizer (CMH) that designates RGB as a stable reference to suppress inconsistent relational supervision while guiding exploitation of complementary structural features.

Result: UniM2 improves mIoU by 6.4% on NYU Depth v2 and 9.8% on MFNet, demonstrating clear advantages over existing frameworks for UMSS.

Conclusion: The proposed UniM2 effectively addresses the novel UMSS problem by enabling complementary sensor information exploitation in a fully label-free setting, transforming conventional fusion methods into consistent performance gains.

Abstract: Multimodal semantic segmentation (MSS) is essential for robust perception in complex environments, yet its potential remains largely untapped because of the prohibitive cost of human annotations. While unsupervised semantic segmentation (USS) has achieved strong results on a single RGB modality, its naive extension to multimodal data is often hindered by fusion degradation. This occurs because, without explicit supervision, existing frameworks struggle to reconcile the heterogeneous structural patterns captured by different sensors and therefore fail to effectively exploit their complementary information. In this paper, we make the first attempt to address the novel problem of Unsupervised Multimodal Semantic Segmentation (UMSS), aiming to effectively exploit complementary sensor information in a fully label free setting. To this end, we propose UniM2 (Unified Multimodal), a novel framework built on DINOv3 that transforms conventional fusion methods into consistent performance gains. Our key idea is to learn a unified latent space driven by Cross Modal Correspondence Synergy (CMCS) to extract intrinsic shared semantic cues, bypassing the need for label guided adaptive fusion. To mitigate inherent intermodal conflicts, we introduce a Cross Modal Harmonizer (CMH) that designates RGB as a stable reference, effectively suppressing inconsistent relational supervision while guiding the model to exploit complementary structural features. Extensive experimental results on NYU Depth v2 and MFNet show that UniM2 improves mIoU by 6.4% and 9.8%, respectively, demonstrating clear advantages over existing frameworks for UMSS.

</details>


### [39] [IQA-T1: Tool-based Visual Evidence Reasoning for Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12375)
*Jinjian Wu,Jiaqi Tang,Wei Wei,Yingying Yan,Jianmin Chen,Botong Geng,Lei Zhang,Qifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: IQA-T1 is a tool-based visual evidence reasoning framework for Image Quality Assessment that enhances MLLM reasoning by autonomously invoking specialized analysis tools (e.g., noise residual maps, gradient statistics, frequency spectra) to generate structured perceptual evidence, achieving state-of-the-art performance across seven IQA benchmarks with improved interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLM-based IQA approaches rely on semantically biased internal representations, making them insensitive to low-level perceptual degradations and limiting generalization and interpretability in open-world environments.

Method: IQA-T1 augments MLLM reasoning by autonomously invoking specialized analysis tools during inference to generate structured visual evidence (noise residual maps, gradient statistics, frequency spectra), which are progressively integrated into the reasoning process. A dataset called Q-Tool with 11k multimodal reasoning chains grounded in tool-generated evidence was constructed to support this paradigm.

Result: Achieves the best overall performance across seven IQA benchmarks while producing interpretable and evidence-grounded quality assessments.

Conclusion: Tool-based visual evidence reasoning significantly improves MLLM-based IQA by grounding predictions in explicit perceptual observations rather than semantic biases, enhancing both generalization and interpretability.

Abstract: Image Quality Assessment (IQA) in open-world environments remains challenging due to limited generalization and interpretability. Recent approaches based on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) introduce textual reasoning for quality prediction, yet their judgments rely heavily on semantically biased internal representations, making them insensitive to low-level perceptual degradations. We propose IQA-T1, a tool-based visual evidence reasoning framework that augments MLLM reasoning with explicit perceptual observations. During inference, the model autonomously invokes specialized analysis tools to generate structured visual evidence, such as noise residual maps, gradient statistics, and frequency spectra, which are progressively integrated into the reasoning process. To support this paradigm, we construct Q-Tool, a dataset containing 11k multimodal reasoning chains grounded in tool-generated evidence. Extensive experiments on seven IQA benchmarks show that IQA-T1 achieves the best overall performance across datasets while producing interpretable and evidence-grounded quality assessments. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/zibuyu-02/IQA-T1.

</details>


### [40] [Demonstration of the common dual-channel feature decoupling characteristic of front-door mediation causal inference methods in whole-slice image classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12376)
*Zhirui Zhang,Tianhang Nan,Yong Ding,Zhuolun Song,Dayu Hu,Xiaoyu Cui*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes and proves two hypotheses for evaluating causal inference MIL methods in WSI analysis: 1) they introduce an independent classification channel for effective WSI classification, and 2) greater feature diversity between new and baseline channels improves elimination of false correlations. These hypotheses were validated on breast cancer and NSCLC datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a theoretical framework for understanding and evaluating how causal inference MIL methods eliminate false correlations between diagnostic and non-diagnostic evidence in Whole Slide Image analysis.

Method: The authors propose two formal hypotheses: (1) causal inference MIL introduces an independent classification channel that completes WSI classification, and (2) larger feature differences between new and baseline channels increase effectiveness in removing false correlations. They evaluate multiple causal inference MIL methods on breast cancer and NSCLC datasets.

Result: The proposed hypotheses were validated on breast cancer and non-small cell lung cancer datasets, demonstrating that the theoretical framework accurately characterizes the mechanism of causal inference MIL methods.

Conclusion: The proposed hypotheses provide a new theoretical perspective for applying causal inference to WSI analysis, explaining that overlaying parallel independent channels to increase deep feature diversity eliminates false associations between WSI-level diagnostic and non-diagnostic evidence.

Abstract: Causal inference using front door intervention and multi-instance learning (MIL) has advanced the analysis of Whole Slide Images (WSI) in digital pathology. These methods adjust feature distributions of subtle evidence sub-images to correctly associate them with WSI-level diagnoses. We propose and prove 2 hypotheses for evaluating such methods: 1) Causal inference MIL introduces an independent classification channel that effectively completes WSI classification; 2) Greater difference between features extracted by the new and baseline channels increases effectiveness in eliminating false correlations. This hypothesis describes the core of causal inference MILs: overlaying parallel, independent channels to eliminate false associations between WSI-level diagnostic and non-diagnostic evidence sub-images by increasing deep feature diversity. Based on these hypotheses, we evaluated several causal inference MILs on breast cancer and non-small cell lung cancer datasets. This hypothesis provides a new theoretical perspective for applying causal inference to WSI analysis.

</details>


### [41] [SeamGen: Artist-Aligned UV Seam Generation via Graph Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12379)
*Hao Xu,Yuqing Zhang,Yiqian Wu,Xueqi Ma,Ding Liang,Yan-Pei Cao,Ying-Tian Liu,Xiaogang Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SeamGen is a generative model that learns UV seam placement from professional artist data using a flow-matching approach with a Mesh Transformer, outperforming traditional distortion-based and semantic-proxy methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UV seam placement is labor-intensive and existing automatic methods rely on handcrafted objectives or semantic proxies that often deviate from artist-preferred patterns and production requirements.

Method: SeamGen uses a flow-matching generative model to learn per-edge seam label distribution from a large corpus of professional seam layouts, with a Mesh Transformer backbone that interleaves local graph attention over mesh edges with global self-attention across vertices, plus training-free inpainting for controllable generation.

Result: SeamGen produces UV layouts that better align with artist-authored preferences and achieve superior perceptual quality compared with distortion-based and semantic-proxy baselines.

Conclusion: Learning priors from professional seam layout data enables better alignment with artist preferences and production requirements than traditional optimization-based approaches.

Abstract: UV seam placement is a critical yet labor-intensive step in 3D content creation, requiring artists to balance chart shape, seam concealment, and alignment with semantic and geometric features. Existing automatic methods are primarily based on per-object optimization, relying on handcrafted objectives to avoid distortion or on proxies from pretrained models to inject semantic information. However, these strategies are not always well aligned with seams used in industrial production pipelines, often resulting in layouts that deviate from artist-preferred seam patterns and practical production requirements. To address these limitations, we propose SeamGen, a generative model for UV seam generation that aligns with artist preferences and production requirements. Instead of depending on manually designed objectives and constraints, SeamGen learns the distribution of per-edge seam labels from a large corpus of existing seam layouts using a flow-matching generative model. A key challenge is that typical Transformer architectures used in flow matching models are designed for sequential representations, such as point clouds, and cannot naturally account for mesh topology. To enable mesh-native learning, we design a Mesh Transformer backbone that interleaves local graph attention over mesh edges with global self-attention across vertices, capturing both fine-grained geometric cues and long-range topological coherence. To further improve inference-time controllability and quality, we exploit the training-free inpainting capability of flow models for both localized seam refinement and constraint-guided seam generation. Extensive experiments show that by learning priors from professional seam layout data, SeamGen produces UV layouts that better align with artist-authored preferences and achieve superior perceptual quality compared with distortion-based and semantic-proxy baselines.

</details>


### [42] [Seeing Globally, Refining Locally: Global Visual Guidance and Local Ultrasound Cues for Robust Freehand 3-D Ultrasound Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12398)
*Yameng Zhang,Zhongyu Chen,Dianye Huang,Xiangyu Chu,K. W. Samuel Au,Zhongliang Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A global-to-local pose estimation framework for freehand 3D ultrasound reconstruction that fuses dual-camera contextual features with B-mode US anatomical features via cross-modal fusion to achieve accurate, drift-resistant probe trajectory estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Freehand 3D US imaging relies on accurate probe pose estimation, but existing trackerless methods suffer from accumulated pose errors over long scanning trajectories, limiting reconstruction quality.

Method: A dual-camera branch aggregates contextual features across views and time for globally consistent trajectory estimation; a B-mode branch aggregates anatomical features from US images for local motion cues; a cross-modal fusion module integrates both to predict pose residuals; a multi-scale pose loss constrains relative motion over multiple temporal horizons.

Result: On FUSION-J and FUSION-L datasets, reduces average trajectory drift to 1.67 mm and 1.29 mm (16.50% and 27.12% improvement over dual-camera baseline), substantially outperforming US-only methods (>13 mm drift). In in vivo forearm artery reconstruction achieves Hausdorff distance of 1.58 mm.

Conclusion: The proposed cross-modal fusion framework effectively suppresses accumulated drift in freehand 3D US reconstruction, demonstrating strong clinical applicability with significant improvements over both camera-only and US-only baselines.

Abstract: Freehand 3-D ultrasound (US) imaging has attracted increasing attention owing to its intuitive volumetric visualization, ease of use, and low cost. However, accurate 3-D reconstruction critically depends on stable probe pose estimation, yet existing trackerless methods remain susceptible to accumulated pose errors, particularly over long scanning trajectories. To address this limitation, we propose a global-to-local pose estimation framework that exploits external camera observations for globally stable localization and B-mode US images for anatomy-aware local refinement. Specifically, the framework comprises a dual-camera branch that performs contextual feature aggregation across camera views and temporal observations to estimate a globally consistent probe trajectory, and a B-mode branch that performs anatomical feature aggregation from sequential US images to capture tissue-dependent local motion cues. A cross-modal fusion module subsequently integrates the contextual camera features and anatomical US features to predict pose residuals and refine the camera-derived estimates in the transformation space. Furthermore, a multi-scale pose loss constrains relative motion over multiple temporal horizons to suppress accumulated drift during extended scans. The proposed framework is validated on phantom and in vivo datasets. On two in-house datasets (FUSION-J and FUSION-L) collected using different machines, the proposed US + Dual-Cam model reduces average trajectory drift to 1.67 mm and 1.29 mm, representing improvement of 16.50% and 27.12%, respectively, over a strong dual-camera baseline, while substantially outperforming US-only pose estimation (>13 mm drift). In in vivo forearm arteries reconstruction, it achieves Hausdorff distances of 1.58 mm, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method on real clinical scenarios.

</details>


### [43] [Physically Aware Radiomics Without Interpolation: Disentangling Voxel Geometry and Signal Modification in CT and MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12399)
*David Corral Fontecha,Juan Miranda Bautista,Pablo Menendez Fernández-Miranda,Sergio Rubio-Martín,Lara Lloret Iglesias,Jose A. Vega*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A voxel-spacing-aware radiomic framework (VS) that incorporates physical geometry into texture computation without resampling, showing near-native agreement with non-resampled extraction (ICC >0.997) while avoiding interpolation artifacts, offering a coherent alternative to isotropic resampling for anisotropic CT/MRI analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard radiomic texture features assume isotropic spatial relationships when computed in voxel-index neighborhoods. In anisotropic images, this confounds voxel geometry with interpolation-induced signal changes, motivating the need for a voxel-spacing-aware approach that incorporates physical geometry without resampling.

Method: Modified PyRadiomics to account for voxel spacing while preserving native image signal. Compared four configurations (NR, RS, VS, FK) across 685 LIDC-IDRI lung nodules and 209 I-SPY2 breast MRI cases using 196 radiomic descriptors. Evaluated robustness via ICC, within-subject variability, Friedman testing, feature selection, ML, multilayer perceptron, and external validation.

Result: VS achieved near-native agreement with NR: median ICC(A,1) of 0.9976 (CT) and 0.9984 (MRI). RS showed lower agreement and larger deviations. FK showed intermediate behavior, confirming spacing metadata alone affects features. Gradient-derived and neighborhood-sensitive descriptors were most affected. VS preserved predictive performance comparable to NR in CT; MRI showed greater variability.

Conclusion: Voxel-spacing-aware extraction separates geometric modeling from interpolation-induced signal modification while preserving the native image signal, offering a coherent alternative to isotropic resampling for radiomic analysis of anisotropic CT and MRI.

Abstract: Objective: Radiomic texture features are usually computed in voxel-index neighborhoods, implicitly assuming isotropic spatial relationships. In anisotropic images, this can confound voxel geometry with interpolation-induced signal changes. We developed a voxel-spacing-aware radiomic framework that incorporates physical geometry into texture computation without resampling.
  Approach: We modified PyRadiomics to account for voxel spacing while preserving the native image signal. Four configurations were compared: native non-resampled extraction (NR), isotropic resampling (RS), voxel-spacing-aware extraction (VS), and fake-isotropic preprocessing (FK), in which spacing metadata were overwritten without altering the image array. Experiments included 685 LIDC-IDRI pulmonary nodules and 209 I-SPY2 breast MRI cases, with 196 radiomic descriptors. Robustness was assessed using ICC, within-subject variability, Friedman testing, feature selection, machine learning, a multilayer perceptron, and external validation.
  Main results: VS showed near-native agreement with NR: median ICC(A,1) was 0.9976 in CT and 0.9984 in MRI. RS produced lower agreement and larger deviations, while FK showed intermediate behavior, confirming that spacing metadata alone can affect radiomic features. Gradient-derived and neighborhood-sensitive descriptors were most affected by preprocessing. VS preserved predictive performance comparable to NR in external CT validation, whereas MRI showed greater variability across preprocessing strategies and classifiers.
  Significance: Voxel-spacing-aware extraction separates geometric modeling from interpolation-induced signal modification while preserving the native image signal, offering a coherent alternative to isotropic resampling for radiomic analysis of anisotropic CT and MRI.

</details>


### [44] [Contrastive-Augmented Flow Matching for Style-Content Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12404)
*Yusong Li,Pingchuan Ma,Ming Gui,Vincent Tao Hu,Björn Ommer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CAtFM integrates contrastive regularization into flow matching to learn disentangled content-style representations, improving retrieval, clustering, and robustness across synthetic and real-world benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion and flow-based models trained with generative objectives often produce entangled or misaligned content-style factors, limiting controllable generation and compositional generalization.

Method: Contrastive supervision is applied to predicted endpoints during invertible flow matching training, enforcing semantic consistency across transported distributions while allowing disentanglement to emerge implicitly without strict factorization assumptions.

Result: CAtFM improves content and style retrieval, enhances embedding cluster separation, and achieves stronger open-set robustness on ImageNet, WikiArt, DomainNet, and DTD, operating mainly in CLIP space with validation on DINO and ALIGN encoders.

Conclusion: Coupling discriminative contrastive constraints with deterministic transport offers a simple and effective way to improve disentanglement and robustness under distribution shift.

Abstract: Learning representations that separate content and style is crucial for controllable generation and compositional generalization. However, diffusion and flow-based models trained primarily with generative objectives often produce entangled or misaligned factors. To address this gap, we introduce Contrastive Augmented Flow Matching (CAtFM), a framework that integrates contrastive regularization into an invertible flow matching formulation to promote structured content-style representations. Rather than constraining intermediate latents or velocity fields, we apply contrastive supervision to predicted endpoints during training, enforcing semantic consistency across transported distributions while allowing disentanglement to emerge implicitly, without assuming strictly pure or fully factorized content and style representations. Our main experiments operate in the CLIP embedding space, with additional validation using frozen DINO and ALIGN encoders. Across synthetic data, in-domain styles, and real-world benchmarks (ImageNet, WikiArt, DomainNet, and DTD), CAtFM improves content and style retrieval, enhances embedding cluster separation, and achieves stronger open-set robustness compared to generative and discriminative baselines. Overall, CAtFM provides a simple way to couple discriminative constraints with deterministic transport, improving disentanglement and robustness under distribution shift.

</details>


### [45] [Virtual Chromoendscopy with Tunable Visibility Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12416)
*Yuhi Kanno,Yusuke Monno,Sho Suzuki,Tomohiro Tada,Masatoshi Okutomi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes V-ECE, a tunable image translation model that enhances standard endoscopy images to virtually simulate chromoendoscopy at user-controlled enhancement levels, eliminating the need for physical dye spraying.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chromoendoscopy improves gastric lesion visibility but requires additional cost and time for dye preparation and spraying; virtual chromoendoscopy addresses this but lacks adjustable enhancement levels tailored to different lesions and practitioner preferences.

Method: A novel image translation model with user-tunable enhancement levels that generates virtual enhanced chromoendoscopy images from standard endoscopy images, combining V-CE with image enhancement techniques.

Result: The proposed model plausibly generates V-ECE images with various enhancement levels using a single unified model.

Conclusion: V-ECE enables customizable, cost-effective virtual enhancement of gastric lesion visibility without physical dye, adaptable to user preferences.

Abstract: Chromoendoscopy (CE) is a common clinical practice that sprays indigo carmine blue dye onto the gastric surface to improve the visibility of gastric lesions, such as an early cancer. While CE is effective in detecting the lesions, preparing and spraying the dye needs additional cost and time, which is undesirable both for patients and medical practitioners. To overcome this issue, virtual chromoendoscopy (V-CE) was recently proposed, which applies a learned image translation model to virtually generate a CE image from a standard endoscopy (SE) image. In this paper, we propose virtual enhanced chromoendoscopy (V-ECE) that combines V-CE with image enhancement techniques to further improve the visibility of gastric lesions. Because a desired enhancement level depends on the inspected lesion and the practitioner's preference, we introduce a novel image translation model that can generate V-ECE images using an enhancement level tunable by a user. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed model can plausibly generate V-ECE images with various enhancement levels using a unified model.

</details>


### [46] [MQAdapter: Multi-Modal Quantum Adapter for Coarse-to-Fine VLM Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12418)
*Yumiao Zhao,Bo Jiang,Min Lu,Xiao Wang,Jin Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MQAdapter is a coarse-to-fine VLM fine-tuning approach for few-shot learning that uses quantum computation. It first retrieves Top-K category candidates as semantic anchors, then employs cross-modal quantum learning to refine visual features via encoding into quantum states, leveraging quantum entanglement and superposition for higher-order cross-modal interactions. It achieves superior performance on 15 datasets with fewer trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs achieve high Top-K accuracy in few-shot classification but struggle with fine-grained discrimination among visually similar categories (poor Top-1 performance). Existing VLM adapters focus on global alignment and fail to exploit semantically similar categories to refine fine-grained visual representations.

Method: A coarse-to-fine fine-tuning approach: (1) Retrieve Top-K category candidates most similar to the input image as semantic anchors; (2) Employ a cross-modal quantum learning mechanism that encodes visual and textual features into quantum states; (3) Leverage quantum entanglement and superposition in a high-dimensional Hilbert space to model higher-order cross-modal interactions, producing more discriminative representations than traditional Euclidean adapters.

Result: Evaluations on 15 datasets demonstrate MQAdapter achieves effective performance while requiring fewer trainable parameters. It is parameter-efficient and can integrate with existing fine-tuning algorithms for further gains.

Conclusion: MQAdapter effectively addresses the fine-grained discrimination limitation of VLMs in few-shot classification by introducing quantum computation for cross-modal feature refinement, offering a parameter-efficient solution that outperforms traditional Euclidean adapters.

Abstract: Large-scale Vision-Language Models have demonstrated impressive transfer learning capabilities across a wide range of tasks. For few-shot classification, we observe that VLMs exhibit a notable ability to filter candidate categories and thus achieve high Top-K accuracy. However, they often struggle with fine-grained discrimination among visually similar categories, resulting in unsatisfactory Top-1 performance, as shown in Figure 1. Existing studies on VLM adapters generally focus on global alignment between visual and textual representations in the feature space, but fail to exploit semantically similar categories to refine fine-grained visual representations. Based on these observations, we propose a novel coarse-to-fine VLM fine-tuning approach for few-shot learning that leverages quantum computation, termed the Multi-Modal Quantum Adapter (MQAdapter). Specifically, MQAdapter first retrieves the Top-K category candidates most similar to the input image and uses them as semantic anchors. It then employs a cross-modal quantum learning mechanism to refine visual features under the guidance of these anchors. The core of this mechanism is the encoding of visual and textual features into quantum states. By leveraging quantum entanglement and superposition in a high-dimensional Hilbert space, MQAdapter effectively models higher-order cross-modal interactions, producing more discriminative representations than traditional Euclidean adapters. MQAdapter is parameter-efficient and can be integrated with various existing fine-tuning algorithms to achieve further performance gains. Evaluations on 15 datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of MQAdapter while requiring fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [47] [DeGuNet: Depth-Guided Ultra-Compact Backbones for Efficient LiDAR-Camera 3D Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12419)
*Haifa Zhang,Yijing Wang,Peixi Peng,Zhiqiang Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DeGuNet is an ultra-compact, plug-and-play image backbone for LiDAR-camera fusion in 3D object detection that replaces heavy 2D-pretrained visual backbones, using depth-guided and sparsity-aware mechanisms to reduce GPU memory by up to 66.5%, achieve 1.16x speedup, and gain up to 6.20 mAP on nuScenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-modal 3D object detection frameworks rely on massive 2D-pretrained visual backbones, introducing parameter redundancy and structural misalignment with LiDAR's sparse Bird's-Eye-View geometry.

Method: DeGuNet is an ultra-compact, plug-and-play image backbone designed for depth-guided representation learning. It incorporates sparsity-aware feature extraction mechanisms to align multi-view images with unstructured LiDAR depth while preventing invalid-region contamination.

Result: Reduces GPU memory consumption by up to 66.5%, achieves 1.16x inference speedup, and delivers up to 6.20 absolute mAP gain on the nuScenes dataset.

Conclusion: DeGuNet establishes a new paradigm for parameter-efficient multi-modal 3D perception by eliminating architectural redundancy and improving both efficiency and accuracy.

Abstract: In autonomous driving perception, the fusion of LiDAR and camera modalities has become the dominant paradigm for 3D object detection. However, current multi-modal frameworks heavily rely on massive visual backbones pretrained on 2D semantic tasks. This reliance introduces substantial parameter redundancy and a structural misalignment, as 2D priors are ill-equipped to handle the extreme sparsity of LiDAR projections required for Bird's-Eye-View geometry. To address this, we present DeGuNet, an ultra-compact and plug-and-play image backbone explicitly designed for depth-guided representation learning. By incorporating sparsity-aware feature extraction mechanisms, DeGuNet effectively aligns multi-view images with unstructured LiDAR depth while strictly preventing invalid-region contamination. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate DeGuNet's broad plug-and-play applicability and superior efficiency. When integrated into established baselines, it fundamentally eliminates architectural redundancy, reducing GPU memory consumption by up to 66.5% and achieving a 1.16x inference speedup. Concurrently, DeGuNet delivers up to a 6.20 absolute mAP gain, establishing a new paradigm for parameter-efficient multi-modal 3D perception.

</details>


### [48] [More Than Where You Are: Learning Semantics, Structure, and Geometry from Cross-View Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12429)
*Mao Chen,Xiangkai Zhang,Zhiyong Liu,Chuankai Liu,Xu Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CROSS is a unified cross-view localization framework using 3D-grounded alignment, structure-aware matching, and hypothesis ranking that achieves state-of-the-art performance while learning stable semantics, reliable structure, and transferable geometry across extreme viewpoint changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-view localization should not be treated merely as 2D matching or pose estimation; the goal is to develop consistent cross-view understanding under extreme viewpoint changes, including stable semantics, reliable structure, and transferable geometry.

Method: CROSS proposes three components: (1) 3D-grounded alignment to provide explicit 3D grounding, (2) structure-aware matching that relaxes strict point-wise matching to preserve semantic consistency, and (3) hypothesis ranking that replaces absolute objectives with better geometric reasoning.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on KITTI and VIGOR datasets, with demonstrated ability to learn stable semantics, reliable structure, and transferable geometry across extreme viewpoint differences.

Conclusion: Cross-view localization can serve as a pathway toward consistent cross-view understanding, not just pose estimation, by incorporating 3D grounding, structure-aware matching, and hypothesis ranking.

Abstract: Consistent cross-view understanding under extreme viewpoint changes is essential for spatial intelligence, as it enables models to recognize the same scene across extreme viewpoint gaps. Cross-view localization naturally provides a promising pathway toward this ability, as it requires a model to align ground-view imagery with geo-referenced satellite-view imagery despite drastic appearance changes to estimate camera poses. Recent visual foundation models have made this long-standing localization problem increasingly feasible by providing rich 2D representations for cross-view matching. However, we argue that cross-view localization should not be viewed merely as 2D matching or pose estimation. In this work, we revisit cross-view localization as more than pose estimation and investigate how it can help the model develop consistent cross-view understanding under extreme viewpoint changes, including stable semantics, reliable structure, and transferable geometry. We identify three key limitations of existing methods that prevent them from achieving this. They usually lack explicit 3D grounding, rely on strict point-wise matching that can weaken semantic consistency, and learn from an absolute objective that provides limited guidance for geometric reasoning. To address these limitations, we propose CROSS, a unified cross-view localization framework built upon 3D-grounded alignment, structure-aware matching, and hypothesis ranking. This formulation makes structure learning an intrinsic requirement, encourages semantic representations to remain stable, and enables the model to acquire transferable geometry. Extensive experiments on the KITTI and VIGOR datasets show that CROSS achieves state-of-the-art performance in cross-view localization. More importantly, CROSS effectively learns stable semantics, reliable structure, and transferable geometry across extremely different viewpoints.

</details>


### [49] [ARDepth: Auto-regressive Monocular Depth Estimation with Progressive Visual Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12433)
*Zijie Wang,Wei Zhang,Weiming Zhang,Xiao Tan,Weikai Chen,Xiaoxu Li,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ARDepth formulates monocular depth estimation as structured auto-regressive generation, progressively constructing depth representations at increasing spatial scales using Scale-Progressive Conditioning and Semantic-Aware Guidance, achieving strong performance with structurally consistent predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion models for MDE implicitly assume depth is a globally smooth field recovered via iterative denoising, failing to explicitly model the piecewise, hierarchical, and scale-dependent organization of scene geometry.

Method: ARDepth uses auto-regressive generation to progressively build depth representations as spatial resolution increases, with Scale-Progressive Conditioning (SPC) injecting multi-scale visual features at each stage, and Semantic-Aware Guidance (SAG) providing scene-level semantic priors for global structural consistency.

Result: The approach achieves strong performance on MDE benchmarks and produces structurally consistent depth predictions across scales, validating auto-regressive generation as an effective alternative paradigm.

Conclusion: Auto-regressive generation is a promising alternative paradigm for geometric modeling in monocular depth estimation, better capturing hierarchical scene structure than global refinement approaches like diffusion models.

Abstract: Diffusion models have recently become the dominant paradigm for monocular depth estimation (MDE). However, they implicitly assume that depth can be recovered as a globally smooth field through iterative denoising, which does not explicitly reflect the piecewise and scale-dependent organization of scene geometry. In practice, geometric structure emerges progressively across spatial scales, where coarse layout, surfaces, and boundaries are constructed in a hierarchical manner. Motivated by this observation, we introduce ARDepth, which formulates depth estimation as structured auto-regressive generation. Instead of recovering depth through global refinement, ARDepth progressively constructs depth representations as spatial resolution increases. To support this generative process, we introduce Scale-Progressive Conditioning (SPC) to inject multi-scale visual features at each generation stage, and Semantic-Aware Guidance (SAG) to provide scene-level semantic priors that enhance global structural consistency. Together, these designs enable the model to capture fine-grained local details while maintaining coherent global geometry. Empirical results demonstrate that our approach achieves strong performance and produces structurally consistent depth predictions across scales, validating auto-regressive generation as a promising alternative paradigm for geometric modeling.

</details>


### [50] [Let RGB Be the Language of Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12450)
*Timing Yang,Jinrui Yang,Xinlong Li,Yuhan Wang,Haoran Li,Yanqing Liu,Guoyizhe Wei,Jixuan Ying,Chen Wei,Rama Chellappa,Yuyin Zhou,Cihang Xie,Alan Yuille,Feng Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RINO proposes a unified vision formulation where diverse visual information (masks, depth maps, etc.) is represented as RGB images, and various visual tasks are converted to RGB-to-RGB image editing problems. A single model without task-specific fine-tuning achieves competitive zero-shot performance on both dense understanding (segmentation, depth estimation) and dense-conditioned generation (pose-to-image) tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a unified framework for visual tasks analogous to language models, where diverse visual information types and tasks share a common representation (RGB) and architecture, eliminating the need for task-specific fine-tuning.

Method: All forms of visual information (masks, depth maps, etc.) are represented as RGB images. General visual tasks are reformulated as RGB-to-RGB image editing problems, allowing a single encoding-decoding architecture to handle multiple tasks through a unified visual interface.

Result: Competitive zero-shot performance on dense understanding tasks (segmentation, depth estimation) and dense-conditioned generation tasks (pose-to-image generation) using a generic image editing backbone without task-specific fine-tuning.

Conclusion: RINO offers a promising pathway toward general unified vision-language systems where diverse visual tasks can be expressed, interpreted, and solved through a shared visual language.

Abstract: This work introduces a unified formulation for vision models, where diverse forms of visual information beyond natural images, such as masks, depth maps, and other structured visual signals, are all represented as RGB images, while general visual tasks can be converted into a common RGB-to-RGB image editing problem. In this paradigm, different types of visual information internally share the same encoding and decoding architecture and parameters as natural images, enabling a single model to transfer across tasks through a unified visual interface, in a way analogous to how language models operate over text. We refer to this formulation as RGB In and RGB Out (RINO). Built upon a generic image editing backbone without task-specific fine-tuning, RINO demonstrates robust and competitive zero-shot performance on both dense understanding tasks such as segmentation and depth estimation (where we unify outputs as RGB), and dense-conditioned generation tasks such as pose-to-image generation (where we unify inputs as RGB). We hope this study provides useful insights toward general unified vision-language systems, where diverse visual tasks can be expressed, interpreted, and solved through a shared visual language. Code is available at https://github.com/yangtiming/RINO.

</details>


### [51] [Steering Diffusion Models via Class-Contrastive Influence for Few-Shot Medical Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12464)
*Jeeyung Kim,Erfan Esmaeili,Qiang Qiu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes Class-Contrastive Influence (C2I), a gradient-based criterion to measure synthetic sample usefulness for few-shot medical image classification, and fine-tunes diffusion models via reinforcement learning with C2I rewards to generate class-informative samples, outperforming existing augmentation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based augmentation methods improve realism, diversity, or domain adaptation but overlook the fundamental question of how to measure and optimize sample usefulness for the downstream classification task.

Method: Proposes C2I, a criterion quantifying sample usefulness via gradient-based influence on the classifier, where effective samples show a strong C2I gap (gradient alignment with same-class validation gradients and opposition to other-class gradients). Diffusion models are fine-tuned with reinforcement learning using C2I-based rewards to generate high-utility samples.

Result: Across several few-shot medical imaging benchmarks, C2I-guided generation improves downstream accuracy and robustness over diffusion-based augmentation baselines.

Conclusion: Synthetic augmentation is most effective when guided by task usefulness (measured via C2I) rather than image quality alone; high-C2I samples correspond to hard, boundary-proximal examples that refine decision boundaries and improve robustness.
