On a sampled dataset comprising 99 managers and the S\&P 500 index (503 securities, 209,351 temporal edges across 48 quarters from 2013--2025), Node Affinity prediction model using Virtual State (NAVIS) achieves a state-of-the-art test Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG) of 0.9127 with features (0.9121 without), outperforming all dynamic graph representation learning competitors by a substantial margin, and outperforming all heuristic methods. Remarkably, a simple Exponential Moving Average baseline achieves 0.8882, surpassing all dynamic graph models except NAVIS and all heuristics except Persistent Forecast (0.8891), highlighting the strong smoothness and persistence of institutional portfolios. Domain-specific node features provide only marginal gains (<1.2\%), indicating that temporal and structural signals in the 13F ownership graph already capture most of the predictable information. By benchmarking a suite of Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB) models under the node affinity prediction setting, both with and without features, on real-world 13F data, this work provides a reproducible foundation for temporal graph machine learning in holdings prediction and portfolio allocation.

</details>


### [126] [Sparse Autoencoders for Interpretable Out-of-Distribution Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12094)
*Ayush Karmacharya,Luke Luschwitz,Lucia Romero,Yanan Niu,Joseph Campbell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper uses sparse autoencoders to learn interpretable features from intermediate network layers for OOD detection, achieving state-of-the-art performance by exploiting that ID and OOD data activate distinct sparse features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable OOD detection is critical for safe ML deployment; existing methods focus on final output layers and neglect rich hierarchical information in intermediate layers, and neural networks produce overconfident predictions on OOD inputs.

Method: Leverages sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to learn interpretable features from intermediate network activations; proposes an OOD score based on cosine similarity between a test sample's sparse feature activations and the mean activations of ID classes.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard OOD detection benchmarks, and yields interpretable insights into how distribution shift affects learned representations.

Conclusion: The post-hoc detection method using SAE-derived sparse features from intermediate layers is both effective for OOD detection and provides interpretability about distribution shift effects on representations.

Abstract: Reliable detection of out-of-distribution (OOD) samples is crucial for the safe deployment of machine learning models. Neural networks often produce overconfident predictions for inputs that deviate from their training data, leading to significant degradation in performance. While many OOD detection methods focus on the final output layer, they neglect the rich hierarchical information present in intermediate network layers. This paper introduces a novel approach that leverages sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to learn interpretable features from these intermediate activations. We find that in-distribution (ID) and OOD data activate distinct sets of these sparse features. We propose a new OOD score derived from the cosine similarity between the sparse feature activations of a test sample and the mean activations of ID classes. Our post-hoc detection method not only achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard OOD detection benchmarks, but yields interpretable insights into how distribution shift affects learned representations.

</details>


### [127] [PFAdapter: Hierarchical LoRA Decomposition for Personalized Federated MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12111)
*Jing Liu,Kun Yang,Yan Wang,Dingkang Yang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Wei Zhang,Yang Liu,Wei Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PFAdapter is a communication-efficient federated fine-tuning framework for MLLMs that uses hierarchical LoRA decomposition (global-shared vs local-private adapters) with orthogonality regularization and selective aggregation, achieving ~50% communication reduction and 2.4-4.8% accuracy gains on edge intelligence tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning of MLLMs faces challenges in balancing global knowledge aggregation with local adaptation under heterogeneous network conditions; conventional federated protocols use uniform parameter aggregation that conflates domain-invariant features with client-specific nuances, causing suboptimal personalization and excessive communication overhead.

Method: Hierarchical LoRA decomposition explicitly separates adapter parameters into global-shared (query and key projections) for universal multimodal semantics and local-private (value and output projections) for edge-specific adaptation. Orthogonality regularization based on Frobenius norm enforces strict separation. Selective aggregation synchronizes only global-shared components, reducing communication costs by ~50%.

Result: On VQA-RAD, SLAKE, Hateful Memes, and CrisisMMD datasets, PFAdapter achieves 2.4% to 4.8% accuracy improvements over state-of-the-art baselines with nearly 50% reduction in communication costs.

Conclusion: PFAdapter establishes an efficient solution for agentic AI deployment in resource-constrained communication networks, enabling personalized federated fine-tuning of MLLMs with substantially reduced communication overhead.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are reshaping communications and networking by deploying autonomous intelligent agents capable of collaborative learning while maintaining data privacy at network edges. Within distributed network environments, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) serve as cognitive engines for edge devices, yet federated fine-tuning faces substantial challenges in balancing global knowledge aggregation with local adaptation under heterogeneous network conditions. Conventional federated protocols typically rely on uniform parameter aggregation, which conflates domain-invariant features with client-specific nuances, thereby resulting in suboptimal personalization and excessive communication overhead. To address these challenges, we propose PFAdapter, a communication-efficient framework introducing hierarchical LoRA decomposition to explicitly separate adapter parameters into global-shared and local-private components. Query and key projections are assigned to global synchronization for capturing universal multimodal semantics across the network, while value and output projections remain localized for edge-specific adaptation. Additionally, orthogonality regularization based on the Frobenius norm enforces strict separation between these components, preventing redundant feature learning. Selective aggregation protocols synchronize only global-shared components across the federated network, preserving local expertise and reducing communication costs by nearly 50%. Extensive experiments on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, Hateful Memes, and CrisisMMD datasets demonstrate that PFAdapter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving accuracy improvements ranging from 2.4% to 4.8% across diverse edge intelligence tasks. Consequently, our framework establishes an efficient solution for agentic AI deployment in resource-constrained communication networks.

</details>


### [128] [Continual Learning with Elastic Regularization and Synthetic Replay for Federated MLLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12112)
*Jing Liu,Chenxuanyin Zou,Jiayang Ren,Gaoyun Fang,Chengfang Li,Yan Wang,Zhenchao Ma,Bo Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCMM is a federated continual learning framework for MLLMs that mitigates catastrophic forgetting via three complementary mechanisms: modality-aware elastic weight consolidation (parameter-level), local generative replay without raw data sharing (data-level), and task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation (aggregation-level). It outperforms baselines on accuracy and backward transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning of MLLMs across distributed networks suffers from catastrophic forgetting—sequential task updates erase previously acquired knowledge across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations—which hinders robust deployment in dynamic, safety-sensitive environments like content moderation.

Method: FedCMM operates at three levels: (1) Parameter-level: modality-aware elastic weight consolidation using separate Fisher information matrices for vision encoder, language backbone, and cross-modal projector; (2) Data-level: local generative replay module synthesizing raw-data-free embedding-level multimodal replay tuples; (3) Aggregation-level: task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation that filters and reweights client updates by gradient cosine similarity.

Result: Experiments on two benchmarks show FedCMM consistently outperforms recent baselines on accuracy and backward transfer.

Conclusion: Holistic, modality-aware optimization enables robust evolutive adaptation across heterogeneous networked AI deployments, confirming that addressing catastrophic forgetting at multiple complementary levels is effective for federated continual multimodal learning.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across distributed networks enables privacy-sensitive adaptation to evolving data streams, yet a fundamental obstacle prevents robust deployment in dynamic environments: catastrophic forgetting, wherein sequential task updates erase previously acquired knowledge across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations. Addressing this challenge is especially critical for autonomous networked AI operating in safety-sensitive domains, such as content moderation, where reliable retention of prior knowledge underpins system integrity. To overcome this, we propose Federated Continual Multimodal Learning (FedCMM), a framework that embeds continual-learning safeguards into the federated optimization loop at three complementary levels. At the parameter level, modality-aware elastic weight consolidation computes separate Fisher information matrices for the vision encoder, language backbone, and cross-modal projector, providing granular, asymmetry-aware protection against modality-specific forgetting. At the data level, each client trains a lightweight local generative replay module to synthesize raw-data-free embedding-level multimodal replay tuples without any raw data sharing. At the aggregation level, Task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation autonomously filters and reweights client updates by gradient cosine similarity, suppressing conflicting directions and stabilizing the global learning trajectory. Extensive experiments on two benchmarks demonstrate that FedCMM consistently outperforms recent baselines on accuracy and backward transfer, confirming that holistic, modality-aware optimization enables robust evolutive adaptation across heterogeneous networked AI deployments.

</details>


### [129] [Evidence-Grounded Verified Agentic Reasoning: A Path Toward Eliminating LLM Hallucination in Empirical Inference via Tool-Attested Kernel Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12650)
*Junyu Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EG-VAR is a Lean 4-based architecture that enforces evidence-grounded and kernel-verified reasoning in LLMs. It guarantees every verified output descends from an attested tool call and valid formal inference, otherwise honestly abstains. On TableBench numerical reasoning it achieves 100% accuracy vs 95% baseline, and on stress tests maintains 100% source-faithfulness. This provides a technical-governance interface for high-stakes empirical claims, turning formalization errors, disputes, and abstentions into auditable targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool access alone does not ensure that LLM empirical reasoning is governable: accepted outputs may not originate from attested evidence, and accepted deductions may not withstand formal scrutiny. The authors aim to make LLM reasoning provably grounded in attested evidence and valid inference.

Method: EG-VAR (Evidence-Grounded Verified Agentic Reasoning) is a Lean 4-based tool-calling architecture where the Lean kernel is the sole minter of Verified claims via tool-attestation axioms and declared source lifts. Every verified output must structurally descend from an attested tool call (Thm. 3.1) and a kernel-checked chain of valid inference (Thm. 3.2); otherwise, the output is an honest Abstain with a replayable audit trail.

Result: On TableBench numerical reasoning (n=120): 120/120 vs 95% same-tool baseline. On counterfactual stress tests (5 domains × 2 models): EG-VAR stays 100% source-faithful while same-tool drops to 80-90% and no-tool to 50-80%. Residual semantic-formalization error: 3.3% on Sonnet, 1.7% on Opus.

Conclusion: EG-VAR positions itself as a technical-governance interface for high-stakes empirical claims, eliminating unsupported Verified outputs while turning formalization errors, lift and source-authority disputes, ambiguities, and abstentions into explicit audit targets. Over time, typed sidecars in datasets, APIs, public records, and AI-generated documents can amortize the formalization burden into reusable infrastructure.

Abstract: Tool access alone does not make LLM empirical reasoning governable: accepted outputs need not descend from attested evidence, and accepted deductions need not hold up under formal scrutiny. We present EG-VAR (Evidence-Grounded Verified Agentic Reasoning), a Lean 4-based tool-calling architecture in which the Lean kernel is the sole minter of Verified claims via tool-attestation axioms and declared source lifts. Every verified output structurally descends from an attested tool call (Thm. 3.1) and a kernel-checked chain of valid inference (Thm. 3.2); residual outputs are honest Abstain with a replayable audit trail. On a subcollection of TableBench numerical reasoning (n=120), EG-VAR attains 120/120 versus a 95% same-tool baseline; on counterfactual stress tests (5 domains x 2 models), EG-VAR stays 100% source-faithful while same-tool drops to 80-90% (no-tool 50-80%). With the LLM as deployment-time formalizer, residual semantic-formalization error is 3.3% on Sonnet and 1.7% on Opus. We position EG-VAR as a technical-governance interface for high-stakes empirical claims: a formal sidecar makes the target proposition, source scope, evidence boundary, proof obligation, and abstention condition auditable, eliminating unsupported Verified outputs today while turning formalization errors, lift and source-authority disputes, ambiguities, and abstentions into explicit audit targets. Over time, typed sidecars in datasets, APIs, public records, and AI-generated documents can amortize this formalization burden into reusable infrastructure.

</details>


### [130] [An Agentic AI Scientific Community for Automated Neural Operator Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12122)
*Luis Loo,Ulisses Braga-Neto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An agentic AI scientific community with virtual labs (LLM planner, numerical worker, LLM reviewer) operating under a citation-based economy autonomously discovers neural operator architectures. It hybridizes building blocks (DeepONet, Fourier, Transformer, wavelet, residual CNN) achieving high accuracy with low parameters. LLM agency is critical for diversity; without it, the community collapses to non-hybridized single-family stacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To autonomously discover neural operator architectures in a decentralized, self-organizing manner, moving beyond manual design and demonstrating that no single architecture universally dominates (a no-free-lunch theorem for neural operators).

Method: A swarm of virtual laboratories with citation-based economy: each lab has an LLM planner (proposes architecture), a numerical worker (trains/measures), and an LLM reviewer (cross-lab peer review). Labs share building blocks: DeepONet, Fourier, Transformer (attention), wavelet, residual CNN. Evaluated on 5 problems (piecewise regression, advection/Burgers 1D PDEs, Navier-Stokes/Darcy 2D PDEs) with 3 repeats each. 9,623 LLM calls logged; ablation replaces LLM agents with rule-based alternatives.

Result: The community discovers high-accuracy, low-parameter-count neural operators. LLM planners hybridize in 99.8% of decisions, returning multi-family hybrids. Ablation with rule-based agents collapses to non-hybridized single-family stacks, confirming LLM agency preserves diversity. Results suggest a no-free-lunch theorem for neural operators.

Conclusion: Autonomous AI scientific communities can effectively discover neural operator architectures. LLM-based agency is essential for maintaining architectural diversity and hybridization; rule-based alternatives fail. No single neural operator architecture universally dominates, implying a no-free-lunch principle in this domain.

Abstract: We present an agentic approach to autonomous neural operator discovery based on an AI scientific community, which consists of a swarm of virtual laboratories that interact under a citation-based economy of influence. Highly-cited labs found new labs that follow their research direction and replace non-performing labs. Each virtual lab contains three agents: an LLM planner that proposes an architecture, a numerical worker that trains and measures it, and an LLM reviewer that participates in cross-lab peer review. All labs share a common vocabulary consisting of DeepONet (branch-trunk), Fourier, Transformer (attention), wavelet, and residual convolutional neural operator building blocks. We evaluate the neural operator AI scientific community on five problems, namely piecewise regression, the linear advection and Burgers 1D PDEs, and the Navier-Stokes and Darcy flow 2D PDEs, while repeating the simulation three times for each problem. The results show that the neural operator AI scientific community is capable of discovering high-accuracy, low-parameter-count neural operator architectures. All 9,623 LLM calls are logged and audited, which reveals that the virtual lab LLM planners choose to hybridize in 99.8% of their logged decisions, consistently returning multi-family hybrids. Moreover, we conducted an ablation study by replacing the LLM agents in each lab by rule-based alternatives, which caused the scientific community to collapse to non-hybridized single-family stacks in several cases, showing that LLM agency is needed to preserve diversity. The results suggest a no-free-lunch theorem for neural operators: there is no universal winner. The code, configurations, and the complete LLM transcripts are released at https://github.com/luislootx/AI-SC.

</details>


### [131] [From Geometric Recovery to Causal Validation: A Reproducible Audit of Sparse Autoencoder Features, from Superposition Geometry to Causal Inertness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12166)
*Mohamed Abdessalem Bal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sparse autoencoder evaluation using cosine similarity conflates decoder-geometry alignment with encoder-activation behavior. Up to 77% of features passing recovery bars (cosine ≥ 0.90) in degraded SAEs and 9% in well-trained ones are causally inert—the matched atom never fires when the feature is present, even at cosine ~1.000. Inertness decomposes into structural inertness (antipodal pairs) and competitive inertness (TopK pathology), plus read/write directions. The authors provide sae-causal-audit, a model-agnostic auditing tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SAE evaluation relies on cosine similarity between ground-truth directions and decoder atoms, which conflates decoder-geometry alignment with encoder-activation behavior, potentially masking causally inert features.

Method: The authors reproduce superposition phase diagrams and TopK-vs-L1 comparisons, then apply causal auditing (ablation and steering) to test whether recovered features are causally active. They package the method as sae-causal-audit.

Result: Up to 77% of features passing a recovery bar in degraded SAEs and 9% in well-trained ones are causally inert. Inertness decomposes into structural inertness (antipodal-pair geometry) and competitive inertness (TopK pathology), and by direction into read- and write-inertness. Five antipodal pairs completely dissociate read/write inertness. A production SAE shows 14% inertness and an atom-collision signal.

Conclusion: Cosine-based recovery metrics are insufficient for evaluating SAE quality. Causal auditing reveals widespread inertness even at high cosine similarity. The authors propose reporting results as a stack of claims with explicit scopes and provide a reproducible auditing pipeline.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are the standard for decomposing superposed neural representations into interpretable features, and evaluation relies predominantly on correlational recovery metrics -- cosine similarity between ground-truth directions and decoder atoms. We show this conflates two distinct claims: decoder-geometry alignment and encoder-activation behavior. We reproduce the superposition phase diagram of Elhage et al. (2022), identifying a convergence artifact at high sparsity and an under-described diffuse sharing regime at extreme overcompleteness. We reproduce the TopK-versus-L1 comparison of Gao et al. (2024), with direct evidence of L1 shrinkage. Our central result is causal: subjecting every recovered feature to ablation and steering, we find up to 77% of features passing a recovery bar (cosine >= 0.90) in a degraded SAE -- and 9% in a well-trained one -- are causally inert: the matched atom never fires when the feature is present, including matches at cosine ~1.000. We package the method as sae-causal-audit, a model-agnostic instrument with a deterministic pipeline. Re-auditing refines the finding: inertness decomposes by cause into structural inertness (antipodal-pair geometry, present in good SAEs) and competitive inertness (a TopK pathology of degraded SAEs), and by direction into read- and write-inertness, which five antipodal pairs dissociate completely -- unmonitorable yet steerable through the same atom, with steering specificities of 143-310 attached to zero ablation effects. We document why byte-exact reproducibility is unavailable by construction, and propose reporting it as a stack of claims with explicit scopes. Applying the instrument to a production SAE reproduces the pattern at small scale (14% inert) and surfaces an atom-collision signal: a handful of atoms recur as the nearest match for dozens of unrelated concepts, replicated across three batches.

</details>


### [132] [Forgetful Attention: A Trainable Support-Vector Memory with Certified Selection and Exact Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12204)
*Vishwajith Ramesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SV-Attention introduces a max-margin memory (one-class SVM with box constraint) whose active-set partition assigns zero weight to reserve tokens, certifying output-preserving eviction and enabling exact deletion via a reversible incremental solver. It achieves certified token selection (0.86 vs 0.32 rare-item recall), surgical forgetting, and 8.6% BPC improvement over sliding-window Transformers on enwik8, though at 35.8× slower throughput than softmax attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time memories (attention) cannot certify that dropping a token leaves outputs unchanged or enable exact deletion of a token's influence, which limits interpretability, privacy (right-to-be-forgotten), and online learning guarantees.

Method: Formulates attention as a one-class SVM with fixed box parameter C; weights are support coefficients; active-set partition gives reserve (non-support) tokens exactly zero weight for output-preserving eviction; a reversible incremental solver deletes a token and recovers the retrained state exactly; training uses a stabilized batched approximation.

Result: In fp64, decrement and refit recover identical partitions (unique optimum); decision functions match to median deviation ~10^-9 (10^-13 on learned keys); certified selection achieves 0.86 vs 0.32 rare-item recall; hybrid obtains 2.178 vs 2.383 BPC on enwik8 (8.6% improvement, p=0.001); 3.22M-param model processes 9,125 tokens/s (35.8× slower than MPS softmax).

Conclusion: SV-Attention provides the first attention mechanism with certified output-preserving token eviction and exact deletion, enabling surgical forgetting, exact editing, and patient-record deletion with strong empirical results, though at a significant computational cost during training.

Abstract: Attention can be viewed as an online learner over context, yet existing test-time memories cannot certify that dropping a token leaves outputs unchanged or delete its influence outright. We introduce Support Vector Attention (SV-Attention), a max-margin memory whose weights are support coefficients of a one-class SVM with fixed box parameter C. Its active-set partition gives reserve tokens exactly zero weight, certifying output-preserving eviction; a reversible incremental solver deletes a token to recover the state produced by retraining without it under the same C. In fp64 experiments, decrement and refit recover identical partitions whenever the optimum is unique, and their decision functions match to a median deviation of about 10^-9 (10^-13 on learned keys); the 10^-2 worst case is confined to ill-conditioned duplicates and remains below coefficient decay in every regime. The exact path reuses the maintained KKT inverse in a custom backward. Training uses a separate stabilized batched approximation and does not carry the exact-deletion certificate; it reaches 9,125 tokens/s on a 3.22M-parameter model, while remaining 35.8 times slower than an MPS softmax reference. At matched budgets, certified selection reaches 0.86 vs. 0.32 rare-item recall and retains 0.80 vs. 0.05 deterioration hours on real MIMIC-IV streams. We also demonstrate surgical forgetting, exact editing, patient-record deletion, and a forgettable retrieval memory over real sentence embeddings. On enwik8, the hybrid obtains 2.178 BPC vs. 2.383 for a matched-state sliding-window Transformer across seven seeds (8.6% paired improvement, p=0.001); a three-seed TinyStories result is directionally positive but not significant (p=0.057).

</details>


### [133] [Speculate with Memory: Lossless Acceleration for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12236)
*Yu Li,Qinyuan Ye,Prafulla Kumar Choubey,Jiaxin Zhang,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM agent speculative execution is improved by adding three memory systems (contrastive transition table, episodic memory, confusion tracker) that learn from past trajectories, yielding 19-39% accuracy gains in action prediction and up to 2.5x in observation prediction without wall-clock cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculators for LLM agents are stateless and discard all information between tasks, preventing prediction quality from improving with experience.

Method: Equipped the speculator with three online memory systems: a contrastive transition table tracking action-sequence statistics, an episodic memory retrieving contextually similar segments, and a confusion tracker suppressing recurring errors.

Result: 19-39% relative accuracy improvement on action prediction; up to 2.5x increase on observation prediction tasks with repetitive action spaces; gains grow continuously with memory accumulation and generalize across speculator models.

Conclusion: Memory-augmented speculation is lossless (zero added wall-clock cost, identical actor trajectory to non-speculative execution) and yields substantial, cumulative gains across multiple speculation types and benchmarks.

Abstract: Speculative execution accelerates LLM agents by using a smaller, cheaper model to predict and pre-launch the next step while the environment is idle. However, existing speculators are stateless and discard all information between tasks, preventing prediction quality from improving with experience. We equip the speculator with three online memory systems that learn from past agent trajectories: a contrastive transition table tracking action-sequence statistics, an episodic memory retrieving contextually similar segments, and a confusion tracker suppressing recurring errors. We evaluate this approach on six benchmarks spanning three speculation types: action prediction, observation prediction, and chained prediction. Memory-augmented speculation yields a 19--39\% relative accuracy improvement on action prediction and up to a $2.5\times$ increase on observation prediction tasks with repetitive action spaces. These gains grow continuously as memory accumulates and generalize across speculator models of varying cost. All speculation is lossless because it runs during idle time at zero added wall-clock cost, and the actor's trajectory is identical to non-speculative execution.

</details>


### [134] [Cluster-Weighted EDMD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12243)
*Lorenzo Tomaz,Judd Rosenblatt,Flavio Kicis,Thomas B. Jones,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CW-EDMD jointly learns a soft phase-space partition and per-cluster EDMD operators via an EM objective that weights transitions by both geometric proximity and prediction residuals, outperforming global EDMD across Lorenz, damped pendulum, and Duffing systems with significant error reductions in most cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global EDMD is inefficient when different state-space regions exhibit distinct local dynamics; the paper seeks to address this by learning local Koopman operators specialized to different phase-space regions.

Method: Cluster-Weighted EDMD (CW-EDMD) uses an EM objective that assigns each transition based on both geometric proximity and prediction residuals, jointly learning a soft phase-space partition and a per-cluster EDMD operator so that clusters specialize where local Koopman models are accurate.

Result: On Lorenz, damped pendulum, and Duffing systems across 36 configurations and 10 seeds, CW-EDMD improved over matched-degree EDMD in one-step and 5s-rollout prediction. Across 288 paired comparisons, significant error reductions occurred in 258 cases, increases in 4, and no differences in 26. Median one-step error reductions: 57x (pendulum), 2.7x (Duffing), 12x (Lorenz).

Conclusion: CW-EDMD significantly outperforms global EDMD by learning locally specialized Koopman operators, achieving substantial error reductions across multiple nonlinear dynamical systems.

Abstract: Extended Dynamic Mode Decomposition (EDMD) approximates Koopman operators from data, but a single global operator is inefficient when different state-space regions exhibit distinct local dynamics. We introduce Cluster-Weighted EDMD (CW-EDMD), which jointly learns a soft phase-space partition and a per-cluster EDMD operator. Its Expectation-Maximization (EM) objective assigns each transition based on both geometric proximity and prediction residuals, so clusters specialize where local Koopman models are accurate rather than where the data are dense. On Lorenz, damped pendulum, and Duffing systems, across 36 configurations and 10 seeds, CW-EDMD improves matched-degree EDMD in one-step and 5s-rollout prediction. Across 288 paired comparisons, there are significant error reductions in 258 cases, increases in 4, and no differences in 26. Median one-step error reductions are 57x, 2.7x, and 12x on pendulum, Duffing, and Lorenz, respectively.

</details>


### [135] [Proximity Features: Privacy-Compliant Cold-Start Personalization at Airbnb](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12246)
*Wei Jiang,Bin Xu,Hui Gao,Bharathi Thangamani,Weiwei Guo,Sundar Srinivasavaradhan,Tracy Yu,Huiji Gao,Michael Kinoti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Airbnb introduces Proximity Features, a privacy-compliant system that aggregates user behavior signals from geographically proximate users (groups of ~1,000) using geo-IP and adaptive clustering, improving recommendations for users with no or limited history, achieving significant booking lifts in production A/B tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A large fraction of Airbnb users (logged-out, first-time, or cookie-restricted) lack sufficient history for personalization in two-sided marketplaces, especially on paid-channel landing pages, and privacy regulations further limit identifier-based tracking.

Method: Proximity Features: geo-IP based adaptive clustering algorithm groups ~1,000 nearby users, aggregates their behavioral signals into user-level features, operates only on consented aggregated data without persistent individual identifiers at inference time.

Result: Deployed in production at Airbnb across multiple surfaces (marketing landing pages, destination recommendation), online A/B experiments show statistically significant booking lifts, with largest gains among users with absent or stale history.

Conclusion: Proximity-based aggregated signals effectively substitute for individual user-level features in privacy-restricted, sparse-history scenarios, improving recommendation and conversion in two-sided marketplaces.

Abstract: Personalization in two-sided marketplaces relies heavily on user-level features, yet for platforms with infrequent, high-consideration purchases, a large fraction of users lack sufficient history for effective recommendation, spanning both paid and organic channels. At Airbnb, a substantial share of search requests comes from logged-out or first-time users, with this challenge especially pronounced on paid-channel landing pages, leaving traditional user-level features unavailable for a large fraction of traffic. Privacy regulations and increasing restrictions on third-party cookies further limit identifier-based tracking for non-essential use cases. This paper introduces Proximity Features, a privacy-compliant feature system that groups users by geographic proximity using geo-IP data and an adaptive clustering algorithm, producing aggregated user-level signals for groups of approximately 1,000 nearby users without requiring a persistent individual identifier at inference time. Privacy is preserved by design: the pipeline operates on consented, aggregated data only within consent-gated privacy controls.
  The system is deployed in production at Airbnb, serving multiple surfaces including marketing landing pages and destination recommendation, with engagement emails integration under way. Online A/B experiments demonstrate statistically significant lifts in bookings, with the largest gains observed among users with absent or stale history.

</details>


### [136] [Understanding Structured Health Data through Interaction-Aware Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12255)
*Ji Hwan Park,Ying Ding,Tianjin Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Interaction-aware mixture-of-experts is used for post-stroke rigidity prediction from multi-level structured health records; routing attribution reveals that view construction matters more for interpretability than for performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To predict post-stroke rigidity using structured health records, and to understand how different views of the data contribute to prediction and interpretability.

Method: Interaction-aware mixture-of-experts (MoE) model applied to multi-level structured health records, with routing attribution used to analyze importance across views.

Result: Minimal performance gains from the MoE approach, but routing attribution reveals systematic differences in importance across data views, highlighting that view construction is key for interpretability.

Conclusion: View construction significantly impacts interpretability even when performance gains are minimal, emphasizing the need for careful design of data views in predictive health models.

Abstract: We study interaction-aware mixture-of-experts for post-stroke rigidity prediction using multi-level views of structured health records. Despite minimal performance gains, routing attribution reveals systematic importance differences across views, underscoring view construction as key to interpretability.

</details>


### [137] [From Many to Meaningful: Feature-Guided Zero-Shot Chronic Kidney Disease Screening Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12260)
*Muhammad Ashad Kabir,Sirajam Munira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study proposes a feature-guided zero-shot framework using large language models (LLMs) for early-stage CKD screening without dataset-specific training, using minimal clinically meaningful features and demonstrating strong performance across three heterogeneous datasets, suggesting LLMs offer a practical, training-free complement to conventional ML methods for community-based screening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early screening of chronic kidney disease (CKD) is critical to prevent irreversible progression, but existing ML-based methods are difficult to deploy in community and resource-limited settings due to reliance on large labeled datasets, resource-intensive pathology tests, high-dimensional features, and limited robustness to population shifts. This motivates exploring LLMs for zero-shot CKD screening using minimal, readily available features.

Method: The study proposes a feature-guided zero-shot framework: (1) ML-based analysis guides feature selection to identify a compact, clinically relevant subset of variables from full clinical inputs; (2) tabular patient records are serialized into text using standardized prompt templates for zero-shot inference; (3) four LLMs (LLaMA-3, Qwen-3, Mistral, GPT-4o-mini) are evaluated in zero-shot setting using both full and selected feature sets across three heterogeneous CKD datasets from three countries.

Result: Across models and datasets, the selected compact feature set yielded consistent and statistically significant improvements in balanced accuracy and probability estimates compared to using the full feature set, achieving performance levels suitable for screening purposes.

Conclusion: LLMs can support clinically meaningful, training-free CKD screening using minimal community-accessible patient features, offering a practical complement to conventional ML methods in real-world screening contexts, particularly in resource-limited settings.

Abstract: Early screening of chronic kidney disease (CKD) is essential for preventing irreversible progression; however, many machine learning (ML)-based screening methods remain difficult to deploy in community and resource-limited screening settings due to their reliance on large labeled datasets, resource-intensive pathology tests, or high-dimensional clinical features, and limited robustness to population and distributional shifts. This study examines the feasibility of using large language models (LLMs) for early-stage CKD screening in a zero-shot setting, without dataset-specific training. We propose a feature-guided zero-shot framework that evaluates LLM performance using a selected set of clinically meaningful, readily available community-based features, rather than exhaustive clinical inputs. Feature selection was guided by ML-based analysis to identify a compact, clinically relevant subset of variables. Tabular patient records were subsequently serialized into text using standardized prompt templates to enable zero-shot inference. The zero-shot performance of four LLMs (LLaMA-3, Qwen-3, Mistral, and GPT-4o-mini) was evaluated using both the full feature set and the selected subset. Generalizability was assessed across three heterogeneous CKD datasets spanning three countries. Across models and datasets, the selected feature set yielded consistent and statistically significant improvements in balanced accuracy and probability estimates, achieving performance levels suitable for screening purposes. These findings suggest that LLMs can support clinically meaningful, training-free CKD screening using minimal community-accessible patient features, offering a practical complement to conventional ML methods in real-world screening contexts.

</details>


### [138] [Saturation Makes Quantization Error Additive: A Coverage Model with a Certificate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12266)
*Joshua Hill*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mixed-precision quantization's loss function can be reliably approximated by per-layer additive effects (85-93% variance explained) or a coverage model, enabling effective allocation even below 4 bits where sensitivity-based methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the common premise in mixed-precision quantization that loss from quantizing a set of layers can be reconstructed from per-layer or pairwise sensitivities measured in isolation, especially at the 4-bit weight-and-activation precisions now being deployed.

Method: Treats the change in loss from quantizing a layer set as a set function on the Boolean cube, analyzes it through two classical changes of basis, proposes a coverage model f(S)=c(1-∏(1-a_i)), and derives two predictors: an additive model (optimal first-order predictor) and the coverage model itself.

Result: Per-layer effects explain 85-93% of variance; monotone transform of per-layer sum misorders at most 2% of pairs; coverage model reproduces variance profile within a few percent; both allocators achieve lowest KL divergence on models from 30B to 355B parameters; below 4 bits, they solve tasks where gradient-sensitivity allocations fail.

Conclusion: Additive per-layer effects are sufficient for accurate mixed-precision quantization allocation, and the proposed coverage model provides effective allocation even in sub-4-bit regimes where prior sensitivity-based methods break down.

Abstract: Mixed-precision quantization must decide which parts of a model to keep at higher precision. A common premise, shared by sensitivity-based methods such as HAWQ and CoopQ, is that the loss from quantizing a set of layers can be reconstructed from per-layer or pairwise sensitivities measured in isolation. We test this premise at the 4-bit weight-and-activation precisions now being deployed, treating the change in loss $f(S)$ from quantizing a layer set $S$ as a set function on the Boolean cube and analyzing it through two classical changes of basis. This analysis yields two findings. First, across configurations drawn from the deployment distribution, 85--93\% of the variance of $f$ is explained by per-layer effects alone. Second, a monotone transform of a sum of per-layer terms reproduces $f$'s ranking of configurations, misordering at most 2\% of pairs.
  We propose the coverage model $f(S)=c\bigl(1-\prod_{i\in S}(1-a_i)\bigr)$, which reproduces the measured variance profile of $f$ to within a few percent from its $L$ fitted break-rates. This structure supports two predictors of a configuration's loss, each with $L+1$ parameters. The additive model is the optimal first-order predictor. By Parseval's identity its mean-squared error equals the variance of $f$ left unexplained by per-layer effects, which we measure on full lattices, estimate out of sample at full-network scale, and report with every result as a certificate of how well any additive model can do. The coverage model itself is the second predictor.
  As allocators at matched memory, they attain the lowest KL divergence among the compared allocators on models from 30B to 355B parameters. Below four bits, the resulting allocations continue to solve code and reasoning tasks at budgets where allocations from gradient sensitivities no longer produce terminating generations.

</details>


### [139] [Track, Rank, Crack: Epistemic Working Memory Scales Multi-Hop Reasoning in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12267)
*Ning Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLEUTH improves multi-hop reasoning agents by introducing structured epistemic working memory (Confirmed Facts, Active Hypotheses, Open Questions), which prevents context dilution and outperforms baselines by up to +19 points on hardest problems; the key insight is that how an agent organizes reasoning matters more than raw model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents that interleave reasoning and tool use degrade sharply as reasoning chains lengthen due to context dilution—early discoveries get buried under later retrievals in the growing context window.

Method: SLEUTH (Structured Epistemic Working Memory) makes the agent's investigative state explicit via three components: Confirmed Facts (grounded to sources), Active Hypotheses (ranked by evidence), and Open Questions (directly drive next action). Additionally, a lightweight commitment trigger is introduced to address the evidence sufficiency problem.

Result: Across five multi-hop benchmarks and five baselines, SLEUTH's advantage grows with difficulty: +5 on HotpotQA to +11 on 4-hop chains, surpassing Reflexion without multiple episodes. Commitment trigger works only with structured state (no improvement on unstructured agents). Enforcing protocol adherence on weaker models recovers +19 points on hardest problems.

Conclusion: Organized epistemic state, not raw model capability, is the necessary and active ingredient for scaling multi-hop reasoning.

Abstract: Language agents that interleave reasoning and tool use degrade sharply as reasoning chains lengthen, even when each individual step is easy. We trace this to context dilution: an agent's investigative state (what it has confirmed, what it suspects, and what it still needs) lives only implicitly in a growing context window, where early discoveries are buried under later retrievals. We introduce SLEUTH, which makes this state explicit and actionable through a structured epistemic working memory: the agent maintains Confirmed Facts grounded to sources, Active Hypotheses ranked by evidence, and Open Questions that directly drive its next action. Across five multi-hop benchmarks and five established baselines, SLEUTH's advantage grows with difficulty, from +5 points on HotpotQA to +11 on 4-hop chains, surpassing Reflexion without multiple episodes. Analyzing where the remaining gap lies, we identify the evidence sufficiency problem: agents often find the answer but fail to commit, exhausting their budget on needless verification. A lightweight commitment trigger fixes this, but only when the agent already maintains structured state: the identical trigger applied to an unstructured agent yields no improvement, isolating organized epistemic state as the necessary condition for effective commitment. Finally, enforcing protocol adherence on a weaker model recovers up to +19 points on the hardest problems, showing that how an agent organizes its reasoning, not raw model capability, is the active ingredient for scaling multi-hop reasoning.

</details>


### [140] [Quantum Port-Hamiltonian Neural Networks: Learning Conservative and Dissipative Dynamics via Measurement-Induced Nonlinearity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12269)
*Dibakar Sigdel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Q-pHNNs are parameterized quantum circuits that learn classical dynamics while preserving physical structure (conservation and passivity) via a novel Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping, achieving low energy drift and accurate damping identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical neural networks for learning physical dynamics often fail to enforce structural properties like energy conservation and passivity, relying on penalty terms that do not guarantee compliance. A quantum-native framework that embeds these properties by construction is needed.

Method: The Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping (IHM) maps the skew-symmetric interconnection matrix J to unitary gates and the dissipation matrix R to Measurement-Induced NonLinearity (MINL) via mid-circuit measurement and classical feedforward. Four architectures are instantiated: Quantum HNN (conservative), Q-pHNN with Born-rule dissipation, joint energy-damping Q-pHNN, and topology-entangled Quantum Graph Neural Network for coupled-phasor networks.

Result: On nonlinear pendulum and damped harmonic oscillator: 1.35% relative energy drift with symplectic integrator and scale correction; 100% energy monotonicity for MINL circuit; 12.1% error in damping-coefficient identification from vector-field snapshots without direct supervision.

Conclusion: Q-pHNNs provide a structure-preserving quantum learning framework where conservation and passivity are enforced by construction, with strong empirical results on benchmark dynamical systems.

Abstract: We introduce Quantum Port-Hamiltonian Neural Networks (Q-pHNNs), a family of parameterised quantum circuits that learn classical dynamics in a structure-preserving manner. The framework relies on the Isomorphic Hamiltonian Mapping (IHM): the skew-symmetric interconnection matrix $\mathbf{J}$ corresponds to unitary gate evolution, and the positive-semidefinite dissipation matrix $\mathbf{R}$ corresponds to Measurement-Induced NonLinearity (MINL) realised via mid-circuit measurement and classical feedforward. This ensures conservation and passivity are enforced by construction rather than penalty terms. We instantiate the IHM in four architectures: (1) a Quantum HNN that learns conservative energy manifolds and extracts Hamilton's equations exactly via the Parameter-Shift Rule; (2) a Q-pHNN using Born-rule measurement for dissipation; (3) a Q-pHNN jointly learning the energy ansatz and damping coefficient; and (4) a topology-entangled Quantum Graph Neural Network for $N$-node coupled-phasor networks. Experiments on the nonlinear pendulum and damped harmonic oscillator demonstrate: (i)~$1.35\%$ relative energy drift with a symplectic integrator and scale correction; (ii)~$100\%$ energy monotonicity for the MINL circuit; and (iii)~$12.1\%$ error in damping-coefficient identification from vector-field snapshots with no direct supervision on the damping coefficient.

</details>


### [141] [A hybrid analytical-PINN model for subsurface simulation of geothermal heat exchangers in heterogeneous underground](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12271)
*Moke Rao,Thomas Hamacher,Smajil Halilovic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A parametric physics-informed neural network is developed to solve heterogeneous soil thermal problems with borehole heat exchangers, using analytical line source models to remove singularities, explicit gradient thermal conductivity formulation, and a learned universal corrector via superposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of solving heterogeneous soil thermal problems with BHEs as singular sources, where direct PINN training is hindered by delta function singularities and heterogeneous conductivity variations.

Method: (1) Decomposes temperature change and transforms the problem into a correction diffusion/advection-diffusion equation with homogeneous initial condition, removing singularities via analytical line source models. (2) Uses explicit gradient thermal conductivity formulation for physics-informed parametrization learning. (3) Trains a PINN to approximate a universal corrector for a single borehole with unit heat extraction rate, using location indicator function as feature input. Loss function combines physics-informed and data-anchored terms, evaluated on adaptively selected training points with sampled conductivity parameters.

Result: Numerical tests demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method based on three different analytical models, showing that the framework successfully handles heterogeneous thermal conductivity and singular sources.

Conclusion: The proposed PINN framework with singularity removal, explicit conductivity parametrization, and learned universal corrector provides an effective and efficient approach for heterogeneous soil thermal problems with BHEs.

Abstract: In this paper, a parametric physics-informed neural network for solving the heterogeneous soil thermal problem with borehole heat exchangers (BHEs) as singular sources is developed. There are three novel features in the present framework; namely, (i) the singularity is naturally removed by using analytical line source models; (ii) using the explicit formulation for gradient thermal conductivity enables physics-informed learning of the parametrization featuring the conductivity; (iii) the learned correction is utilized as an efficient universal corrector via superposition principles. We first introduce the decomposition of the temperature change and transform the approximation of the entire heterogeneous response to the correction compensating the difference between the practical solution and idealized homogeneous approximation. In such a way, the delta function singularity is excluded and the bulk heat transfer is captured for the sake of facilitating the effective training of the neural network. The original problem is then reformulated as a governing correction diffusion or advection-diffusion equation subject to a homogeneous initial condition. The linearly varying thermal conductivity is used to model the soil heterogeneity. We propose a physics-informed neural network to approximate a universal corrector with respect to a single borehole with unit heat extraction rate. As a result, the network is trained by minimizing the physics-informed and data-anchored loss function that is evaluated for sampled conductivity parameters on adaptively selected training points. In addition, we include the location indicator function regarding the source as a feature input of network and find that it helps the network to process the local information. We perform numerical tests to exhibit the effectiveness of the proposed method based on three different analytical models.

</details>


### [142] [Gradient Flow Dynamics and Implicit Bias of Diagonal Linear Networks under Infinitesimal Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12332)
*Jiajie Zhao,Jianxing Wang,Junjie Yang,Zhiwei Bai,Yaoyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends the analysis of gradient flow dynamics in diagonal linear networks under infinitesimal initialization, showing that training trajectories for both deep and two-layer diagonal linear networks can be characterized by Algorithm 1, which converges to a modified ℓ1 norm minimization problem, establishing that the implicit bias corresponds to this modified ℓ1 norm, and identifying the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) as the key geometric structure governing the dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize previous results (Pesme & Flammarion, 2023) on gradient flow dynamics of diagonal linear networks under infinitesimal initialization to deeper architectures and a broader class of two-layer diagonal linear networks, and to characterize the implicit bias of these models.

Method: The authors extend Theorem 1 from prior work, analyze both deep diagonal linear networks and a broader class of two-layer diagonal linear networks (Definition 4.1), propose Algorithm 1 to equivalently characterize training trajectories, and identify the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) as the geometric structure shaping learning.

Result: Training trajectories are characterized by Algorithm 1, which converges to a modified ℓ1 norm minimization solution, establishing that the implicit bias corresponds to a modified ℓ1 norm under infinitesimal initialization for both architectures.

Conclusion: The implicit bias of diagonal linear networks under infinitesimal initialization corresponds to a modified ℓ1 norm minimization, and the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) is the key geometric structure underlying these dynamics.

Abstract: We study the gradient flow dynamics of diagonal linear networks for regression tasks under infinitesimal initialization. Extending Theorem 1 from Pesme & Flammarion (2023), we generalize the analysis to both deep diagonal linear networks and a broader class of two-layer diagonal linear networks (as defined in Definition 4.1). Specifically, we demonstrate that the training trajectories of these models can be equivalently characterized by the proposed Algorithm 1. We further prove that this algorithm converges to the solution of a modified $ \mathcal{l}_1 $ norm minimization problem. As a result, we establish that the implicit bias of both network architectures corresponds to a modified $ \mathcal{l}_1 $ norm in the regime of infinitesimal initialization. Additionally, we provide insights into the underlying mechanisms governing these dynamics by identifying the Structural Invariant Manifold (SIM) (Zhao et al., 2026) as the key geometric structure that shapes the learning process.

</details>


### [143] [Generating Developable 3D Molecules via Pocket-Conditioned Diffusion and Property-Aware Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12349)
*Ruoxi Gao,Jiangweizhi Peng,Ziqi Chen,Frazier N. Baker,David C. Kombo,John L. Kane,Andrew A. Scholte,Yi Li,Matthew J. LaMarche,Luigi I. Iconaru,Hans-Peter Biemann,Mingyi Hong,Xia Ning*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: conDitar-dev is a conditional diffusion-based SBDD framework that generates molecules with strong binding affinities and favorable ADMET properties, outperforming baselines on human disease targets and validated experimentally on PD-L1 and CSF1R targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug discovery is time-consuming and resource-intensive; existing diffusion-based SBDD methods couple pocket and ligand representation, model only atom-level interactions, and prioritize binding affinity over developability properties (e.g., ADMET).

Method: Three modules: (1) msPRL (multi-scale pretrained pocket representation learning), (2) conDitar (pocket-conditioned diffusion model guided by msPRL), (3) paOPT (generation-time optimization for ligand developability).

Result: Average binding score of -8.85 kcal/mol; up to 73% improvement over conDitar across five ADMET properties; experimentally validated: PD-L1 binders with KD of 3.49 and 3.75 μM via SPR; CSF1R inhibitors with IC50 as low as 200 nM.

Conclusion: conDitar-dev effectively generates developable molecules with strong binding affinity and favorable ADMET profiles, validated by wet-lab experiments and hit expansion, including drug repositioning opportunities.

Abstract: Drug discovery and development is time-consuming and resource-intensive, motivating computational approaches such as diffusion models for de novo drug design. Many such models follow the structure-based drug design (SBDD) paradigm, generating molecules to fit a target binding pocket. However, existing diffusion-based SBDD methods typically couple pocket and ligand representation learning, model interactions only at the atom level, and prioritize binding affinity over other developability properties. Here, we introduce conDitar-dev, a conditional diffusion-based SBDD framework for generating ligands with strong binding affinities and favorable ADMET properties. It consists of three modules: msPRL, a pretrained multi-scale pocket representation learning module; conDitar, a pocket-conditioned diffusion model guided by msPRL representations; and paOPT, a generation-time method for optimizing ligand developability. On a newly curated benchmark of human disease targets, conDitar outperforms state-of-the-art SBDD baselines, achieving an average binding score of -8.85 kcal/mol. Across five ADMET properties, conDitar-dev improves performance by up to 73% over conDitar. To further validate the abilities of conDitar-dev to generate developable molecules, we have applied it to two validated druggable targets: programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-L1) and colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R) proteins. Top-ranked generatively designed molecules and their analogs have been experimentally synthesized and biologically tested. Two molecules generated directly by conDitar-dev for PD-L1 exhibited SPR-derived $K_D$ values of 3.49 and 3.75 $μ$M, respectively. Hit expansion based on conDitar-dev-designed molecules identified selective CSF1R inhibitors with IC$_{50}$ values as low as 200 nM, while also uncovering opportunities for drug repositioning.

</details>


### [144] [Reducing information dependency does not cause training data privacy. Adversarially non-robust features do](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12354)
*Rasmus Torp,Shailen K. Smith,Adam Breuer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the belief that information dependency (memorization) drives training data exposure in image reconstruction attacks. It shows exposure persists without memorization and is instead linked to adversarial robustness. The authors find that models relying on "non-robust" features resist Model Inversion Attacks (MIAs), even when memorizing data or missing most training pixels. They introduce Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT), which learns non-robust features intentionally, achieving better reconstruction defense and higher accuracy than state-of-the-art defenses, revealing a new privacy-robustness tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing view that information dependency (rote memorization) drives training data exposure in image reconstruction attacks, and to explain why recent defenses that inhibit Model Inversion Attacks (MIAs) do not reduce standard measures of information dependency.

Method: The authors present three surprising empirical findings, provide causal evidence linking MIA privacy to non-robust features from the adversarial examples literature, and introduce Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT), a training regime that intentionally learns non-robust features.

Result: (1) Recent MIA defenses do not reduce HSIC-based information dependency; (2) models that maximally memorize training data remain robust to MIA reconstruction; (3) models trained without seeing 97% of training pixels can still be reconstructed by MIA. AT-AT achieves superior reconstruction defense and higher accuracy than state-of-the-art defenses.

Conclusion: Training data exposure under MIA is not driven by memorization but by a tunable connection to adversarial robustness via non-robust features. The paper revises the prevailing understanding of training data exposure and reveals a new privacy-robustness tradeoff.

Abstract: In this paper, we challenge the prevailing view that information dependency (including rote memorization) drives training data exposure to image reconstruction attacks. We show that extensive exposure can persist without rote memorization and is instead caused by a tunable connection to adversarial robustness. We begin by presenting three surprising results: (1) recent defenses that inhibit reconstruction by Model Inversion Attacks (MIAs), which evaluate leakage under an idealized attacker, do not reduce standard measures of information dependency (HSIC); (2) models that maximally memorize their training datasets remain robust to MIA reconstruction; and (3) models trained without seeing 97% of the training pixels, where recent information-theoretic bounds give arbitrarily strong privacy guarantees under standard assumptions, can still be devastatingly reconstructed by MIA.
  To explain these findings, we provide causal evidence that privacy under MIA arises from what the adversarial examples literature calls ``non-robust'' features (generalizable but imperceptible and unstable features). We further show that recent MIA defenses obtain their privacy improvements by unintentionally shifting models toward such features. To establish this causal relationship, we introduce Anti Adversarial Training (AT-AT), a training regime that intentionally learns non-robust features to obtain both superior reconstruction defense and higher accuracy than state-of-the-art defenses. Our results revise the prevailing understanding of training data exposure and reveal a new privacy-robustness tradeoff.

</details>


### [145] [Same Loss, Same Noise, Opposite Schedules: Noise Structure and Optimizer Normalization Jointly Determine Whether Learning-Rate Cooldown Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12360)
*Subham Singh,Ashutosh Mishra,Subha Raut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a provable explanation for when learning rate cooldown (decay) in the WSD schedule helps or not, identifying gradient noise structure and optimizer normalization as key factors. Under multiplicative noise, SGD self-anneals and doesn't need cooldown, while normalized methods like signSGD require cooldown due to a noise floor of order η². The authors derive exact stationary laws, prove dissociation under smoothness, and validate on real tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cooldown phase of WSD learning-rate schedules is widely used in large-model pretraining but its benefits are inconsistent—it lowers final loss in some settings and does nothing in others. The paper aims to provide a theoretical account of when cooldown helps and when it doesn't, based on gradient noise structure and optimizer properties.

Method: The authors analyze strongly convex objectives under multiplicative (gradient-proportional) noise, comparing SGD (which contracts geometrically at constant LR) with sign-based/normalized methods (adaptive optimizer surrogates). They solve the signSGD stationary law on quadratics exactly, prove local dissociation under (L₀,L₁)-smoothness, extend the noise floor to normalized SGD in d>1 via scale-invariance, and establish robustness to momentum and heavy-tailed noise. Simulations and real classification experiments with measured gradient noise validate the theory.

Result: Under multiplicative noise, SGD self-anneals (gradient shrinks proportionally, no cooldown needed). Normalized methods (signSGD, normalized SGD) settle on a noise floor of order η² and reach the minimizer only as LR→0. Additive noise reinstates a floor for all methods. The mechanism explains whether cooldown helps based on noise regime.

Conclusion: The mechanism explains whether cooldown helps: it depends on gradient noise structure and optimizer normalization. SGD doesn't need cooldown under multiplicative noise due to self-annealing; normalized methods require cooldown to overcome the noise floor. The interior cooldown fraction used at scale lies outside stationary landscape-and-noise geometry.

Abstract: The cooldown phase of a warmup-stable-decay (WSD) learning-rate schedule, now a default in large-model pretraining, lowers the final training loss in some settings and does nothing in others. We give a provable account of which case obtains, and it turns on two properties together: the structure of the gradient noise and whether the optimizer normalizes its update. On a strongly convex objective with multiplicative (gradient-proportional) noise, stochastic gradient descent contracts geometrically at a constant learning rate, so cooldown has nothing to improve. Under the same objective and noise, sign-based and normalized methods, the standard surrogates for adaptive optimizers, settle on a noise floor of order $η^2$ and reach the minimizer only as the learning rate is driven to zero; any additive noise then reinstates a floor for every method. The mechanism is elementary: an SGD step shrinks in proportion to the gradient and so anneals itself, whereas a normalized step keeps unit scale and cannot. We solve the signSGD stationary law on the quadratic exactly and obtain the floor constant in closed form, prove a local form of the dissociation under $(L_0,L_1)$-smoothness, extend the floor to normalized SGD in dimension d>1 by a scale-invariance argument, and establish robustness to momentum and heavy-tailed noise. Simulation confirms every prediction, and we demonstrate the resulting noise-regime diagnostic on a real classification task with directly measured gradient noise. The mechanism explains whether cooldown helps; the interior cooldown fraction used at scale lies outside stationary landscape-and-noise geometry.

</details>


### [146] [SinAE: A Single-Architecture Flow-Matching Autoencoder for Cross-Domain Atomic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12380)
*Yuxuan Ren,Fan Yang,Jianhua Yao,Yatao Bian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SinAE is a single-architecture flow-matching autoencoder using vanilla Transformers (no equivariant/graph operators) that achieves near-lossless reconstruction for molecules, crystals, and proteins in 3D, enabling cross-domain transfer and strong generation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D generative models for small molecules, crystals, and proteins are fragmented across domain-specific architectures (graph, equivariant, frame-based). Cross-domain training could alleviate per-domain data scarcity, but existing methods cannot handle heterogeneous structural priors simultaneously, and no prior latent autoencoder is both lossless and architecturally general across all three domains.

Method: SinAE uses a vanilla Transformer encoder-decoder with iterative flow-matching decoding to shift reconstruction burden away from the encoder, achieving near-lossless reconstruction. A Diffusion Transformer prior operates on the same per-token latent space. No equivariant, graph, or domain-specific operators are used.

Result: SinAE reduces reconstruction errors by orders of magnitude compared to prior latent baselines. The Diffusion Transformer prior achieves strong performance on molecular, crystal, and protein generation benchmarks. Joint molecule-crystal training strictly improves both domains, demonstrating cross-domain transfer.

Conclusion: A single, simple architecture without domain-specific inductive biases can unify generation across molecules, crystals, and proteins, with cross-domain training providing measurable benefits.

Abstract: Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its own graph, equivariant, or frame-based architecture. Cross-domain training would mitigate per-domain data scarcity, but direct generation in 3D coordinate space cannot easily handle the heterogeneous structural priors of all three domains, and no prior latent autoencoder is simultaneously lossless and architecturally general across all three. We introduce SinAE, a single-architecture flow-matching autoencoder for molecules, crystals, and proteins, with vanilla Transformer encoder and decoder and no equivariant, graph, or domain-specific operators. Rather than requiring the encoder to capture fine-grained geometry, SinAE shifts the reconstruction burden into an iterative flow-matching decoder, achieving near-lossless reconstruction across domains and reducing reconstruction errors by orders of magnitude relative to prior latent baselines. The same per-token latent supports a standard Diffusion Transformer prior that reaches strong performance on molecular, crystal, and protein generation benchmarks. Joint molecule--crystal training strictly improves both domains, providing direct evidence of cross-domain transfer through a shared atomic latent. Code is available at https://github.com/BlueWhaleLab/SinAE .

</details>


### [147] [Differentiable Clone-Structured Causal Graphs for End-to-End Cognitive Map Learning from Image Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12382)
*Arash Nikzad,Sasan Sarbishegi,Ali Dasmeh,Muhammad Asif,Parsa Gharavi,Erik Husom,Sagar Sen,Andrew B. Lehr,Olivier Penacchio,Ana Clemente,Tristan M. Stöber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents gradCSCG, a differentiable version of the Clone-Structured Causal Graph (CSCG) algorithm that enables end-to-end learning of structured world maps directly from raw image sequences by coupling it with a VQ-VAE perceptual front-end and loss-balancing mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CSCG can learn interpretable maps from aliased observations but requires a predefined discrete alphabet and cannot process raw image sequences end-to-end because its expectation-maximization formulation is incompatible with neural network modules.

Method: The authors reformulate CSCG as a fully differentiable module (gradCSCG) with a soft emission forward pass, couple it with a VQ-VAE perceptual front-end, and introduce loss-balancing mechanisms to prevent module collapse during joint training.

Result: gradCSCG reproduces CSCG's results on symbolic grid worlds, recovers room topology from heavily aliased observations on both grid worlds and MNIST image sequences, and achieves high edge precision and recall across four heavily aliased environments directly from visual input.

Conclusion: gradCSCG provides a proof of principle that CSCG can serve as a composable building block in deep learning architectures for end-to-end structured map learning from raw sensory input.

Abstract: How can an agent build a structured map of its world from nothing but an ongoing sequence of raw sensory input and its own movements, especially when natural variation means exact sensory patterns rarely repeat? The Clone-Structured Causal Graph algorithm (CSCG), a normative hippocampus model, shows how an interpretable map can be learned from aliased observations. However, CSCG requires a predefined discrete alphabet, and its expectation-maximization formulation is not easily combined with existing neural network modules, preventing the end-to-end processing of raw image sequences. We remove this barrier by reformulating CSCG as a single, fully differentiable module, gradCSCG, and coupling it to a learned vector-quantized variational autoencoder (VQ-VAE) perceptual front-end. A soft emission forward pass allows the map-learning objective to flow back into perception, while a set of loss-balancing mechanisms mitigates module collapse during joint training. We demonstrate, first, that gradient training reproduces CSCG's results on original symbolic grid worlds by recovering room topology from heavily aliased observations. Second, we show that map recovery remains robust on MNIST image sequences, where each visit to a location yields a newly sampled image of its assigned digit. Across four heavily aliased environments, the end-to-end pipeline successfully uncovers the underlying adjacency graph with high edge precision and recall, directly from visual input. This work provides a proof of principle that CSCG can serve as a composable building block in a deep learning architecture.

</details>


### [148] [ReDiTT: Retrieval Augmented Conditional Diffusion Transformers for Asynchronous Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12391)
*Saiyue Lyu,Zhitian Zhang,Ruizhi Deng,Thibaut Durand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReDiTT is a retrieval-augmented conditional diffusion transformer for asynchronous time series prediction that retrieves structurally similar latent sequences from a memory bank as reference conditions via cross-attention, achieving state-of-the-art next event prediction and long-horizon forecasting on seven real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the inherent uncertainty of future events in asynchronous time series prediction and stabilize long-horizon forecasting while improving sample diversity.

Method: ReDiTT uses a diffusion-based model operating in latent space with retrieval augmentation: it retrieves structurally similar latent sequences from a memory bank during training and inference, incorporating them as reference conditions through cross-attention to provide global structural guidance for generation.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on next event prediction and long-horizon forecasting across seven real-world datasets.

Conclusion: Retrieval-augmented conditioning in latent diffusion transformers stabilizes long-horizon forecasting and improves sample diversity for asynchronous time series prediction.

Abstract: We present a diffusion based model for asynchronous time series prediction, where the goal is to predict the next inter event time and event type. To address the inherent uncertainty of future events, we introduce ReDiTT, a retrieval augmented conditional diffusion transformer that operates in latent space. ReDiTT retrieves structurally similar latent sequences from a memory bank during both training and inference and incorporates them as reference conditions through cross attention. This retrieval based conditioning allows the model to attend to relevant temporal dynamics and provides global structural guidance for generation. As a result, ReDiTT stabilizes long horizon forecasting and improves sample diversity. Experiments on seven real world datasets demonstrate state of the art performance on next event prediction and long horizon forecasting. Our code is available at https://github.com/BorealisAI/ReDiTT.

</details>


### [149] [Mechanical Analysis of Parachute Suspension Line Deployment with Binding Tapes Using PINN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12409)
*Xiang Zhao,Ronghui Quan,Yaqi Xiao,Junlin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A PINN algorithm is developed to predict dynamic tension during parachute suspension line extraction and straightening, outperforming traditional ODE integration methods in speed and accuracy, with binding tape parameter effects analyzed and validated against flight test data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing numerical integration of ordinary differential equations for calculating suspension line tension fails to rapidly acquire tension values at arbitrary positions along the lines, motivating the need for a more efficient and accurate method.

Method: A physics-informed neural network (PINN) algorithm is developed for tension prediction during line extraction and straightening, incorporating physical laws into the neural network training process.

Result: The PINN framework outperforms traditional integration methods in both computational efficiency and numerical accuracy. The regulatory law of binding tape parameters on line dynamic tension is also investigated.

Conclusion: Comparative validations against flight test data and conventional numerical results verify the reliability and effectiveness of the proposed PINN framework for predicting suspension line tension during the initial deployment stage.

Abstract: Parachutes are widely utilized in aviation, aerospace and lifesaving missions. As the initial stage of parachute deployment, suspension line extraction and straightening directly determines the smooth implementation of subsequent inflation procedures. This ultra-short process involves intricate dynamic load variations. Most existing studies adopt numerical integration of ordinary differential equations to calculate line tension, yet this method fails to rapidly acquire tension values at arbitrary positions along suspension lines. This paper develops a physics-informed neural network (PINN) algorithm for tension prediction during line extraction and straightening, which outperforms traditional integration methods in both computational efficiency and numerical accuracy. Furthermore, the regulatory law of binding tape parameters on line dynamic tension is investigated. Comparative validations against flight test data and conventional numerical results verify the reliability and effectiveness of the proposed PINN framework.

</details>
