### [150] [Fisher Rank Inflation: A Spectral Signature of Memorization under Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12438)
*Satwik Bathula,Anand A. Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Fisher Rank Inflation" — a spectral signature in per-example last-layer gradients that transiently expands during memorization of corrupted labels and contracts after fitting. This phenomenon enables identification of corrupted examples, correlates with corruption severity, and precedes observable test degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the spectral signature left by label noise in the gradient space during the transition from learning clean structure to memorizing corrupted labels, and to leverage this for detecting corrupted examples.

Method: Analyzes the centered scatter of per-example last-layer gradients; introduces Fisher effective rank as a spectral metric; derives a first-order leave-one-out attribution formula; validates on CIFAR-10/100/10N with SmallCNN, ResNet18, and Vision Transformers under varying synthetic corruption levels.

Result: Fisher effective rank shows a consistent inflation–collapse trajectory aligned with memorization. Corrupted examples are enriched among top rank-contributing samples (top-100 noisy fractions 69.2%-96.2% synthetic; 94.4% on CIFAR-10N). Peak effective rank increases monotonically with corruption severity (28.88 clean to 97.09 at 60% corruption). Rank inflation onset can precede test degradation.

Conclusion: Fisher Rank Inflation is a robust spectral signature that connects corrupted-example enrichment, corruption severity, and the transition from structure learning to memorization, offering a diagnostic tool for detecting label noise without requiring clean holdout sets.

Abstract: Deep networks trained with label noise often learn clean structure before memorizing corrupted labels. We show that this transition leaves a spectral signature in the centered scatter of per-example last-layer gradients. Its effective rank transiently expands during memorization and contracts after corrupted labels are fit. We call this phenomenon Fisher Rank Inflation. Corrupted labels increase effective rank by injecting spectral mass into low-energy or previously unused eigendirections, increasing the entropy of the gradient spectrum. We derive a first-order leave-one-out attribution formula, identify conditions under which corrupted examples contribute more strongly than clean examples, and explain why attribution signals weaken once the normalized Fisher-gradient spectrum stabilizes. We test these predictions on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and CIFAR-10N using SmallCNN, ResNet18, and Vision Transformers. Across settings, Fisher effective rank exhibits a consistent inflation--collapse trajectory aligned with memorization. At peak-rank checkpoints, corrupted examples are enriched among the highest rank-contributing samples, with top-100 noisy fractions from \(69.2\%\) to \(96.2\%\) across five-seed synthetic-corruption experiments and \(94.4\%\pm1.9\%\) on CIFAR-10N. First-order spectral attribution closely matches exact leave-one-out contributions in convolutional models and remains enriched in the Vision Transformer. Peak effective rank increases monotonically with corruption severity, from \(28.88\pm1.95\) under clean training to \(97.09\pm1.78\) at \(60\%\) corruption. In several settings, the retrospectively identified onset of rank inflation precedes observable test degradation. These results establish Fisher Rank Inflation as a spectral signature connecting corrupted-example enrichment, corruption severity, and the transition from structure learning to memorization.

</details>


### [151] [The Computational Basis of Confidence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12447)
*Dharshan Kumaran,Viorica Patraucean,Maks Ovsanikov,Petar Veličković,Nathaniel Daw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether answer logits in multimodal language models reflect a latent decision variable for normative confidence or a heuristic preference signal, using statistical decision confidence (SDC) from neuroscience. Across perceptual and memory tasks, logit differences satisfy SDC signatures (e.g., folded-X pattern), indicating they behave as monotonic decision variable readouts. In complex visual reasoning, SDC signatures break down, marking the framework's boundary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work evaluates confidence by its calibration to correctness but overlooks what the confidence signal fundamentally represents. The authors aim to determine whether answer logits encode a normative latent decision variable (sufficient for Bayesian confidence) or merely a heuristic preference signal.

Method: The authors adopt statistical decision confidence (SDC), a normative framework from computational neuroscience, and treat the answer-logit difference (LD) as a candidate readout of the latent decision variable. They test qualitative SDC signatures (including the correct/error folded-X pattern) across three perceptual discrimination tasks, one memory-based decision task, and one complex visual reasoning task, using three multimodal non-reasoning models and one reasoning model.

Result: Across perceptual and memory tasks, LD satisfied all SDC signatures, including the diagnostic folded-X pattern. In complex visual reasoning, LD still predicted correctness beyond objective task difficulty, but the full geometric signatures of SDC were absent, indicating a boundary when explicit normative process models are unavailable.

Conclusion: Answer logits in multimodal language models behave as monotonic readouts of a latent decision variable (rather than heuristic preference scores) in simpler tasks, but SDC's applicability breaks down in complex reasoning settings. SDC is established as a unifying framework for studying confidence across biological and artificial intelligence.

Abstract: Reliable confidence -- the probability that a model's own answer is correct -- is essential for the trustworthy deployment of language models. Existing work has largely evaluated confidence by how well it predicts correctness and whether it is calibrated, leaving open a more fundamental question: what does the confidence signal itself represent? Answer logits may reflect a latent decision variable sufficient to compute normative confidence, or instead a heuristic preference signal that combines the available evidence in a non-Bayesian manner. We address this using statistical decision confidence (SDC), a normative framework from computational neuroscience. Treating the answer-logit difference (LD) as a candidate readout of the latent decision variable, we test the qualitative signatures predicted by SDC. Across three perceptual discrimination tasks and a memory-based decision task, spanning three multimodal non-reasoning models and one reasoning model, LD satisfied these signatures -- including the diagnostic correct/error folded-X pattern -- showing that, in these settings, answer logits behave as monotonic readouts of a latent decision variable rather than heuristic preference scores. In complex visual reasoning, LD continued to predict correctness beyond objective task difficulty, but the full geometric signatures of SDC were absent, illustrating the current boundary of the framework when explicit normative process models are unavailable. These results provide a computational account of confidence in multimodal language models, delineate when answer logits behave as readouts of a latent decision variable, and establish SDC as a unifying framework for studying confidence across biological and artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [152] [Exploring Zero-Shot Foundation Models for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12454)
*Martin Uray,Saverio Messineo,Roland Kwitt,Stefan Huber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Investigates zero-shot application of univariate forecasting foundation model (TimesFM) to multivariate time series anomaly detection (SWaT benchmark). Neither naive strategy (per-feature forecasting with thresholded errors, nor using FM as embedder with outlier detectors) matches baselines. FM's strong temporal capture makes persistent anomalies indistinguishable. However, error peaks at anomaly boundaries show potential for change-point detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection (MTSAD) is critical for reliability in industrial and financial domains. Traditional approaches require application-specific models that are costly and hard to scale. Foundation Models (FMs) pre-trained on broad data with zero-shot generalization have emerged for univariate forecasting, raising the question of whether they can address MTSAD without task-specific training.

Method: Applied TimesFM (a univariate forecasting FM) to the Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) MTSAD benchmark via two zero-shot strategies: (1) per-feature forecaster with thresholded prediction errors, and (2) embedder whose intermediate representations feed standard outlier detectors.

Result: Neither strategy is competitive with established baselines. Embeddings show only partial separation between normal and anomalous segments. The FM yields low error even within fully anomalous windows because it is too effective at capturing temporal dynamics, making persistent anomalies indistinguishable from normal behavior. However, error peaks at anomaly boundaries.

Conclusion: Naive zero-shot FMs are unsuitable for MTSAD but promising for change-point detection, as error peaks at anomaly boundaries indicate reliable detection of distribution changes.

Abstract: Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection (MTSAD) is essential for reliability and safety in domains such as industrial process monitoring and financial risk management, yet conventional approaches rely on application-specific models that are costly to train and hard to scale. Foundation Models (FMs), pre-trained on broad data with strong zero-shot generalization, have recently become available for univariate time series forecasting, raising the question of whether they can address MTSAD without task-specific training. We investigate the zero-shot application of a univariate forecasting FM, TimesFM, to industrial MTSAD on the Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) benchmark, evaluating two strategies: treating the FM as a per-feature forecaster with thresholded prediction errors, and as an embedder whose intermediate representations feed standard outlier detectors. Neither of our proposed setups is competitive with established baselines; embeddings reveal only partial separation between normal and anomalous segments, insufficient for reliable detection. The cause is that the FM is too effective at capturing temporal dynamics, yielding low error even within fully anomalous windows, so persistent anomalies become indistinguishable from normal behavior. However, these observations yield valuable insights: the error peaks at anomaly boundaries, indicating FMs reliably detect distribution changes. We conclude that the proposed naive zero-shot FMs are unsuitable for MTSAD but promising for change-point detection.

</details>


### [153] [Sample Efficient Generative Optimization for Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12488)
*Sarina Kopf,Cristina Nevado,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEGO is a sample-efficient framework combining Bayesian optimization with generative models that achieves state-of-the-art molecular optimization using only 10% of oracle calls required by other methods, advancing toward experiment-driven discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular optimization requires searching vast chemical spaces under tight evaluation budgets because high-fidelity oracles and experimental measurements are costly, making sample efficiency critical for practical impact.

Method: SEGO uses a probabilistic surrogate model to hypothesize where hits lie, steers a generative model to propose candidates in that region, selects the best candidate via an acquisition function, and uses oracle feedback to refine both the surrogate and generator.

Result: SEGO achieves state-of-the-art performance on the PMO benchmark using only one-tenth of the oracle calls of other methods, and on a multiparameter docking task it reaches ten hits in roughly half the oracle calls of existing approaches.

Conclusion: SEGO's gains move molecular optimization closer to campaigns driven by direct experimental feedback, demonstrating that sample efficiency can significantly reduce reliance on costly evaluations.

Abstract: Molecular optimization in drug discovery, materials design, and catalysis requires searching vast chemical spaces under tight evaluation budgets, since high-fidelity oracles and experimental measurements are costly. The practical impact of an optimization method therefore hinges on its sample efficiency: how few evaluations it needs to find strong candidates. We introduce Sample Efficient Generative Optimization (SEGO), a framework for Bayesian optimization on adaptively generated molecules. In SEGO, a probabilistic surrogate model forms a hypothesis about where hits lie in chemical space, a generative model is steered to propose candidates in that region, the most promising candidate is selected via an acquisition function, and the resulting oracle call is used both to sharpen the surrogate and to anchor the generator in real reward. SEGO attains state-of-the-art performance on the practical molecular optimization (PMO) benchmark using only one tenth of the oracle calls consumed by other methods, and on a multiparameter docking task it reaches ten hits in roughly half the oracle calls of existing approaches. These gains move molecular optimization closer to campaigns driven by direct experimental feedback.

</details>


### [154] [Adversarial Attacks on Online Handwriting using Salience-based Temporal Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12500)
*Yataro Tamura,Brian Kenji Iwana,Jiseok Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A salience-guided temporal editing attack for online handwriting recognition that inserts/deletes points at salient time steps (rather than adding noise) preserves handwriting shape/smoothness and achieves stronger one-shot black-box transferability than conventional image-based adversarial attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for online handwriting recognition are vulnerable to adversarial attacks. Existing image-based adversarial methods add spatial perturbations that introduce high-frequency jitter and unnatural stroke artifacts when applied to online handwriting (time series of pen trajectories). A more natural threat model is needed.

Method: Proposes a salience-guided temporal editing attack that inserts and deletes points at time steps selected by gradient-based temporal salience (activation mapping), preserving handwriting shape and smoothness. Evaluated on Unipen and CASIA-OLHWDB datasets under white-box and one-shot black-box settings.

Result: Conventional image-based attacks achieve strong white-box performance but poor transferability. The proposed temporal editing attack achieves stronger one-shot black-box transferability while preserving visual structure of handwriting.

Conclusion: Temporal editing is a relevant threat model for online handwriting recognition, particularly in one-shot black-box transfer settings.

Abstract: Deep learning models for online handwriting recognition have been shown effective and are increasingly deployed in practical applications. However, their vulnerability to adversarial attacks is still a challenge. Existing adversarial methods are predominantly designed for image-based inputs and typically rely on additive spatial perturbations. When applied to online handwriting, which is inherently represented as a time series of pen trajectories, such perturbations often introduce high-frequency jitter and visibly unnatural stroke artifacts. In this work, we propose a novel adversarial attack framework for online handwriting recognition based on salience-guided temporal editing. Instead of adding noise, the proposed method generates adversarial examples by inserting and deleting points at time steps selected according to temporal salience, preserving the shape and smoothness of the original handwriting. Temporal salience is estimated using gradient-based activation mapping, which guides edits toward time steps that strongly support the original class prediction. We evaluate the proposed approach on the Unipen and CASIA-OLHWDB datasets under both white-box and one-shot black-box attack settings. Experimental results demonstrate that while conventional image-based attacks achieve strong white-box performance, they exhibit poor transferability across models. In contrast, the proposed temporal editing attack achieves stronger one-shot black-box transferability while preserving the visual structure of the handwriting. These results indicate that temporal editing is a relevant threat model for online handwriting recognition, particularly in one-shot black-box transfer settings.

</details>


### [155] [What Does Goodness Measure? A Likelihood-Ratio Account of Forward-Forward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12501)
*Paolo Giannitrapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Forward-Forward (FF) algorithm's heuristics (squared goodness, threshold, inter-layer normalization) are shown to be principled under a generative model: squared goodness is a likelihood-ratio test statistic for scale difference, generalizable to Mahalanobis and heavy-tailed forms; normalization must preserve per-coordinate energy to avoid depth collapse; and pairwise objectives suffer from scale-inflation that whitened goodness resolves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a principled generative-model interpretation for the seemingly heuristic choices in the Forward-Forward algorithm, specifically the squared goodness measure, the threshold, and inter-layer normalization.

Method: Reinterprets FF's squared activation sum as a sufficient statistic for a likelihood-ratio test between two zero-mean populations differing in scale; generalizes to anisotropic (Mahalanobis) and heavy-tailed (saturating statistic with divisive normalization) cases; analyzes inter-layer normalization requirements (preserving per-coordinate energy) and identifies a scale-inflation shortcut in pairwise objectives that whitened goodness removes.

Result: FF's squared goodness is not heuristic but a likelihood-ratio test statistic; generalizations include Mahalanobis goodness for anisotropic populations and saturating statistics for heavy-tailed ones; inter-layer normalization must preserve per-coordinate energy (explaining depth collapse under unit-norm normalization); pairwise objectives have a scale-inflation shortcut that whitening eliminates.

Conclusion: The FF algorithm's design choices have a rigorous statistical foundation under a generative model, enabling principled generalizations and revealing pitfalls (depth collapse, scale inflation) that can be addressed via whitened goodness.

Abstract: The Forward-Forward (FF) algorithm trains each layer locally, so that a scalar goodness - the sum of squared activations - is high on real inputs and low on contrastive ones, with activations normalized between layers. Both choices are usually treated as heuristics. Under an explicit generative model they are not: the squared goodness is the sufficient statistic of a likelihood-ratio test between two zero-mean populations differing in scale, and the FF threshold is its boundary. It generalizes: anisotropic populations yield a Mahalanobis goodness, the plain square being its isotropic case; heavy-tailed populations yield a saturating statistic whose slope is a posterior precision - divisive normalization - with bounded evidence and an advantage only under aggregation. The same lens characterizes the inter-layer normalization: it must remove the length while preserving per-coordinate energy, explaining a depth collapse we observe under unit-norm normalization; and the pairwise objective admits a scale-inflation shortcut that a whitened goodness removes.

</details>


### [156] [OOD-RL-Bench: A Benchmark Framework for Out-of-Distribution Detection in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12523)
*Emil Mittag,Richard Dazeley,Peter Vamplew*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OOD-RL-Bench is a framework for evaluating out-of-distribution detectors in RL trajectories, addressing the gap in existing OOD benchmarks that ignore temporal, action-dependent structures. Experiments in LunarLander-v3 with DQN show detection varies significantly by anomaly type—observation perturbations and regime switches are easier, while delay and action-conditioned dynamics remain challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current OOD detection benchmarks evaluate image classifiers or static low-dimensional datasets, failing to account for the complex, action-dependent temporal structure inherent in RL trajectories.

Method: Proposed OOD-RL-Bench framework integrates detectors and anomaly injectors through shared interfaces and configuration, enabling evaluation without modifying the core benchmark loop. Tested using DQN policy in LunarLander-v3 with matched-time AUROC, AUPRC, false-positive rate, detection delay, and segmented-onset metrics.

Result: Observation perturbations and regime switches are identified with high accuracy, while observation delay and action-conditioned dynamics remain difficult even when post-onset anomaly scores are compared against clean scores from the same timesteps.

Conclusion: The framework, trained policy checkpoint, and complete results are publicly released as a reproducible artefact to advance OOD detection in RL.

Abstract: Reliable reinforcement learning (RL) agents must maintain operational integrity amidst sensor malfunctions, dynamic disturbances, and slow environmental shifts. The detection of out-of-distribution conditions is pivotal to determining when an agent's observations, transitions, or trajectory dynamics deviate from the assumptions underpinning its policy training. Current out-of-distribution (OOD) detection benchmarks typically evaluate image classifiers or static low-dimensional datasets, failing to account for the complex, action-dependent temporal structure inherent in RL trajectories. To address this gap, we present OOD-RL-Bench, a comprehensive and extensible framework designed to evaluate OOD detectors against categories of anomalies injected into RL trajectories. Detectors and anomaly injectors are integrated through shared interfaces and configuration, which allows new scoring methods and perturbation families to be evaluated without modification of the core benchmark loop. We evaluate the utility of the framework using a Deep Q-Network policy within the LunarLander-v3 environment. We assess the performance of each detector across a suite of anomaly types using matched-time AUROC, matched-time AUPRC, matched-time false-positive rate, detection delay, and segmented-onset metrics. Our analysis reveals significant performance variance across anomaly types: observation perturbations and regime switches are identified with high accuracy by several methods, while observation delay and action-conditioned dynamics remain difficult even when post-onset anomaly scores are compared against clean scores from the same timesteps. We make the framework, trained policy checkpoint, and complete results publicly available as a reproducible artefact.

</details>


### [157] [From Preimage Search To Source-Grounded Feature Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12526)
*Kaixiang Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method for source-grounded feature inversion that conditions the inverse on the local network geometry at the target-generating input, using closed-form Wiener maps to repair adjoint signals during reverse pass, enabling feature visualization without per-query optimization across diverse architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard feature inversion methods search for any input matching a target feature representation, but this underdetermined problem does not uniquely identify the inverse tied to the specific source sample that generated the feature, limiting interpretability.

Method: Uses backpropagation's reverse dependencies but repairs the adjoint signal via a closed-form matrix Wiener map from a mean-seed VJP to the upstream state, plus a second Wiener map for JVP forward-consistency residual; composes repaired states through the DAG in one finite reverse pass with a calibrated zero-intercept map family.

Result: Works across CNN and Transformer architectures, tensor components, and visual distributions without query-specific optimization. Matched target/source controls verify dependence on selected feature and local operators rather than target-independent templates. Prediction-conditioned feature atlases align with independent interventions.

Conclusion: Source-grounded feature inversion enables inspection of the hidden feature hierarchy at the level of individual layers and channels, linking extracted features to internal evidence shaping decisions.

Abstract: Interpreting a neural network requires understanding what its internal features extract from a particular input. Feature inversion seeks to express a selected feature in the input domain, but canonical iterative methods search for an input whose re-encoded representation matches the target. Because many inputs can satisfy this constraint, target matching alone does not specify the inverse associated with the sample that generated the feature. We formulate source-grounded feature inversion by conditioning the inverse on the source-local network geometry at the target-generating input. At each boundary of the computational DAG, backpropagation provides the correct reverse dependencies but transports an adjoint signal rather than an upstream-state estimate. We locally repair this signal with a closed-form matrix Wiener map from a mean-seed VJP to the upstream state, followed by a second Wiener map for the JVP forward-consistency residual, and compose the repaired states through the same DAG in one finite reverse pass. One calibrated zero-intercept map family supports new inputs, depths, channels, and channel groups across diverse CNN and Transformer architectures, tensor components, and visual distributions without query-specific optimisation. Matched target and source controls verify that each inverse depends on the selected feature and the local operators of the sample being explained, rather than a target-independent image template. Prediction-conditioned feature atlases align these visualisations with independent interventions on the corresponding internal features. Together, source-grounded feature inversion opens the model's hidden feature hierarchy to inspection at the level of individual layers and channels, linking what the network extracts from an input to the internal evidence that shapes its decision.

</details>


### [158] [A JoLT for the KV Cache: Near-Lossless KV Cache Compression via Joint Tucker and JL-Residual Allocation for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12550)
*Rahul Krishnan,Volker Schulz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JoLT applies a partial Tucker decomposition to the KV cache tensor (heads × tokens × features), compressing only token and feature axes, and restores truncated energy via JL-rotated low-bit residual. A Lagrangian dual jointly allocates Tucker ranks and residual bit-widths under a byte budget, achieving near-lossless 2-3x compression on Mistral-7B and LLaMA-2-13B across perplexity, GSM8K, and RULER benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The KV cache is the dominant memory bottleneck in long-context transformer inference, growing with batch size, context length, and depth. Existing low-rank and quantization methods treat cache slices as 2D matrices, failing to exploit the natural 3D tensor structure (heads × tokens × features) where each axis carries different redundancy levels.

Method: JoLT first applies a partial Tucker decomposition to each layer's KV cache tensor, compressing only the token and feature dimensions while preserving head and layer axes. The truncated energy is recovered via a Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) rotated low-bit residual. A Lagrangian dual optimization jointly allocates Tucker ranks and residual bit-widths per layer group under a single byte budget, separately for keys and values.

Result: Near-lossless 2-3x compression on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and LLaMA-2-13B: perplexity, GSM8K accuracy, and RULER retrieval remain within statistical noise of uncompressed baseline. At 2x compression, relative Frobenius error is 0.009 (K) and 0.006 (V), roughly an order of magnitude better than cross-layer SVD and 4-bit quantization. FlashJoLT (randomized SVD variant) achieves 5-13x compression-time speedup at matched quality.

Conclusion: Explicitly treating the KV cache as a third-order tensor and compressing only the axes with high redundancy (tokens, features) while using JL-residual recovery yields substantially better compression-quality trade-offs than prior 2D low-rank or quantization methods, establishing a new state-of-the-art for KV cache compression.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache has become the dominant memory cost of transformer inference. It grows with batch size, context length, and depth, and at long context it, rather than the model weights, sets the ceiling on throughput. Two families of methods reduce it. Low-rank methods factor two-dimensional slices of the cache, either per-head matrices or cross-layer feature blocks, and quantization methods lower the bit-width of every entry. Neither family exploits the fact that the cache at a layer is naturally a third-order tensor whose three axes, the heads, the tokens, and the features, carry very different amounts of redundancy. We take this tensor view directly. Our method, JoLT, applies a partial Tucker decomposition that compresses only the token and feature axes while leaving the head and layer axes intact, and then restores the energy that truncation discards with a Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) rotated low-bit residual. A single Lagrangian dual allocates the Tucker ranks and the residual bit-widths together, per layer group and separately for keys and values, under one byte budget. The result is a near-lossless 2-3x compression: perplexity, GSM8K accuracy, and RULER needle-in-a-haystack retrieval all stay at or within statistical noise of the uncompressed baseline on both a grouped-query-attention model (Mistral-7B-v0.3) and a multi-head-attention model (LLaMA-2-13B). At 2x, JoLT reconstructs the cache to relative Frobenius error 0.009 (K) and 0.006 (V) on both architectures, roughly an order of magnitude below cross-layer SVD and 4-bit quantization. A randomized-SVD variant, FlashJoLT, delivers a 5-13x compression-time speedup at matched quality.

</details>


### [159] [Lightweight Multi-Scale Anomaly Detection for Resource-Constrained Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12599)
*Raheen Junaid Wani,Smruti R. Sarangi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LMSAE is a lightweight multi-scale autoencoder (<500 KB) using Discrete Wavelet Transform for time-series anomaly detection, achieving competitive accuracy with 9x lower latency and 2x lower power on edge devices like Jetson Nano.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep-learning anomaly detection methods are computationally expensive and lack multi-scale sensitivity, making them unsuitable for resource-constrained edge/IoT environments.

Method: Proposes LMSAE, a lightweight autoencoder that uses Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) for multi-scale feature extraction and a multi-scale loss function to enhance sensitivity to subtle anomalies.

Result: Competitive/superior detection performance with <500 KB model size; 9x reduction in inference latency and 2x reduction in power consumption on NVIDIA Jetson Nano compared to baselines.

Conclusion: LMSAE demonstrates that compact, efficient models can achieve strong anomaly detection performance, making them well-suited for edge deployment in IoT and sensor networks.

Abstract: Time-series anomaly detection is increasingly important in IoT systems, sensor networks, and edge monitoring applications, where models must operate under strict constraints on memory, latency, and power consumption. While recent deep-learning approaches have improved detection accuracy, many remain computationally expensive and often fail to capture subtle anomalies due to limited multi-scale sensitivity. Autoencoders are widely used for anomaly detection because they reconstruct normal patterns well, leading to elevated reconstruction errors for anomalous inputs. Their simplicity and efficiency also make them suitable lightweight backbones for handling multi-scale inputs. To address these challenges, we propose a Lightweight MultiScale AutoEncoder (LMSAE) network for univariate time-series anomaly detection, designed to be compact and computationally efficient. LMSAE leverages the Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) to extract multi-scale features and employs a multi-scale loss function to improve sensitivity to subtle or hidden anomalies. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive or superior detection performance despite using significantly fewer parameters and a model size of less than 500 KB. LMSAE also achieves low-latency, low-power inference on the NVIDIA Jetson Nano, with 9x reduction in inference latency and 2x reduction in power consumption, making it ideal for edge deployment.

</details>


### [160] [The Geometry of Memorization: Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity as a Diagnostic for Flow Matching Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12616)
*Shuchan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity (FTSS) g(t), a gradient-free metric that analyzes flow geometry in continuous-time generative frameworks by tracking singular values of the state-transition matrix. It reveals that overfitting causes spectral collapse (reduced effective dimensions), and uses this to detect memorization purely from internal trajectory dynamics without external queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current continuous-time generative frameworks develop complex path deformations that are poorly understood. The authors aim to expose flow geometry and detect memorization without relying on external membership queries or baseline data comparisons.

Method: The paper proposes FTSS g(t), a gradient-free forward-pass metric that computes the root-mean-square singular value of the state-transition matrix as a continuous proxy for stable rank. It analyzes spectral dynamics under varying data regimes to identify geometric pathologies.

Result: Under data scarcity, overfitting models exhibit spectral collapse (decreasing effective dimensions), while generalizing models maintain stable effective dimensions. This spectral difference serves as a detectable geometric pathology.

Conclusion: FTSS enables internal geometric auditing for generative memorization detection, eliminating the need for external membership queries or baseline comparisons.

Abstract: Continuous-time generative frameworks construct probability paths between base and target domains by optimizing time-dependent velocity fields. While theoretical targets favor straight trajectories, empirical networks develop complex path deformations. This paper presents the Finite-Time Spectral Sensitivity (FTSS) g(t), a gradient-free, forward-pass metric that exposes flow geometry by tracking the root-mean-square singular value of the state-transition matrix. Serving as a continuous proxy for stable rank, g(t) reveals a distinct geometric pathology under data scarcity: while generalizing models maintain stable effective dimensions, overfitting causes a spectral collapse. We leverage this structural phenomenon to develop an internal geometric audit based on g(t). Our framework detects generative memorization using purely internal trajectory dynamics, removing the need for external membership queries or baseline data comparison.

</details>


### [161] [Learning Forced Multibody Dynamics on Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12627)
*Martine Dyring Hansen,Marta Ghirardelli,Elena Celledoni,David Martin de Diego,Brynjulf Owren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a geometric architecture for learning mechanical system dynamics from position data using discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations on Lie groups, preserving invariants and conservation laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To learn dynamics of mechanical systems directly on manifold-valued configuration spaces while respecting geometric structure, using only position data (since velocity data may be unavailable or noisy).

Method: Uses discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations formulated on Lie groups to model dynamics, operating solely on position measurements while preserving geometric invariants and conservation laws.

Result: Extends to multibody systems, accommodates external control inputs, and shows strong performance on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

Conclusion: The geometric approach successfully learns mechanical system dynamics from position-only data while respecting underlying geometric structure, making it applicable to realistic settings with noisy or missing velocity measurements.

Abstract: We propose an architecture for learning the dynamics of mechanical systems based on discrete forced Euler-Lagrange equations on Lie groups using only position data. By formulating the dynamics directly on manifold-valued configuration spaces, the method naturally respects the geometric structure of the systems and preserves geometric invariants and conservation laws. The reliance on position measurements alone makes the framework applicable in settings where velocity data are unavailable or noisy. The approach extends naturally to multibody systems, accommodates external control inputs, and demonstrates strong performance on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

</details>


### [162] [AdaPCLA: Adaptive Prior-Calibrated Logit Adjustment for Long-Tailed Longitudinal EHR Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12645)
*Shuai Cui,Chen Wenxuan,Wenjie Du,Jian Lou,Dan Li,Wenjie Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaPCLA is a framework for longitudinal EHR generation that improves tail-event co-occurrence through simulated annealing training and zero-shot distribution control, achieving significant gains in tail plausibility and cross-population adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard autoregressive models for EHR generation underrepresent co-occurrence structures of rare events (diseases, symptoms), reducing fidelity for rare subpopulations.

Method: AdaPCLA uses a data distribution-aware training strategy with simulated annealing to internalize data knowledge parameters, plus training-free zero-shot distribution control for diverse clinical populations. Theoretical analysis uses label-wise empirical NTK to characterize rare-code logit updates and derive a prior-internalization bound.

Result: Improves TailPairSeen over HALO by 114.2% on MIMIC-III and 65.1% on MIMIC-IV; outperforms GPT-style generation by 3.5% F1 for zero-shot cross-population adaptation.

Conclusion: AdaPCLA consistently improves tail plausibility, downstream utility, and zero-shot control for longitudinal EHR generation.

Abstract: Generative modeling of longitudinal Electronic Health Records is increasingly important for privacy-preserving research, yet standard autoregressive models tend to underrepresent the co-occurrence structure of tail events (i.e., diseases, symptoms), reducing the fidelity and faithfulness of generated data for rare subpopulations. To this end, we propose AdaPCLA framework, which enables generative models to adaptively fit and generate EHR data through a data distribution-aware training strategy; this is achieved by internalizing data knowledge parameters by simulated annealing training. It also supports training-free adaptation to a diverse clinical population for generation through zero-shot distribution control. Moreover, our theoretical analysis characterizes rare-code logit updates through the label-wise empirical NTK and derives a prior-internalization bound for how annealing speed and NTK conditioning affect retained prior signals. Experiments on real-world data show that AdaPCLA achieves consistent gains in tail plausibility, downstream utility, and zero-shot control; in particular, it improves TailPairSeen over HALO by 114.2% on MIMIC-III and 65.1% on MIMIC-IV, outperforms GPT-style generation by 3.5% F1 for zero-shot cross-population adaptation.

</details>


### [163] [Extractable Memorization From First Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12649)
*A. Feder Cooper,Marika Swanberg,Jamie Hayes,Lea Duesterwald,Christopher De Sa,Daniel E. Ho,Mark A. Lemley,Percy Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses validity issues in extractable memorization claims for LLMs by proposing matched comparisons—comparing generation probabilities of training vs. comparable non-training sequences—to establish rigorous baselines for memorization. The authors formalize two approaches (a conformal test and a census method) and show that prior work overstates or understates memorization due to lack of such calibration. They demonstrate that many claimed memorization instances are actually false positives from predictability, and propose refined criteria for "extractable memorization."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on extractable memorization suffers from contradictory validity problems: some studies overstate memorization by using sequences too short to distinguish it from predictability, while others undermine extraction as evidence because models can reproduce real-world text not explicitly trained on. Both overlook the need to determine what generation probability is high enough to indicate memorization.

Method: The authors propose matched comparisons: measuring generation probabilities of training sequences and comparable non-training sequences, where non-training probabilities serve as a predictability baseline. They formalize this as (1) a conformal test that calibrates a threshold to a chosen false positive rate (FPR) under population sampling, and (2) a census that calibrates against a matched non-training document for single-document cases (e.g., books).

Result: On Wikipedia, OLMo 2 32B reproduces non-training 10-token suffixes roughly 24% as often as training ones, indicating that share of training generation rate reflects false positives, not memorization. For Llama 3.1 70B on books, calibrated thresholds are as low as 1e-27, supporting memorization claims for sequences infeasible to extract via sampling.

Conclusion: Matched comparisons enable rigorous, calibrated memorization claims and reveal validity issues in prior setups. The authors refine "extractable memorization" to require a valid memorization claim and near-certain generation within a realistic budget.

Abstract: Recent work on extractable memorization in LLMs suffers from two contrasting validity problems. Some studies overstate extraction, e.g., relying on sequences too short to distinguish memorization from predictability. Others imply that extraction is unreliable evidence of memorization, since models can also reproduce real-world text they weren't explicitly trained on. In different ways, both overlook what makes a valid extraction claim: the model must generate a training sequence with high enough probability to indicate memorization. To determine what's high enough, one has to perform a matched comparison: measuring the generation probabilities of both the training sequences of interest and comparable non-training sequences. Because non-training sequences cannot have been memorized, their probabilities provide a baseline for predictability; a training sequence exceeding this baseline provides evidence of memorization. We formalize matched comparisons in two ways: (1) a conformal test that calibrates a threshold to a chosen FPR when training and non-training sequences are sampled from populations, and (2) a census that calibrates against a matched non-training document when the object is a single document (e.g., a book). We show that matched comparisons enable rigorous, calibrated memorization claims, and reveal where prior setups have validity issues. For instance, on Wikipedia OLMo 2 32B reproduces non-training 10-token suffixes roughly 24% as often as training ones: that share of the training generation rate reflects false positives, not memorization. For Llama 3.1 70B on books, the thresholds we calibrate are as low as 1e-27, supporting memorization claims for sequences that no feasible sampling budget would extract. Based on these results, we refine "extractable memorization" to require a valid memorization claim and near-certain generation within a realistic budget.

</details>


### [164] [Learning-based Probabilistic Load Forecasting with Post-hoc and In-model Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12730)
*Sarah Al-Shareeda,Gulcihan Ozdemir,Heung Seok Jeon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares modular post-hoc residual-quantile vs. integrated in-model quantile-learning schemes for probabilistic building load forecasting under feature reconstruction, finding that integrated quantile learning with Temporal Fusion Transformer yields the best performance (2.2-3.6% MAPE, 28-83W RMSE, 5x narrower intervals), while reconstructed inputs increase Quantile Score by 106% with little width change, revealing limits of post-hoc methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart-building load forecasters are trained on dense multivariate data but deployed with limited inputs, requiring feature reconstruction that introduces uncertainty. If this reconstruction uncertainty is unaccounted for, prediction intervals become miscalibrated, harming demand-response scheduling.

Method: Develops a unified one-day-ahead probabilistic forecasting framework aligning temporal resolution, reconstructing missing inputs, and deriving causal features. Compares modular post-hoc residual-quantile vs. integrated in-model quantile-learning across three DL backbones (recurrent, hybrid recurrent, TFT) under identical conditions.

Result: Uncertainty placement is backbone-dependent. TFT with integrated quantile learning achieves 2.2-3.6% MAPE, 28-83W RMSE, and ~5x narrower intervals than modular at closest-to-nominal coverage. Reconstructed inputs increase QS by 106% with nearly unchanged interval width. Diebold-Mariano tests and robustness checks confirm ranking.

Conclusion: Integrated quantile learning is superior for probabilistic forecasting under input reconstruction, but models do not automatically absorb reconstruction-induced uncertainty. Post-hoc residual quantiles have inherent limits when inference depends on reconstructed inputs.

Abstract: Smart-building load forecasters are often trained offline on dense, multivariate, high-frequency data, but deployment may provide only hourly, feature-limited inputs. Missing features must then be reconstructed, and their errors can propagate through the model. If this input uncertainty is not reflected, prediction intervals may become miscalibrated, affecting demand-response scheduling. Our work examines where uncertainty should be placed once inference inputs are reconstructed. We develop a unified one-day-ahead probabilistic forecasting framework that aligns temporal resolution, reconstructs the unavailable inputs, and derives causal features, and we compare a modular post-hoc residual-quantile scheme with an integrated in-model quantile-learning scheme. The comparison uses three mid-scale Deep Learning (DL) backbones: recurrent, hybrid recurrent, and attention-based Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) models, under identical inputs, forecasting horizon, preprocessing rules, and training budgets. Results show that uncertainty placement is backbone-dependent. Integrated quantile learning is most reliable with the TFT, yielding 2.2-3.6% MAPE and 28-83W RMSE on the labeled test window, while producing intervals about 5x narrower than the modular intervals at the closest-to-nominal coverage level. Diebold-Mariano tests support the TFT ranking and the mixed behavior of the recurrent backbones. A reconstruction-sensitivity test shows that reconstructed inputs increase the Quantile Score (QS) by 106% while interval width remains nearly unchanged, indicating that the model does not automatically absorb reconstruction-induced uncertainty. Robustness checks against non-DL baselines and seasonal hold-out weeks support this ranking. Our results expose the limits of post-hoc residual quantiles when inference depends on reconstructed inputs.

</details>


### [165] [What Makes a Representational Prior Work? Feature Families, Label-Free Invariances, and Critical Windows in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12735)
*Gunner Levi Howe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates what makes contrastive priors effective for accelerating grokking (delayed generalization), finding that (1) feature-family alignment determines whether a prior permits generalization, (2) a fully label-free invariance prior (commuted pairs) accelerates generalization reliably, (3) the prior is only needed during an early critical window, and (4) structure injection flattens the weight-norm delay-law exponent by ~17-fold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize what makes a contrastive prior work for accelerating grokking, following a companion paper that showed grokking delay is caused by the time to form task-structured representations, which can be injected via a contrastive prior.

Method: 188 new runs across four axes: content (feature families), supervision (label-free vs label-supervised), timing (early vs continuous vs late windows), and setting (modular multiplication, depth variants, normalization variants). Uses a clamp sweep to quantify weight-norm delay-law exponent changes.

Result: (1) A coherent prior from the wrong feature family blocks generalization like a random partition (p=0.43). (2) A label-free invariance prior (commuted pairs) generalizes 15/15 runs at 2.7× speedup, outperforming label-supervised prior (p=0.038); combined with clamp yields 17× median speedup (5/5). (3) Prior applied only in first 2000 epochs (4% of budget) generalizes 10/10 at 2.7×, beating continuous application (8/10, 1.25×). (4) Structure injection flattens weight-norm delay-law exponent ~17-fold (31× vs 1.22× per +10 norm units).

Conclusion: Feature-family alignment determines whether a prior permits generalization; invariance content suffices for acceleration without labels; a brief early window captures nearly all benefit; the dissociation replicates across settings.

Abstract: Companion work showed the grokking delay is causally the time to form task-structured representations, injectable via a contrastive prior. Here we characterize what makes such a prior work, across four axes, in 188 new runs. Content: a coherent, learnable prior built from the wrong feature family (magnitude bands) blocks generalization like a random partition (1/15 vs 0/20 grok; $p=0.43$ between them), confirming the companion's prediction that priors act at the level of the circuit's features. Supervision: a fully label-free invariance prior -- positives are commuted pairs $(a,b)\sim(b,a)$ only -- generalizes in 15/15 runs at a median $2.7\times$ speedup, more reliably than the label-supervised prior itself ($p=0.038$), and combined with a weight-norm clamp yields the strongest method we test (median $17\times$, 5/5) -- strongest meaning reliably fast: plain cross-entropy with a clamp matches this speed only at the exact critical norm, while the prior keeps it fast across the entire clamp range. Timing: the prior is only needed early -- applied solely during the first 2000 epochs (4% of budget) it generalizes 10/10 at $2.7\times$, beating continuous application (8/10, $1.25\times$) and a duration-matched later window ($2.1\times$). Setting: the dissociation replicates on modular multiplication and across depths and normalization variants, and a clamp sweep quantifies the companion's central claim: structure injection flattens the weight-norm delay-law exponent about 17-fold (plain cross-entropy slows $31\times$ per +10 norm units, a lower bound as higher cells are censored, versus $1.22\times$ with the prior). Honest boundary: tasks that generalize before memorizing have no delay to control. Feature-family alignment decides whether a prior permits generalization; invariance content suffices for acceleration without labels; a brief early window captures nearly all of the benefit.

</details>


### [166] [Constraint-Aware Aggregation for Federated Reinforcement Learning in Microgrid Energy Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12763)
*Usman Haider,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes constraint-aware aggregation rules for Federated Reinforcement Learning (FedRL) in distributed energy coordination, showing that a simple penalty-based weighting scheme ($w_i \propto R_i - αV_i$) effectively improves safety and reward trade-offs over FedAvg without requiring dual optimization or local training modifications, validated on DairyGridEnv and real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FedRL aggregation methods like FedAvg ignore system-level constraints, leading to unsafe global behavior in distributed energy resource coordination where safety is critical.

Method: Proposes constraint-aware aggregation rules that incorporate both local performance (reward $R_i$) and estimated constraint violation ($V_i$) into server-side updates. The key method is a penalty-based rule $w_i \propto R_i - αV_i$, compared against a combined reward-violation scheme with tunable $λ$.

Result: Penalty-based aggregation substantially reduces violations while improving reward relative to FedAvg in both synthetic and real load-driven settings (DairyGridEnv, Finland load profiles, German FIELD dataset). The combined reward-violation scheme exposes tunable trade-off via $λ$ but is less stable.

Conclusion: Lightweight aggregation strategies can substantially improve empirical safety in federated reinforcement learning while preserving standard communication protocols, with the penalty-based rule providing the most reliable reward-safety trade-off without complex optimization.

Abstract: Federated Reinforcement Learning (FedRL) enables coordination of distributed energy resources without sharing raw local data, but standard aggregation methods such as FedAvg do not account for system-level constraints, often leading to unsafe global behavior. In this work, we study constraint-aware aggregation for federated reinforcement learning in distributed energy coordination. We propose aggregation rules that incorporate both local performance and estimated constraint violation into the server-side update. Among these, a simple penalty-based rule, $w_i \propto R_i - αV_i$, consistently provides the most reliable trade-off between reward and safety, without requiring dual optimization or modifications to local training. \textcolor{black}{We evaluate our approach on DairyGridEnv, a benchmark modeling multiple farms coordinating battery storage under stochastic demand and a shared grid capacity constraint, and further assess robustness using real load-driven demand profiles from Finland and the German FIELD dataset. Across multiple seeds, penalty-based aggregation substantially reduces violations while improving reward relative to FedAvg in both synthetic and real load-driven settings.} A combined reward-violation scheme exposes a tunable trade-off via $λ$, but is less stable. These results demonstrate that lightweight aggregation strategies can substantially improve empirical safety in federated reinforcement learning while preserving standard communication protocols.

</details>


### [167] [Learning Mechanistic Reasoning for Chemical Reactions with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12771)
*Xingyu Dang,Haocheng Tang,Junmei Wang,Yanjun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a large-scale reasoning dataset of reaction mechanisms and FukuyamaBench, a benchmark for hierarchical mechanism reasoning. Fine-tuning Qwen3-30B-A3B on mechanism-aware training improves exact pathway match accuracy (8.3%) over the specialized FlowER model (5.1%), demonstrating enhanced chemical reasoning in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs focus on coarse-grained name reactions for product prediction and retrosynthesis, leading to physical inconsistencies and hallucinations; specialized small-scale generative models for mechanism inference have limited generalization. There is a need for mechanism-aware LLMs that can perform stepwise deduction of reaction mechanisms.

Method: Built a novel large-scale reasoning dataset of reaction mechanisms; created FukuyamaBench (from Fukuyama's Advanced Organic Reaction Mechanism book) as a rigorous benchmark for hierarchical mechanism reasoning; fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B on mechanism-aware training.

Result: Fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B achieves 8.3% exact pathway match on FukuyamaBench Set A, surpassing the specialized FlowER model (5.1%).

Conclusion: Mechanism-aware training substantially enhances chemical reasoning in language models, enabling better stepwise deduction of reaction mechanisms.

Abstract: Reaction mechanisms consist of the step-by-step sequences of elementary reactions that explain chemical transformations. Learning the mechanism logic is therefore essential for enhancing the fundamental chemical intelligence of large language models (LLMs). The stepwise deduction of reaction mechanism aligns naturally with the reasoning paradigms of reasoning LLMs. However, current chemical LLMs primarily emphasize coarse-grained name reactions for product prediction and retrosynthesis, often leading to physical inconsistencies and hallucinations. In contrast, specialized small-scale generative models for mechanism inference typically suffer from restricted generalization capacity across diverse chemical spaces. To overcome these limitations, we built a novel, large-scale reasoning dataset of reaction mechanisms. Furthermore, we established the FukuyamaBench, a difficult benchmark derived from Fukuyama's Advanced Organic Reaction Mechanism book, to rigorously evaluate model performance on hierarchical mechanism reasoning. Our fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B achieves 8.3% exact pathway match on FukuyamaBench Set~A, surpassing the specialized FlowER model (5.1%), demonstrating that mechanism-aware training substantially enhances chemical reasoning in language models.

</details>


### [168] [AVQ-Attention: Adaptive Vector-Quantized Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12789)
*Winfried van den dool,Patrick Forré,Amir Habibian,Yuki M. Asano,Max Welling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AVQ Attention adaptively allocates codebook capacity based on attention importance, using child codewords to refine important regions while keeping coarse quantization elsewhere, achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs than fixed-codebook VQ-attention with O(MN) complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention has O(N²) complexity; VQ-attention reduces it to O(MN) but uses uniform codebook capacity regardless of where attention mass concentrates, leading to coarse approximation of high-attention regions and wasted capacity on low-attention regions.

Method: AVQ starts with a small set of codewords, identifies the most important codes during the forward pass via importance scoring, refines them with pre-learned child codewords, and replaces parent contributions. The full adaptive refinement process is implemented with custom Triton kernels within Flash Attention's tiled computation paradigm.

Result: AVQ maintains O(MN) complexity while achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs compared to fixed-codebook VQ-attention.

Conclusion: Adaptive codebook allocation based on attention importance is an effective strategy for improving VQ-attention, enabling finer quantization where attention mass concentrates without increasing overall computational complexity.

Abstract: The $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ complexity of attention over $N$ tokens remains a computational bottleneck in transformer models. Vector-Quantized (VQ) attention reduces this to $\mathcal{O}(MN)$ by representing keys with $M$ codewords, but applies uniform codebook capacity regardless of where attention mass concentrates: high-attention regions of key space may be coarsely approximated while low-attention regions waste representational capacity. We propose Adaptive Vector-Quantized (AVQ) Attention, which adaptively allocates codebook capacity based on attention importance. Starting from a small set of codewords, our method identifies the most important codes during the forward pass and refines them with pre-learned child codewords, achieving fine-grained quantization where it matters most while maintaining coarse quantization elsewhere. We develop an implementation using custom Triton kernels that enables the full adaptive refinement process, including importance scoring, child codeword insertion, and parent contribution replacement, to be carried out within the tiled computation paradigm of Flash Attention with minimal overhead. Our approach maintains $\mathcal{O}(MN)$ complexity while achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs compared to fixed-codebook VQ-attention.

</details>


### [169] [Accelerating Masked Diffusion Large Language Models: A Survey of Efficient Inference Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12829)
*Daehoon Gwak,Minhyung Lee,Junwoo Park,Jaegul Choo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey introduces a unified latency decomposition framework for diffusion large language models (dLLMs) to disentangle algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors affecting inference speed. It categorizes acceleration techniques across three axes—algorithmic innovations, architectural/system optimizations, and inference-time scaling—and provides guidelines for reproducible benchmarking of parallel generation efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While dLLMs theoretically enable parallel generation, practical speedups are not guaranteed due to complex interactions between algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors. Existing benchmarks conflate these factors, making rigorous comparisons of inference efficiency difficult.

Method: The paper proposes a unified latency decomposition framework to disentangle the factors contributing to end-to-end latency. It categorizes acceleration techniques into three axes: (1) algorithmic innovations, (2) architectural and system optimizations, and (3) inference-time scaling. It also provides guidelines for reproducible benchmarking.

Result: A systematic categorization of dLLM acceleration techniques and a unified framework for analyzing and comparing inference latency across different approaches.

Conclusion: Realizing the parallel generation potential of dLLMs requires specialized inference mechanisms and careful co-design across algorithms, architectures, and systems. The framework and guidelines aim to enable fair comparisons and highlight open challenges for achieving practical speedups.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) offer a theoretical advantage in parallel generation over standard autoregressive models. However, parallel generation alone does not guarantee practical speedups. Realizing this efficiency requires specialized inference mechanisms, such as diffusion-aware caching and reuse. Consequently, as inference efficiency becomes a prerequisite for practical deployment, recent research has actively explored acceleration techniques across algorithms, architectures, and systems. However, rigorous comparisons remain difficult, as end-to-end latency stems from intricate trade-offs between algorithmic, architectural, and system-level factors that are often conflated in existing benchmarks. In this survey, we introduce a unified latency decomposition framework for dLLMs to disentangle these factors and analyze their impact on inference speed in real deployments. Guided by this framework, we categorize acceleration techniques along three axes covering algorithmic innovations, architectural and system optimizations, and inference-time scaling. Finally, we provide guidelines for reproducible benchmarking and highlight open challenges for realizing the full potential of parallel generation.

</details>


### [170] [Verifier-Based Reinforcement Fine-Tuning of Reasoning Models for Thermal Energy Storage Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12856)
*Takumi Shioda,Kohei Terashima,Tatsuo Nagai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper adapts an open-weight reasoning model using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) based on exact dynamic programming (DP) action values to schedule thermal energy storage (TES) in buildings. With only 30 training prompts, reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) reduces emissions from 70.5 to 61.2 kg-CO2, approaching the DP optimum of 60.8 kg-CO2, outperforming GPT-4o and nearly matching GPT-5 and MPC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Buildings need to shift cooling loads in response to grid conditions via TES, but existing approaches like MPC and reinforcement learning are difficult to scale across diverse buildings. A scalable, transferable scheduling method is needed.

Method: The study converts exact offline DP action values into dense verifiable rewards for every candidate action. Using only 30 training prompts, reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) trains an open-weight reasoning model as an upper-level scheduler that outputs hourly heat-pump setpoints from text-based states and forecasts.

Result: RFT reduces emissions from 70.5 to 61.2 kg-CO2, close to the DP optimum of 60.8 kg-CO2. GPT-5 (reasoning LLM) nearly matches DP and MPC without task-specific training, while GPT-4o (non-reasoning LLM) performs worse than the no-storage baseline. RFT mainly stabilizes observable planning patterns (candidate comparison, look-ahead, feasibility checking) rather than creating new strategies. The planning patterns persist under forecast errors, unseen TES conditions, and partially transfer to a battery task.

Conclusion: DP-based verifiable rewards offer a practical way to adapt open-weight reasoning models to building storage scheduling. Results motivate higher-fidelity tests of whole-building control and scalable verifiers for city-scale energy management.

Abstract: Buildings are expected to shift cooling loads in response to grid conditions. Thermal energy storage (TES) enables this shift, but scheduling it well requires planning hours ahead under storage constraints. Model predictive control (MPC) and reinforcement learning are difficult to scale across buildings. This study instead adapts an open-weight reasoning model through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We convert exact offline dynamic-programming (DP) action values into dense rewards for every candidate action. Using only 30 training prompts, reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) trains the model as an upper-level scheduler that outputs hourly heat-pump setpoints from text-based states and forecasts. Evaluation uses a deliberately simple office-building TES benchmark where exact DP is tractable and the optimum is known. RFT reduces the open-weight model's emissions from 70.5 to 61.2 kg-CO2, close to the DP optimum of 60.8 kg-CO2. GPT-5 nearly matches DP and MPC without task-specific training, while GPT-4o, a non-reasoning LLM, produces higher emissions than the no-storage baseline, so inference-time reasoning appears important. Trace analysis shows that RFT mainly stabilizes observable planning patterns (candidate comparison, look-ahead, and feasibility checking) rather than creating a new strategy. Robustness and generalization tests clarify what transfers: the reinforced planning patterns persist under forecast errors and an unseen TES condition and carry over to a battery task, but its different structure limits the gains. DP-based verifiable rewards offer a practical way to adapt open-weight reasoning models to building storage scheduling. These results motivate higher-fidelity tests of whole-building control and scalable verifiers for city-scale energy management.

</details>


### [171] [Energy-Based Physics-Informed Form Finding for Clustered Tensegrity Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12888)
*Jing Qin,Muhao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an energy-based learning framework for tensegrity form-finding and physical property prediction that integrates total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations into the training objective, improving physical consistency, robustness, and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tensegrity form-finding and physical property prediction are challenging inverse problems due to strong nonlinearity from geometry-force coupling, stability requirements, and constraint enforcement (boundary conditions, symmetry). Traditional methods lack robustness to noise and outliers.

Method: An energy-based learning framework that incorporates total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations into the training objective, enabling simultaneous prediction of equilibrium nodal configurations and associated physical quantities (member forces, force densities).

Result: Numerical experiments on tensegrity structures (prism and lander systems) demonstrate great potential for scalable form finding and accurate structural property prediction.

Conclusion: The proposed energy-based learning framework improves physical consistency, robustness, and data efficiency compared to traditional methods for clustered tensegrity form finding and physical property prediction.

Abstract: Tensegrity form-finding and physical property prediction are fundamental inverse problems in structural mechanics, which aim to determine equilibrium configurations and internal force distributions. These problems are challenging due to strong nonlinearity arising from the coupling between geometry and forces, the need to ensure structural stability, and the enforcement of constraints such as boundary conditions and symmetry. Moreover, traditional methods often lack robustness to noise and outliers. This paper proposes an energy-based learning framework for clustered tensegrity form finding and physical property prediction. The proposed approach incorporates total potential energy minimization and constitutive relations into the training objective, enabling the simultaneous prediction of equilibrium nodal configurations and associated physical quantities, including member forces and force densities. By incorporating energy-based physical losses directly into the learning process, the framework improves physical consistency, robustness, and data efficiency. Numerical experiments on tensegrity structures, including prism and lander systems, show the great potential of the proposed approach and demonstrate its capability for scalable form finding and accurate prediction of structural properties.

</details>


### [172] [Contrastive-Collapsed Loss for Flexible and Geometrically Optimal Embeddings and Faster Convergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12916)
*Blanca Cano-Camarero,Ángela Fernández-Pascual,José R. Dorronsoro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoCo is a novel loss function that combines intra-class collapse and inter-class contrast to learn normalized, well-structured representations. It achieves competitive performance on tabular data, with theoretical advantages in initialization, gradient informativeness, and class collapse over dot regression and cross-entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To design a loss function that simultaneously encourages intra-class representation collapse and inter-class angular separation, overcoming limitations of existing objectives like dot regression and cross-entropy in terms of initialization, gradient quality, and class-wise collapse strength.

Method: CoCo loss promotes intra-class collapse (representations within same class cluster tightly) and inter-class contrast (large angular separation between classes). Theoretical analysis compares CoCo to dot regression and cross-entropy on optimal configuration initialization, gradient informativeness, and class collapse incentives. Empirical validation on OpenML-CC18 tabular benchmark against kernel SVM, Random Forest, dot regression, and cross-entropy neural networks.

Result: CoCo achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods on diverse tabular datasets. Theoretical and empirical evidence shows tighter class clustering and faster convergence compared to dot regression and cross-entropy-based neural networks.

Conclusion: CoCo loss is an effective objective for learning discriminative representations while maintaining competitive predictive performance, benefiting from closer initialization to optimal embeddings, more informative gradients, and stronger class-wise collapse incentives.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce CoCo, a loss function aimed at learning normalized and well-structured representations. The proposed loss encourages intra-class collapse and inter-class contrast while preserving sufficient flexibility for neural networks to approximate geometrically optimal embeddings with large angular separation between classes. We provide a theoretical analysis positioning CoCo with respect to related objectives such as dot regression and cross-entropy, showing that the new proposed loss benefits from closer initialization to the optimal configuration, more informative gradients, and stronger incentives for class-wise representation collapse. Extensive experiments on diverse tabular datasets from the OpenML-CC18 benchmark show that CoCo achieves competitive performance with state-of-the-art methods, including kernel SVM, Random Forest, dot regression, and cross-entropy-based neural networks. In addition, both theoretical arguments and empirical analyses demonstrate that the proposal promotes tighter class clustering and faster convergence. These results highlight CoCo loss as an effective objective for learning discriminative representations while maintaining competitive predictive performance.

</details>


### [173] [Efficient Sequential Calibration with $O(T^{2/3-ε})$ Error Bound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12928)
*Zihan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an efficient randomized forecaster that achieves expected calibration error O(T^{2/3-ε}) for online binary sequential calibration, improving over the recent breakthrough that overcame the classical T^{2/3} barrier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To further improve calibration error bounds in online binary sequential prediction, building on recent work that broke the long-standing T^{2/3} barrier for calibration error.

Method: Combines the SPR-Calibration procedure (which controls calibration w.r.t. surrogate conditional-mean estimates) with an outer Blackwell-style correction layer (which controls the discrepancy between surrogate sequence and true outcomes). Error decomposition separates surrogate calibration error from residual discrepancy; the latter is bounded via quadratic potential arguments and sparsity of SPR-Calibration.

Result: Expected calibration error O(T^{2/3-ε}) for some constant ε>0, strictly improving over the T^{2/3} rate.

Conclusion: The proposed two-layer architecture (SPR-Calibration + Blackwell correction) yields a provably better calibration rate than existing methods, demonstrating that the T^{2/3} barrier can be broken further.

Abstract: We study the online binary sequential calibration problem. A recent breakthrough by \citet{dagan2024breaking} overcomes the classical \(T^{2/3}\) barrier for calibration error. Building on this result, we present an efficient randomized forecaster that achieves an expected calibration error \(O(T^{2/3-\varepsilon})\) for some constant \(\varepsilon>0\).
  Our forecaster combines the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} procedure \citep{dagan2024breaking} with an outer Blackwell-style correction layer. The \textsc{SPR-Calibration} procedure controls calibration with respect to a surrogate sequence of conditional-mean estimates, while the correction layer controls the additional error incurred when these surrogates are used to approximate the true outcomes. The analysis decomposes the total calibration error into the surrogate calibration error and the residual discrepancy between the surrogate sequence and the true outcomes. The former is bounded by the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} guarantee in \citet{dagan2024breaking}, and the latter is controlled using a quadratic potential argument together with the sparsity of the \textsc{SPR-Calibration} forecaster.

</details>


### [174] [The Spectrum Is Not Enough: When Context Helps Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13006)
*Mert Onur Cakiroglu,Mehmet Dalkilic,Hasan Kurban*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that spectral predictability scores (like spectral entropy) are not equivalent to measuring whether adding context (longer lookback, retrieval, or foundation models) improves forecasting. The authors prove that any phase-invariant spectral index cannot capture beyond-second-order structure that retrieval and foundation models exploit. They introduce a "coverage deficit" diagnostic to separate second-order (linear) from beyond-second-order value, validated across seven benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners misinterpret spectral predictability scores as answering whether adding context (retrieval, pretrained models, longer lookback) will help forecasting. These are fundamentally different questions, as spectral indices are phase-invariant and cannot capture beyond-second-order structure that modern forecasting methods provide.

Method: The authors (1) prove an impossibility result that any power-spectrum-based index is invariant under phase randomization, while beyond-second-order value is not; (2) construct surrogate pairs fixing spectrum and marginal; (3) propose a label-free diagnostic called "coverage deficit" whose principal term measures beyond-spectrum structure as the gain of analog over linear prediction.

Result: On seven benchmarks: window-keyed retrieval's value collapses across surrogate pairs (ECL median +33% → -35%, p<10^-40) while spectral indices stay frozen; foundation model value splits into surviving second-order part and collapsing beyond-linear margin; longer linear window's value survives. Leave-one-dataset-out, the structure term predicts beyond-spectrum value sign where spectral indices trail.
