Conclusion: Spectral predictability indices answer a different question than whether adding context helps forecasting. The proposed coverage deficit diagnostic separates second-order from beyond-second-order contributions, enabling better deployment decisions for forecasting systems.

Abstract: A growing family of indices scores how predictable a series is from its spectrum. Practitioners increasingly read these scores as answering a different question: whether \emph{adding context}, a longer lookback, a retrieval plug-in, or a pretrained model, will help. These are not the same question. The value of context is a property of the operating point, not of the series. Any index built from the power spectrum is invariant under phase randomization, whereas the beyond-second-order value that retrieval and foundation models supply is not, because a phase-randomized series is asymptotically Gaussian. We state this as an impossibility result and isolate it with surrogate pairs that fix the spectrum and the marginal by construction. We then give a label-free, configuration-level diagnostic, the coverage deficit, whose principal term measures beyond-spectrum structure as the gain of analog over linear prediction. On seven benchmarks the prediction holds: window-keyed retrieval's value collapses across surrogate pairs (ECL median $+33\%\!\to\!-35\%$, $p{<}10^{-40}$) while every spectral index stays frozen; a foundation model's value splits into a surviving second-order part and a small beyond-linear margin that collapses; a longer linear window's value survives. Leave-one-dataset-out, the structure term predicts the sign of beyond-spectrum value where the spectral indices trail it, and the reverse holds for the second-order mechanism. We introduce no new forecaster; the contribution is the distinction, a controlled comparison, and a diagnostic for the deployment decision. Code: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SINE.

</details>


### [175] [TerraZero: Procedural Driving Simulation for Zero-Demonstration Self-Play at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13028)
*Zhouchonghao Wu,Akshay Rangesh,Weixin Li,Wei-Jer Chang,Zachary Lee,Tim Wang,Wei Zhan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TerraZero is a procedural driving simulator and self-play training stack that achieves 1.3M agent-steps per second, trains policies from scratch via RL without human demonstrations, and tops the InterPlan long-tail benchmark while being the safest on routine-driving val14.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing driving simulators either sacrifice speed for realism/diversity or omit heterogeneous agent behaviors, traffic rules, and safety-critical long-tail scenarios; logged data rarely covers rare events needed for robust autonomous driving training.

Method: A configurable C engine with zero-copy CPU-GPU pipeline; logged data used only for map geometry; randomized rule-based road users, signal controllers, agent dynamics, rewards, and sizes per episode for unbounded scenario generation; pure self-play RL across GPUs with no human demonstrations or fallback planner.

Result: 1.3M agent-steps/sec on a server-grade GPU; first fully learned policy to top InterPlan long-tail benchmark; best collision and time-to-collision on val14; competitive with reference-anchored methods on Waymo Open Sim Agents realism; zero-shot generalization across cities and left-hand traffic.

Conclusion: TerraZero demonstrates that pure RL self-play with procedurally generated maps can replace human demonstrations and fallback planners, achieving state-of-the-art safety and long-tail performance across both ego driving and simulation agent roles.

Abstract: Training robust autonomous driving agents requires a simulator that is fast enough for reinforcement learning at scale, realistic enough to ground behavior in real-world map structure, and diverse enough to cover the safety-critical long tail that logged data rarely contains. We present TerraZero, a procedural driving simulator and self-play training stack. A configurable C engine runs simulation on the CPU and policy inference on the GPU over a zero-copy path, sustaining 1.3M agent-steps per second on a single server-grade GPU, far faster than existing object-level simulators, while keeping fidelity lighter single-agent systems omit: heterogeneous agents, multiple dynamics models, and full traffic-rule enforcement. TerraZero treats logged data only as a source of real-world map geometry, populating each map with randomized rule-based road users and signal controllers and randomizing agent dynamics, rewards, and sizes per episode, so a map yields an unbounded set of scenarios. Every reported policy trains from scratch by reinforcement learning alone on a compute-efficient self-play recipe across GPUs, with zero human demonstrations and no fallback planner at inference. Policies generalize zero-shot across cities and datasets, including emergent left-hand-traffic driving without explicit supervision. As an ego policy, TerraZero is the first fully learned policy to top the InterPlan long-tail benchmark, ahead of larger learned planners; on routine-driving val14 it ranks among the best approaches and is the safest, posting the best collision and time-to-collision scores. On Waymo Open Sim Agents realism the same recipe outperforms other demonstration-free methods and is competitive with the strongest reference-anchored self-play method. One stack serves both roles: driving policies across dynamics for cars and trucks, and sim agents that jointly control vehicles, pedestrians, and cyclists.

</details>


### [176] [The Seriality Gap in Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13031)
*Jorge Diaz Chao,Konpat Preechakul,Yuxi Liu,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Video diffusion models struggle with multi-ball bounce dynamics because their denoising loop doesn't provide scalable serial computation for dependent-event reasoning, a phenomenon called the "seriality gap."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate why video diffusion models fail on tasks requiring reasoning about causal chains (e.g., multi-ball collisions) and to identify the structural limitation behind this degradation.

Method: Controlled experiments on multi-ball hard-sphere dynamics comparing standard bidirectional video diffusion with length-matched single-ball controls, intervention studies varying serial computation (autoregressive/blockwise generation, architectural depth), and a theoretical proof about denoising steps and serial computation.

Result: Performance degrades as causal chain length increases in multi-ball settings but not in length-matched single-ball controls. Methods increasing serial computation (autoregressive generation, deeper architectures) improve performance disproportionately. Theoretically, denoising steps do not add serial computation beyond the backbone for deterministic video prediction.

Conclusion: There is a structural obstacle—the "seriality gap"—where video diffusion models' denoising loop cannot provide scalable serial compute, making them fundamentally limited for serial reasoning and simulation tasks.

Abstract: When one ball strikes another, then another, video models should predict the consequences of each bounce. In controlled experiments on multi-ball hard-sphere dynamics, we find that the performance of standard bidirectional video diffusion degrades as the causal chain lengthens, even when provided more denoising steps. In a length-matched single-ball control, where ball-ball interactions are absent, the degradation largely disappears, isolating dependent-event structure rather than video length as the cause. Across intervention studies, methods that increase effective serial computation improve performance disproportionately, including autoregressive/blockwise generation and architectural depth. We identify this pattern as the seriality gap: a mismatch between tasks requiring growing serial computation and video diffusion models whose denoising loop does not provide scalable serial compute. We then prove that, for deterministic video prediction, denoising steps do not add serial computation beyond the backbone, indicating a structural obstacle for video diffusion on serial reasoning and simulation tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [177] [Predicting Acceptance and Review Effort in Human and Agent Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12057)
*Kartik Ghanshyambhai Pansuriya,Ehsan Ghorbani,Deepak Singh,Eman Abdullah AlOmar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether the acceptance and review effort of pull requests (PRs) can be predicted at submission time, before any reviewer discussion or CI feedback. Using the AIDev dataset and submission-time features only, tree-based models achieve F1 > 0.95 for acceptance prediction, while review-effort prediction remains challenging, indicating that early PR models can assist triage but not replace human judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI coding agents increasingly submit code changes alongside humans, maintainers need effective triage for PRs. The paper aims to determine if PR outcomes (acceptance and review effort) can be estimated from submission-time signals alone, before any reviewer or CI feedback is available.

Method: Using the AIDev dataset, the authors construct a leakage-aware prediction pipeline. Features are limited to submission-time information: PR text characteristics, metadata, repository context, temporal signals, and lightweight diff statistics. They evaluate Logistic Regression, Random Forests, Gradient Boosting, Extra Trees, and MLPs across pooled, human-only, agent-only, and balanced contributor views.

Result: Acceptance prediction is highly feasible with tree-based models achieving F1 > 0.95; textual clarity and metadata are key predictors. Review-effort prediction (comment counts, time-to-merge) is only modestly explained by submission-time features, suggesting that reviewer availability, project workflow, and team-specific practices dominate.

Conclusion: Early PR models can support triage and reviewer prioritization, but should be used as advisory tools rather than automated decision-makers due to limited explainability of review effort from submission-time features alone.

Abstract: Pull requests (PRs) are a central mechanism for reviewing and integrating code changes in modern software repositories. As AI coding agents begin to submit more code changes alongside human developers, maintainers face a new challenge: deciding which PRs are likely to be accepted and which ones may require substantial review effort. This paper studies whether such outcomes can be estimated at the time a PR is opened, before reviewer discussion, CI feedback, or merge decisions are available. Using the AIDev dataset, we construct a leakage-aware prediction pipeline for human- and agent-authored PRs. The feature set is limited to submission-time information, including PR text characteristics, metadata, repository context, temporal signals, and lightweight diff statistics. We evaluate classical machine-learning models, including Logistic Regression, Random Forests, Gradient Boosting, Extra Trees, and MLPs, across pooled, human-only, agent-only, and balanced contributor views. Our results show that acceptance prediction is feasible from early signals: tree-based models achieve F1 scores above 0.95, with textual clarity and metadata among the most influential predictors. Review-effort prediction is more difficult. Comment counts and time-to-merge are only modestly explained by submission-time features, suggesting that reviewer availability, project workflow, and team-specific review practices play a major role. These findings indicate that early PR models can support triage and reviewer prioritization, but should be used as advisory tools rather than automated decision-makers.

</details>


### [178] [AutoTrace: From Patches to Triggers via Agentic Interprocedural Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12058)
*Arastoo Zibaeirad,Marco Vieira,Thomas Zimmermann*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AutoTrace is an agentic pipeline that localizes vulnerability triggers by exploring code property graphs layer by layer with LLM agents and deterministic admissibility gates. It achieves 75.0% VulnHit and 80.8% FuncHit on InterPVD, surpassing prior SOTA. The paper also introduces SinkTrace-Bench, a dataset of 1,542 verifier-confirmed source-to-sink causal chains, exposing the causal-reasoning gap in frontier LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trigger localization is harder than binary vulnerability detection because it requires interprocedural, causal reasoning—the triggering statement often lies several call layers outside the patched function, beyond static rules and pattern-matching LLMs.

Method: AutoTrace uses an agentic pipeline that explores a code property graph layer by layer, with LLM agents deciding where to look next and deterministic admissibility gates ensuring every reported trigger is backed by explicit graph evidence. SinkTrace-Bench constructs matched vulnerable/patched source-to-sink causal chains verified by a verifier and audited against expert annotations.

Result: AutoTrace achieves 75.0% VulnHit and 80.8% FuncHit on InterPVD, surpassing prior SOTA. Frontier LLMs struggle on SinkTrace-Bench, exposing the causal-reasoning gap.

Conclusion: AutoTrace effectively localizes both intra- and interprocedural vulnerability triggers without relying on ungrounded model judgment, and SinkTrace-Bench reveals that even the strongest LLMs struggle with causal reasoning for vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Given a vulnerability-fixing commit, trigger localization asks which specific statement turns the vulnerable program state into a concrete unsafe operation. This question is harder than binary vulnerability detection because the answer demands interprocedural, causal reasoning: in a substantial fraction of real-world CVEs the triggering statement lies several call layers outside the patched function, beyond the reach of static rule sets and pattern-matching language models alike. We present AutoTrace, an agentic pipeline that localizes vulnerability triggers by exploring a code property graph layer by layer, with LLM agents deciding where to look next and deterministic admissibility gates deciding what evidence is required before a trigger can be reported. Agents never accept a trigger on their own authority; every reported trigger is backed by explicit evidence drawn from the graph, so the pipeline covers both intra- and interprocedural vulnerabilities without relying on ungrounded model judgment. On the full InterPVD benchmark, AutoTrace reaches 75.0% VulnHit and 80.8% FuncHit, surpassing the prior state of the art on the same corpus. Building on the same machinery, we construct SinkTrace-Bench, a dataset that exposes each vulnerability as a source-to-sink (S2S) causal chain from attacker-controlled input through propagation to the dangerous operation, drawn from matched vulnerable and patched program states. It comprises 1,542 verifier-confirmed, perfectly balanced vulnerable/safe samples whose label fidelity we audit against expert annotations. Benchmarking frontier LLMs on it, we find that even the strongest struggle to separate the matched pairs, exposing the causal-reasoning gap that trigger localization targets. Artifact available at https://github.com/Erroristotle/AutoTrace.

</details>


### [179] [Beyond Test Presence: Assessing the Quality and Robustness of Agent-Generated Tests in Open-Source Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12068)
*Preet Jhanglani,Zeel Kaushal Desai,Vidhi Kansara,Eman Abdullah AlOmar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A large-scale study comparing 204,673 test artifacts (human vs. AI agent) finds that AI agents excel at edge-case coverage (variety score 0.62 vs 0.32) but produce flakier tests (candidate rate 0.41 vs 0.30), while humans lead slightly in assertion strength (88.1% vs 85.37%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CI/CD evaluation benchmarks (e.g., SWE-bench) focus on pass-rates rather than test quality, potentially masking "stealth technical debt" where tests pass but lack coverage or semantic value.

Method: Analyzed 204,673 test artifacts (24,941 human-authored, 179,732 AI-generated) from AIDev dataset using AST-based static analysis to assess Assertion Strength, Edge-Case Coverage, and Flakiness Potential.

Result: AI agents outperform humans in edge-case coverage (variety score 0.62 vs 0.32; null-safety 13.40% vs 8.3%) but humans have stronger assertions (88.1% vs 85.37%). AI tests show higher flakiness risk (0.41 vs 0.30) due to file I/O and non-deterministic logic.

Conclusion: AI agents lack "environmental awareness" for stable, hermetic tests despite excelling at rigorous boundary testing; suggests need for quality-aware evaluation beyond pass-rate metrics.

Abstract: The integration of AI-powered coding agents into Continuous Integration/Continuous Delivery (CI/CD) pipelines has fundamentally altered how software verification is conducted. While these agents successfully automate the test generation, current evaluation benchmarks (e.g., SWE-bench) largely focus on pass-rates rather than the intrinsic quality of the generated tests. This raises the possibility of "stealth technical debt", in which test suites pass execution but do not offer comprehensive coverage or semantic value. We address this methodological gap through a large-scale, empirical comparison of 204,673 test artifacts which comprises of 24,941 human-authored files and 179,732 agent-generated files; sourced from the AIDev dataset. Using the Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) parsing with Python's naive ast module, we implemented a "white-box" static analysis framework to evaluate three quality dimensions: Assertion Strength (RQ1), Edge-Case Coverage (RQ2), and Flakiness Potential (RQ3). Our results present a nuanced inversion of traditional assumptions. AI agents performed better than humans in Edge-Case Coverage, with almost twice the variety of boundary checks (Variety Score: 0.62 vs 0.32) and a higher frequency of null-safety testing (13.40% vs. 8.3%), even though human developers had a slight advantage in Assertion Strength (88.1% strong assertions vs. 85.37% for agents). But this thoroughness comes at a price: due mostly to their reliance on file I/O and non-deterministic logic, agent-generated tests exhibited a higher risk of flakiness (Candidate Rate: 0.41 vs. 0.30). These findings suggest that while AI agents excel at rigorous boundary testing, they lack the "environmental awareness" needed to write stable, hermetic tests.

</details>


### [180] [Fault Injection in OpenAPI Specifications for Evaluating Black-Box Testing Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12101)
*Hamza Bin Mazhar,Yuqing Wang,Mika V. Mäntylä*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes, injects them at five severity levels into two microservice benchmarks, and evaluates three black-box testing tools. Results show specification faults cause heterogeneous degradation across tools and systems, with some faults (method semantics) broadly impacting metrics while others (schema relaxations) silently degrade request/response quality without affecting coverage metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows OpenAPI specifications are often incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrect, yet most studies on OpenAPI-based black-box testing assume correct specifications. This gap motivates the need to systematically understand how specification faults impact testing tool effectiveness.

Method: The authors introduce a literature-grounded taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes, inject faults at five severity levels into two microservice benchmarks (TrainTicket, SocialNetwork), and evaluate three testing tools (EvoMaster, RESTler, Schemathesis) using code coverage, specification coverage, request/response quality, and behavioral diversity.

Result: Specification faults cause strong and heterogeneous degradation across tools and systems. Faults in method semantics broadly degrade all metrics, while response code modifications have weak impact. Schema constraint relaxations cause hidden degradation—no impact on coverage metrics but large impact on request/response quality.

Conclusion: Specification quality directly shapes black-box API testing effectiveness. Code and specification coverage-only evaluations can understate the impact of specification faults and should be complemented by request/response quality and behavioral diversity metrics.

Abstract: OpenAPI specifications are the primary input for black-box testing tools in microservice systems (MSS), yet prior work shows these specifications are often incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrect. Despite this, most studies on OpenAPI-based black-box testing assume correct specifications and evaluate tool performance. We address this gap by introducing a literature-grounded taxonomy of six OpenAPI specification fault classes. We inject faults at five severity levels, and evaluate the resulting mutated specifications on two microservice benchmarks, TrainTicket and SocialNetwork, using three testing tools: EvoMaster, RESTler, and Schemathesis. We measure the impact of these faults using code coverage, specification coverage, request/response quality, and behavioral diversity. Our results show that specification faults cause strong and heterogeneous degradation patterns across testing tools and systems. Faults in method semantics cause broad degradation across all metrics, while others, such as modifications to response codes, remain weak. Relaxations of schema constraints cause hidden degradation, with no impact on code and specification coverage but a large impact on request/response quality. These findings demonstrate that specification quality directly shapes black-box API testing effectiveness. Also, code and specification coverage-only evaluations can understate the impact of specification faults on black-box testing in MSS and should be complemented by request/response quality and behavioral diversity.

</details>


### [181] [TraceSynth: Generating Production-Quality Kernel Traces with Constraint-Guided Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12104)
*Yuvraj Sehgal,Sneh Patel,Mahsa Panahandeh,Naser Ezzati-Jivan,Francois Tetreault*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TraceSynth is a diffusion-based framework for generating synthetic kernel execution traces to augment limited real data for ML-based system diagnostics, achieving strong performance on compute-heavy workloads while highlighting context length as the dominant quality factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting real kernel execution traces in industrial production systems is costly due to runtime overhead, high storage demands, and privacy constraints, creating a need for synthetic data generation to augment limited real traces for downstream ML tasks.

Method: TraceSynth models traces as multi-channel sequences (event types, timestamps, CPU affinity, thread IDs, process metadata) using a Transformer-based denoising diffusion process with constraint-guided repair to enforce system invariants.

Result: On deterministic compute-heavy workloads (scimark2), synthetic augmentation achieves 87.2% F1-Macro at context length L=4096, only 2.6 points below real-only baselines. Context length L=4096 yields +104% relative improvement over L=256. Constraint-guided repair improves quality by up to 4.3%. Lightweight 2-channel models retain 97-99% of full 6-channel performance at half the cost.

Conclusion: TraceSynth supports cost-effective augmentation of kernel execution traces in production observability pipelines and helps identify when synthetic data can substitute for limited real traces.

Abstract: Machine learning models for system diagnostics rely on kernel execution traces to capture fine-grained system behavior, but collecting production traces in industrial systems is costly due to runtime overhead, storage demands, and privacy constraints. We present TraceSynth, a diffusion-based framework for generating synthetic kernel traces that augment limited real data for downstream ML tasks. TraceSynth models traces as multi-channel sequences (event types, timestamps, CPU affinity, thread identifiers, and process metadata) using a Transformer-based denoising diffusion process with constraint-guided repair to enforce system invariants. Across six benchmarks, results show strong workload dependence. For deterministic, compute-heavy workloads (scimark2), synthetic augmentation achieves 87.2% F1-Macro at context length L=4096, only 2.6 percentage points below real-only baselines. Context length is the dominant quality factor, with L=4096 yielding a +104% relative improvement over L=256, while constraint-guided repair improves synthetic data quality by up to 4.3%. Ablation studies show that lightweight 2-channel models retain 97-99% of the performance of full 6-channel models at roughly half the computational cost. TraceSynth supports cost-effective augmentation of kernel execution traces in production observability pipelines and helps identify when synthetic data can substitute for limited real traces.

</details>


### [182] [TestMiner: Software Testing Analysis for GitHub Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12223)
*Andre Hora,José Miguel Rojas,Romain Robbes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TestMiner is a tool that explores and visualizes software testing practices in GitHub repositories, providing statistics, test location analysis, metrics across releases, and dependency insights. Evaluated with 50 undergraduate students who gave positive feedback on exploring real-world testing practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software systems have diverse testing characteristics (e.g., varying test types, different organizational conventions across ecosystems like Python/Java vs. Go/Rust), making it difficult to understand how a project approaches testing.

Method: The authors developed TestMiner, a tool that analyzes GitHub repositories to provide an overview of testing practices including test statistics, test location, test metrics across releases, and testing-related dependencies.

Result: Used in an undergraduate Software Testing course with 50 students exploring real-world GitHub repositories. Students provided positive feedback and could critically examine test organization, test evolution, test fixtures, mocking, and edge-case testing.

Conclusion: TestMiner effectively helps users understand and explore software testing practices in real-world repositories, with positive reception from students in an educational setting.

Abstract: Software systems have unique testing characteristics. Some projects can emphasize unit tests, while others may focus on end-to-end testing. Test organization may vary across ecosystems: in languages like Python and Java, tests are typically placed in dedicated folders, whereas Go and Rust projects commonly co-locate tests with source code. These distinctions make it harder to understand how a project approaches testing. In this paper, we present TestMiner, a tool for exploring software testing in GitHub repositories. TestMiner provides an overview of a project's testing practices, including test statistics, test location, test metrics across releases, and dependencies related to testing. We used TestMiner in an undergraduate Software Testing course, where 50 students explored the testing practices of real-world GitHub repositories. Overall, students expressed positive feedback regarding TestMiner. They were able to critically explore a variety of testing practices, including test organization, test evolution, test fixtures, mocking, and edge-case testing. TestMiner is available at: https://andrehora.github.io/testminer. Screencast: https://youtu.be/w1sBgLTq-7Y.

</details>


### [183] [WIP: Software Engineering Competencies in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12259)
*Lynn Vonderhaar,Massood Towhidnejad,Omar Ochoa*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper reviews industry AI needs to propose two new competencies—AI Literacy and AI Development—for inclusion in the Software Engineering Competency Model (SWECOM), addressing the gap between rapidly evolving industry demands and static curriculum guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry AI needs evolve faster than accreditation bodies update curriculum guidelines/competency models, creating a misalignment between university education and workforce requirements.

Method: Literature review of AI needs in industry, followed by analysis to identify skill areas and propose two new competencies (AI Literacy and AI Development) for SWECOM.

Result: Identification of necessary skill areas for two new AI competencies to be integrated into the Software Engineering Competency Model.

Conclusion: The SWECOM model requires updating with AI Literacy and AI Development competencies to better align university curricula with current industry AI demands.

Abstract: A university education is meant to fully prepare graduates to enter and succeed in the workforce in their field. Accreditation bodies and professional organizations help to achieve this by publishing curriculum content, skills and competency models to outline the basics that universities must teach within a degree program. Although these curriculum guidelines and competency models are periodically updated to align university curricula with current industry needs, the needs of industry evolve rapidly and are often not properly reflected within the model, a situation which is occurring now with the rise of Artificial Intelligence (AI) use in industry. This paper reviews literature describing the AI needs within industry to motivate two new AI competencies, AI Literacy and AI development, within the Software Engineering Competency Model (SWECOM). This work then analyzes the industry needs described in the literature to propose the necessary skill areas for these new competencies.

</details>


### [184] [Code-MUE: Measuring Code LLMs' Uncertainty through Execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12273)
*Xiaoning Ren,Yinxing Xue,Lei Ma,Yuheng Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Code-MUE is a black-box framework that uses execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs and Von Neumann entropy to estimate uncertainty in Code LLMs by measuring semantic diversity through runtime behavior, achieving up to -0.98 Spearman correlation with functional correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing uncertainty estimation methods fail for Code LLMs due to: (1) white/grey-box techniques not working on closed-source models, and (2) standard black-box metrics focusing on superficial textual similarity rather than semantic correctness, missing the syntax-semantics gap in code.

Method: Code-MUE constructs Semantic Interaction Graphs from execution traces of generated code, then computes the Von Neumann entropy of the solution space to quantify global semantic diversity, grounding uncertainty in observable runtime behavior rather than textual similarity.

Result: Across eight state-of-the-art LLMs, Code-MUE achieves a strong negative correlation with functional correctness (Spearman's correlation up to -0.98), significantly outperforming lexical and embedding-based baselines.

Conclusion: Code-MUE enables robust risk detection and selective prediction in practical workflows, effectively bridging the syntax-semantics gap for black-box uncertainty estimation in code generation.

Abstract: As Code Large Language Models (LLMs) become central to modern software engineering, their inherent stochasticity poses significant real-world risks, where even minor errors can lead to severe functional, security, or safety consequences. Reliable automation, therefore, demands the ability to distinguish between confident, well-supported predictions and stochastic guessing. However, existing uncertainty estimation methods face a critical gap: white and grey-box techniques are often inapplicable to closed-source models, while standard "black-box" text metrics fail to capture the unique fragility of code, where syntactic variation does not always imply semantic divergence. To bridge this syntax-semantics gap, we introduce Code-MUE, a purely black-box framework that measures uncertainty through execution-based Semantic Interaction Graphs. Unlike prior approaches that rely on superficial textual similarity, Code-MUE grounds uncertainty in observable runtime behavior, calculating the Von Neumann entropy of the solution space to quantify global semantic diversity. A large-scale empirical study across eight state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrates that Code-MUE achieves a strong negative correlation with functional correctness (Spearman's correlation up to -0.98), significantly outperforming lexical and embedding-based baselines while enabling robust risk detection and selective prediction in practical workflows.

</details>


### [185] [XScientist: A Git-Like Research Protocol for Long-Running Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12301)
*Jixiang Luo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: XScientist is a git-like research protocol and operating system for autonomous science that treats each run as a portable, inspectable research artifact (ARA) with full provenance, rather than just a PDF, enabling reproducible, reviewable, and forkable research pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous research systems are evaluated as one-shot paper generators, hiding the operational challenges of long-running, branching, failure-prone research and the lack of auditable handoffs between agents and humans, making such systems difficult to trust.

Method: XScientist proposes a git-like research protocol with an Agent-Native Research Artifact (ARA) that records an exploration DAG, per-node code/outputs, claim-to-evidence anchors, content hashes, provenance, and re-execution hooks. It includes deterministic integrity forensics, sample gates, truth contracts, reviewer-oriented repair loops, and daemon controls.

Result: The system enables each generated paper to be inspectable as a science exploration tree where failed branches, repaired experiments, ablations, and manuscript claims remain connected to their producing nodes, allowing continuous observability of the entire pipeline.

Conclusion: XScientist moves autonomous science from single-run demos toward reproducible, reviewable, and forkable research infrastructure by providing a practical, auditable protocol and safeguards.

Abstract: Autonomous research systems are often evaluated as one-shot paper generators: given a topic, they produce a manuscript and a small set of experiment logs. This framing hides the operational problem that makes such systems difficult to trust: research is long-running, branching, failure-prone, and dependent on auditable handoffs between agents and humans. XScientist is a git-like research protocol and operating system for this setting. It orchestrates idea generation, experiment execution, manuscript drafting, self-review, repair, quality gating, daemon scheduling, and reproducibility artifacts as one continuously observable pipeline. The central design choice is to treat each run as a portable research artifact rather than only as a PDF. XScientist exports an Agent-Native Research Artifact (ARA), a protocol that records an exploration DAG, per-node code and outputs, claim-to-evidence anchors, content hashes, provenance, and re-execution hooks. This makes each generated paper inspectable as a science exploration tree: failed branches, repaired experiments, ablations, and manuscript claims remain connected to the nodes that produced them. The system also includes deterministic integrity forensics, sample gates, truth contracts, reviewer-oriented repair loops, and long-running daemon controls. This paper describes the current XScientist architecture, the ARA protocol surface, and the practical safeguards needed to move autonomous science from single-run demos toward reproducible, reviewable, and forkable research infrastructure. The implementation and manuscript source are maintained in the public GitHub repository at https://github.com/smileformylove/XScientist.

</details>


### [186] [How Many Interviews Are Enough in a Software Engineering Study? Preliminary Findings on Sample Size and Saturation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12326)
*Ronnie de Souza Santos,Italo Santos,Mauricio Rodrigues,Cleyton Magalhaes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study analyzes how interview sample adequacy and saturation are operationalized in empirical SE research (2016-2025), finding substantial variation in sample sizes (most commonly 13-24 interviewees) and heterogeneous reporting practices regarding saturation and sample justification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interview sample adequacy and saturation are reported inconsistently across the literature, motivating a systematic investigation of how these concepts are operationalized in empirical SE research.

Method: The authors analyzed papers published between 2016 and 2025 across major software engineering venues, focusing on interview sample sizes, saturation discussions, and sample adequacy justifications.

Result: Sample sizes vary substantially; studies with fewer than 12 interviewees were common in specialized industrial contexts, while the most recurrent range was 13-24 interviewees. Saturation and sample adequacy justification were heterogeneous, often relying on implicit/contextual reasoning rather than explicit methodological discussion.

Conclusion: The findings provide initial empirical insights into methodological reporting practices in interview-based SE research and contribute to discussions on qualitative rigor and transparency.

Abstract: Background. Interview based studies are widely used in empirical software engineering to investigate human, organizational, and socio technical phenomena, yet interview sample adequacy and saturation are reported inconsistently across the literature. Aims. This paper investigates how interview sample adequacy and saturation are operationalized in empirical software engineering research. Method. We analyzed papers published between 2016 and 2025 across major software engineering venues, focusing on interview sample sizes, saturation discussions, and sample adequacy justifications. Results. Preliminary findings indicate substantial variation in sample sizes, from highly specialized small sample studies to broader investigations involving large interview datasets. Studies involving fewer than 12 interviewees were common and frequently associated with specialized industrial contexts or constrained organizational access. However, the most recurrent range was 13 to 24 interviewees, suggesting that moderate sized samples represent the most common configuration in empirical software engineering research. Saturation and sample adequacy justification were heterogeneous, with many studies relying on implicit or contextual reasoning rather than explicit methodological discussion. Conclusions. Our findings provide initial empirical insights into methodological reporting practices in interview based software engineering research and contribute to ongoing discussions regarding qualitative rigor and transparency.

</details>


### [187] [Skills That Don't Exist: A Large-Scale Study of Hallucinated Skill Recommendation in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12340)
*Weifeng Yuan,Wenbo Guo,Feng Dong,Haoyu Wang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLM agents frequently hallucinate fake skill names when recommending registry skills, enabling supply-chain attacks; measurement across 15,000 prompts shows 36-43% hallucination rates across all configurations, with repeated fake names making exploitation trivial; existing defenses severely hurt usability, requiring ecosystem-wide structural changes rather than model-level fixes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents can install skills from open registries, but developers rely on agents to recommend which skills to use. The authors discovered that agents frequently invent non-existent skill names (skill name hallucination), which creates a security vulnerability where attackers can pre-register malicious skills under those hallucinated names to conduct supply-chain attacks.

Method: Conducted the first large-scale measurement of skill name hallucination across 15,000 prompts, testing 12 configurations (4 standalone LLMs and 8 agents). Hallucinated names were conservatively counted only if missing from all live registries and GitHub. Also tested 4 model-level defenses: retrieval grounding, prompt engineering, fine-tuning, and constrained decoding.

Result: Every configuration hallucinates; average rates are 36.0% for standalone LLMs and 36.9% for agents, rising to 43.1% on real-world developer questions. 5,669 distinct hallucinated names generated. Agents repeat the same fake names across prompts and models. The strongest defense (retrieval grounding) reduced hallucination to 3.2% but crippled usefulness (correct skill recommended only ~1/6 times).

Conclusion: Skill name hallucination is a highly exploitable vulnerability requiring minimal attacker effort. Fixing it cannot rely solely on prompt engineering or model tuning; it demands ecosystem-wide structural changes such as registry-level name reservations and verified recommendation pipelines.

Abstract: LLM agents acquire new capabilities by downloading skills from open registries. Instead of browsing these catalogs manually, developers typically ask the agent to recommend and install a skill. This convenience hides a risk: agents frequently invent names for skills that exist in no registry. We term this flaw skill name hallucination. A fake name may seem harmless, but it opens the door to supply-chain attacks. Because registries rarely verify publishers, an adversary can prompt the agent, collect the fake names it returns, pre-register malicious skills under them, and wait for a victim to install the payload. We conducted the first large-scale measurement of skill name hallucination, evaluating 15,000 prompts across 12 configurations (4 standalone LLMs and 8 agents). We conservatively counted a name as hallucinated only if it was missing from all live registries and GitHub. The results reveal a systemic vulnerability: every configuration hallucinates. Rates average 36.0% for standalone LLMs and 36.9% for agents, rising to 43.1% on real-world developer questions. In total, the systems generated 5,669 distinct hallucinated names. Crucially, these names are not random noise. Agents repeat the same fake names across prompts and models, giving attackers highly reliable targets to hijack. Finally, we tested four model-level defenses and found a severe conflict between security and usability. The strongest, retrieval grounding, cut the hallucination rate from 40.8% to 3.2% but crippled usefulness: even the best-defended system recommended the correct skill only about one in six times. Skill name hallucination is thus a highly exploitable vulnerability requiring minimal attacker effort. Fixing it cannot rely on prompt engineering or model tuning alone. It demands ecosystem-wide structural changes: registry-level name reservations and verified recommendation pipelines.

</details>


### [188] [Detecting Rendering Bugs in Imperative Data Visualization Libraries via Equivalent Mutations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12363)
*Weiqi Lu,Yongqiang Tian*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VIZDETOUR detects rendering bugs in imperative data visualization libraries by appending semantically equivalent API call sequences (mutations) to seed scripts and comparing visual outputs for discrepancies. It found 47 previously unknown bugs across matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly, with 39 confirmed and 18 fixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rendering bugs in imperative data visualization libraries produce incorrect visual outputs without crashes or exceptions, and there is no oracle specifying expected rendering for arbitrary plotting scripts. Updates to one graphic element can inadvertently affect others, causing subtle visual inconsistencies.

Method: VIZDETOUR transforms the oracle problem into an equivalence-checking problem by appending semantically equivalent API call sequences (mutations) to seed plotting scripts. It constructs a render tree, identifies traceable graphic elements and mutable properties, synthesizes endpoint-preserving mutation sequences, and compares rendered outputs using perceptual hashing.

Result: Evaluated on matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly using scripts from official example galleries. Discovered 47 previously unknown bugs; 39 confirmed, 18 fixed.

Conclusion: Equivalent mutation-based testing is effective for detecting rendering bugs in imperative visualization libraries, transforming the oracle problem into an equivalence-checking problem.

Abstract: Imperative data visualization libraries construct plots through a sequence of stateful API calls that incrementally create and update graphic elements. Rendering bugs in these libraries often manifest as incorrect visual outputs rather than crashes or exceptions, making them difficult to detect automatically. A fundamental challenge is the lack of an oracle that specifies the expected rendering of an arbitrary plotting script. Furthermore, an update to one graphic element may inadvertently affect other elements or properties, leading to subtle inconsistencies in the final rendered image.
  This paper presents VIZDETOUR, an automated testing approach for detecting rendering bugs in imperative data visualization libraries via equivalent mutations. The key idea is to transform the oracle problem into an equivalence-checking problem. Starting from a seed plotting script, VIZDETOUR appends a short sequence of semantically equivalent API calls that temporarily modify the visualization state and then restore it to its original state. Although these mutations exercise different execution paths, they should preserve the final rendering. Any visual discrepancy between the original and mutated scripts therefore indicates a rendering bug. To generate such mutations, VIZDETOUR constructs a render tree from the seed script, identifies traceable graphic elements and mutable properties, and synthesizes endpoint-preserving mutation sequences. It then compares the rendered outputs using perceptual hashing. We evaluate VIZDETOUR on matplotlib, bokeh, and plotly using scripts collected from their official example galleries. VIZDETOUR discovers 47 previously unknown bugs, of which 39 are confirmed and 18 are fixed.

</details>


### [189] [Automatic Testing of Interacting Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12452)
*Fabio Cavaleri,Alessio Gambi,Paolo Arcaini,Dejan Ničković,Mauro Pezzè*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: EVITA is a multi-objective optimization approach for generating test scenarios that trigger diverse interactions among multiple autonomous vehicles, revealing safety-critical behaviors that single-AV testing approaches miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scenario-based testing approaches test single AVs and miss critical behaviors emerging from multi-AV interactions. Testing multiple interacting AVs faces a combinatorial explosion problem.

Method: EVITA uses multi-objective optimization to generate scenarios that trigger multiple and diverse AV interactions while minimizing scenario complexity.

Result: EVITA triggers a higher variety of AV interactions than state-of-the-art approaches, improving the likelihood of revealing safety-critical behaviors.

Conclusion: Multi-objective optimization is effective for generating diverse multi-AV interaction scenarios, addressing the limitations of single-AV testing and the combinatorial explosion problem.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles (AVs) must be thoroughly tested to meet high safety standards and avoid endangering both AV passengers and road users. Scenario-based testing implements driving scenarios in virtual simulation environments as a cost-effective alternative to field testing. Common scenario-based testing approaches set the environment and the surrounding traffic and test a single AV. Recent studies show that the approaches that test single AVs miss critical behaviors that emerge from interactions among multiple AVs. Effective approaches to test scenarios that emerge from n-way interactions must address the combinatorial explosion that the presence of multiple AVs further exacerbates. In this paper, we propose EVITA, an approach that leverages multi-objective optimization to generate scenarios that trigger multiple and diverse AVs interactions, while minimizing the complexity of the generated scenarios, to effectively test multiple interacting AVs and reveal safety-critical scenarios that current approaches overlook. The experimental results that we discuss in this paper confirm that EVITA triggers a higher variety of AVs interactions than state-of-the-art approaches, thus improving the likelihood to reveal safety-critical behaviors.

</details>


### [190] [CodeOwl: Automatic Generation of Tiered Parsons Problems for Introductory Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12460)
*Luca Cisternino,Florian Obermüller,Gordon Fraser*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CodeOwl is an AI tool that automatically generates tiered Parsons problems for programming education, with 98.7% of 297 tiered sequences showing positive complexity increase, though teachers desired more curriculum control and students requested better feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing learner heterogeneity in programming education is challenging due to variations in student speed, prior knowledge, and motivation, and manually creating differentiated resources like tiered sequences is labour-intensive.

Method: CodeOwl is an AI-driven tool that automates the generation of tiered Parsons problems from sample tasks or programming concepts; evaluated via mixed-method framework with complexity analysis, expert ratings, and user studies on 297 tiered sequences.

Result: 98.7% of 297 tiered sequences achieved positive complexity increase from Tier 1 to Tier 3; experts rated problem statements as highly clear; teachers praised utility but wanted more curriculum alignment control; students reported positively but requested enhanced feedback and alternative interaction modes.

Conclusion: CodeOwl effectively automates tiered Parsons problem generation to address learner heterogeneity, but future work should focus on greater teacher control over curriculum alignment and improved student feedback mechanisms and interaction modes.

Abstract: Addressing learner heterogeneity in programming education is challenging due to variations in student speed, prior knowledge, and motivation. While differentiated instruction, such as tiered sequences, allows students to engage at appropriate difficulty levels, manually creating these resources is labour-intensive. This paper introduces CodeOwl, an AI-driven tool that automates the generation of tiered Parsons problems. Starting from a sample task or specific programming concepts, CodeOwl produces tiered sequences of Parsons problems automatically. We evaluated CodeOwl with a mixed-method framework comprising complexity analysis, expert ratings, and user studies. Analysis of 297 tiered sequences (three tiers each) revealed that 98.7% achieved a positive complexity increase, successfully rising in difficulty from Tier 1 to Tier 3. Experts rated the generated problem statements as highly clear. While teachers praised the tool's utility, they identified a need for greater control over curriculum alignment. Similarly, students reported positively but requested enhanced feedback mechanisms and alternative interaction modes.

</details>


### [191] [Understanding before Naming! Enhancing LLM-based Method Name Prediction with Code Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12467)
*Wei Liu,Weisong Sun,Tingting Xu,Hanwei Qian,Yi Zhao,Chunrong Fang,Xia Feng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes SMNP, a method name prediction approach that uses LLM-based evaluation (more aligned with human judgment than token similarity metrics) and a summarization-and-refinement strategy (improving over direct code-to-name mapping). Experiments on 5 LLMs and 2 datasets show SMNP's effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Method names are critical for software quality but manually designing them is challenging. Existing MNP methods rely on token similarity metrics that poorly reflect human judgment, and current LLM-based approaches use direct code-to-name mapping rather than mimicking the human process of understanding functionality before naming.

Method: Empirical studies comparing 6 metric-based evaluators, 5 LLM-based evaluators, and 6 human evaluators; comparing direct generation vs. summarization-and-refinement strategies; proposing SMNP with MNP-oriented summarization and chain-of-thought enhanced refinement.

Result: LLM-based evaluators (especially DeepSeek-based) align better with human judgment than traditional metrics. Summarization-and-refinement generally improves semantic quality. Three limitations identified: inaccurate summaries, semantic misalignment, close semantic scores. SMNP demonstrates effectiveness and robustness across 5 LLMs and 2 datasets.

Conclusion: LLM-based evaluation is superior to token similarity metrics for assessing method name quality, and the summarization-and-refinement paradigm (embodied in SMNP) better matches human naming processes, leading to higher quality generated method names.

Abstract: Method names are critical to software quality, affecting code comprehensibility, maintainability, and developer collaboration. However, manually designing meaningful method names is challenging. Method Name Prediction (MNP), which automatically generates method names from code snippets, has recently attracted attention. Although large language models (LLMs) show promising performance for MNP, two challenges remain. First, existing evaluations mainly rely on token similarity metrics, which often fail to reflect human judgments of semantic quality. Second, current LLM-based MNP methods usually generate names through direct code-to-name mapping, which differs from the human process of understanding functionality before naming. To address these challenges, we conduct empirical studies on LLM-based evaluation and MNP strategies. We compare 6 metric-based evaluators, 5 LLM-based evaluators, and 6 human evaluators. Results show that LLM-based evaluators, especially DeepSeek-based evaluators, are more consistent with human judgments than traditional metrics. We further compare direct generation and summarization-and-refinement strategies. Results indicate that summarization and refinement generally improve the semantic quality of generated names. Case studies reveal three limitations: inaccurate summaries, semantic misalignment, and close semantic scores. Based on these findings, we propose SMNP, an MNP approach combining MNP-oriented summarization and chain-of-thought enhanced refinement. Experiments on 5 LLMs and 2 datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of SMNP.

</details>


### [192] [Agent-Safety Evaluations as Load-Bearing Evidence: A Vendor-Neutral, Cross-Harness Reconstructability Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12469)
*Oleg Solozobov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces reconstructability metrics and Evidence Sufficiency Cards to evaluate whether agent-safety evaluation results are actually load-bearing, finding that replay preconditions are unmet in all scored traces and sufficiency scores range from 0.458-0.833 across inputs sharing the same surface reading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many agent-safety evaluation results are not yet load-bearing evidence because identical nominal outcomes (task success, attack success, monitor scores) may rest on materially different evidence regimes, yet no vendor-neutral instrument exists to score reconstructability as an evaluation-validity metric.

Method: The paper introduces: (1) a property-level reconstructability metric over eight decision-property classes; (2) a cross-harness adapter emitting per-decision Evidence Sufficiency Cards; (3) a per-run monitor-coverage release check; (4) a counterfactual-replay intervention protocol with a replayability-precondition probe; and (5) a claim-evidence overclaim gap definition.

Result: On public and bundled traces (without new model runs), twelve-field sufficiency spans 0.458-0.833 across four inputs sharing a surface reading, and replay preconditions are unmet in every scored trace. In a synthetic release-gate pair, the sufficiency gate blocks the raw variant (0.542) and passes the instrumented variant (0.667).

Conclusion: Safety-evaluation claims should travel with their reconstructability vector; the paper provides a reproducibility package that regenerates every reported number.

Abstract: Many agent-safety evaluation results are not yet load-bearing evidence: identical nominal outcomes (task success, attack success, monitor scores) may sit atop materially different evidence regimes. No vendor-neutral, runnable instrument scores reconstructability as an evaluation-validity metric: whether captured evidence can reconstruct the decision a claim depends on. This paper introduces a property-level reconstructability metric over eight decision-property classes and a cross-harness adapter emitting per-decision Evidence Sufficiency Cards backing a per-run monitor-coverage release check. It specifies a counterfactual-replay intervention protocol, implements its replayability-precondition probe, and defines a claim-evidence overclaim gap. On public and bundled traces, without new model runs, twelve-field sufficiency spans 0.458-0.833 across four inputs sharing a surface reading; replay preconditions are unmet in every scored trace. In a synthetic release-gate pair, the sufficiency gate blocks the raw variant (0.542) and passes the instrumented (0.667). Safety-evaluation claims should travel with their reconstructability vector; a reproducibility package regenerates every reported number.

</details>


### [193] [Taming the Drift: Context-aware Repair of Dockerfile Drift during Software Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12541)
*Chengjie Wang,Jingzheng Wu,Xiang Ling,Tianyue Luo,Chen Zhao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Cadre is a context-aware framework that uses a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG) to repair Dockerfile drift, achieving 35.22% repair rate—2.78× over rule-based and 1.24× over LLM-based baselines on the new D³ benchmark of 1,040 real-world drift instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dockerfile drift causes CI/CD build failures, and existing rule-based and retrieval-based approaches analyze Dockerfiles in isolation, failing to handle context-dependent failures.

Method: Cadre uses static analysis to build a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG) mapping instructions to file-level and inter-instruction dependencies. It selects causally relevant context and generates targeted patches. DodeX pipeline mines real-world drift from GitHub Actions CI logs preserving full build configurations.

Result: On D³ benchmark with 1,040 drift instances, Cadre achieves 35.22% repair rate (2.78× rule-based, 1.24× LLM-based), keeps 95.25% prompts under 30k tokens, avoids prompt-overflow failures that plague LLM baselines, and improves as drift ages across commits.

Conclusion: Explicit dependency modeling via CDG and the two-step workflow significantly improve Dockerfile drift repair, supporting context-aware infrastructure-as-code maintenance.

Abstract: Docker is widely used to create reproducible build environments, but Dockerfile drift, the divergence between a Dockerfile and its evolving source code, can cause CI/CD builds to fail. Existing rule-based and retrieval-based repair approaches analyze Dockerfiles in isolation and therefore struggle with context-dependent failures. We present Cadre, a context-aware framework for automated Dockerfile drift repair. Cadre uses static analysis to construct a Context-aware Dependency Graph (CDG), which maps each Dockerfile instruction to its file-level and inter-instruction dependencies. Guided by the CDG, Cadre first selects the context causally relevant to a failure and then generates a targeted patch from that context. We also introduce DodeX, a pipeline that mines real-world Dockerfile drift instances from GitHub Actions CI logs while preserving the complete build configurations omitted by static-snapshot datasets. Using DodeX, we construct $D^3$, a benchmark of 1,040 drift instances reproducible locally with the original CI parameters. Across $D^3$, Cadre achieves a 35.22\% repair rate, 2.78$\times$ that of the rule-based baseline and 1.24$\times$ that of the best LLM-based baseline. Its two-step workflow keeps 95.25\% of prompts below 30k tokens and avoids the prompt-overflow failures that prevent competing LLM-based methods from producing patches in 41 to 58 cases per method. Ablation results confirm that both the CDG and the two-step workflow improve repair performance. Cadre's advantage over diff-only approaches also increases as drift ages across commits, supporting explicit dependency modeling for context-aware infrastructure-as-code maintenance. Code and data are available at https://github.com/dw763j/Cadre.

</details>


### [194] [Evaluation Bias and Epistemic Inequality in Global Software Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12563)
*Sam Khosravi,Amir H. Payberah*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates fairness and accountability in global software development, finding that East African engineers perform comparably to European peers but are systematically undervalued in competence assessments, highlighting biases across asymmetric regional contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of empirical evidence on how software engineering competence is evaluated across unequal global regions, particularly between emerging East African tech ecosystems and established Northwestern European ones, and to uncover potential fairness issues in competence perception.

Method: On-site mixed-methods study with 48 software engineers across four countries (Rwanda, Uganda, Sweden, Netherlands), combining programming tasks, system design exercises, code reviews, and semi-structured interviews.

Result: Senior engineers in Rwanda performed comparably to European peers, but European participants systematically underestimated East African engineers' competence. Regional differences in communication styles and organizational practices were also observed.

Conclusion: Competence assessment in global software development is biased by regional perception gaps, with implications for fairness, accountability, and equitable valuation of engineering talent across asymmetric global contexts.

Abstract: This paper examines fairness and accountability in global software development by focusing on how competence is assessed and valued across unequal regional contexts. We compare software engineers from East Africa (Rwanda and Uganda) and Northwestern Europe (Sweden and the Netherlands), regions that are increasingly connected but embedded in asymmetric technological, economic, and institutional structures. Despite the rapid growth of African technology ecosystems, empirical evidence on everyday engineering practice and evaluation in these contexts remains limited. We present findings from an on-site mixed-methods study with 48 software engineers across four countries. The study combines programming, system design, and code review tasks with semi-structured interviews. Our results reveal consistent gaps between measured performance and perceived competence. Senior engineers in Rwanda often performed at a level comparable to that of their European peers, yet European participants systematically underestimated the competence of East African engineers. We also observed differences in communication styles and organizational practices across regions, reflecting distinct but complementary ways of working.

</details>


### [195] [Hierarchical Fault Localization for Autonomous Driving Systems with Hypothesis Validation and Intent Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12598)
*Rui Zheng,Changwen Li,Yi Ji,Rongjie Yan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: HINT is a two-phase framework for hierarchical ADS fault localization that uses hypothesis validation and intent analysis to localize root causes at both module and source code levels, achieving 77.8% Class@5 accuracy on real-world bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ADS testing techniques can detect system-level failures or attribute them to coarse-grained modules, but fail to localize root causes in source code, making debugging labor-intensive for developers.

Method: HINT operates in two phases: Phase I transforms failure-triggering recordings into multi-modal abstractions and uses causal reasoning to identify the responsible module; Phase II reconstructs design-side intent and implementation-side behavior, then localizes suspicious code through reliability-aware consistency checking without costly re-simulation.

Result: Evaluated on Apollo across diverse failure modes and modules, HINT achieves the strongest overall performance across module-level diagnosis and code-level localization metrics, with 77.8% end-to-end Class@5 accuracy on real-world bugs.

Conclusion: HINT effectively bridges the gap between system-level failure detection and source-code-level debugging for ADS by providing hierarchical fault localization through hypothesis validation and intent analysis.

Abstract: Comprehensive testing is essential for the safety and reliability of Autonomous Driving Systems (ADS). Existing techniques can detect system-level failures or attribute them to coarse-grained modules, but they often fall short of localizing the root cause in source code. As a result, debugging remains labor-intensive, requiring developers to connect behavioral violations with complex implementation logic. To address this gap, we present HINT, a two-phase framework for hierarchical ADS fault localization based on hypothesis validation and intent analysis. In Phase I, HINT transforms failure-triggering execution recordings into multi-modal abstractions and uses causal reasoning to identify the responsible module. In Phase II, it reconstructs design-side intent and implementation-side behavior, then localizes suspicious code through reliability-aware consistency checking, without costly re-simulation. We evaluate HINT on Apollo across diverse failure modes and modules. The results show that HINT achieves the strongest overall performance across module-level diagnosis and code-level localization metrics, with 77.8% end-to-end Class@5 accuracy on real-world bugs.

</details>


### [196] [Multi-Perspective Agentic Program Repair via Code Property Graphs and Temporal Execution Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12605)
*Zhili Huang,Ling Xu,Hongyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CT-Repair is an agentic APR framework that uses structured static/dynamic evidence (CPG+TEG) with three finite-state-machine-guided agents and a three-stage filtering pipeline to compact execution traces, achieving 489 repaired bugs on Defects4J v3.0 and outperforming ReinFix and RepairAgent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two limitations exist in LLM-based APR: raw execution traces are too large/repetitive for effective model context, and repeated patch sampling often yields different implementations without distinct root-cause hypotheses or repair strategies.

Method: CT-Repair represents static and dynamic evidence as queryable Code Property Graph (CPG) and Temporal Execution Graph (TEG), applies a three-stage filtering pipeline to construct compact TEGs, and uses three finite-state-machine-guided agents to analyze bugs from static, dynamic, and hybrid perspectives to produce evidence-grounded repair strategies. A strategy-guided generation procedure instantiates these strategies as candidate patches and refines the most promising strategy via validation feedback.

Result: On 854 Java bugs from Defects4J v3.0, CT-Repair correctly repairs 489 bugs in mixed-model config and 388 bugs under controlled GPT-5.4-mini config (19 and 30 more than ReinFix and RepairAgent). The union of three evidence perspectives repairs 99 more bugs than the strongest individual perspective. Execution filtering narrows candidate methods by 94.85% on average; behavior filtering further reduces retained runtime records by 55.97%.

Conclusion: Structured runtime evidence and multi-perspective reasoning improve repair effectiveness without relying solely on larger patch-generation budgets.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have improved automated program repair (APR), but two limitations remain. First, raw execution traces are often too large and repetitive to serve as effective model context. Second, repeated patch sampling may produce different implementations without yielding distinct root-cause hypotheses or repair strategies. We present CT-Repair, an agentic APR framework representing static and dynamic evidence as queryable Code Property Graph (CPG) and Temporal Execution Graph (TEG). CT-Repair applies a three-stage filtering pipeline to construct compact TEGs. Three finite-state-machine-guided agents analyze each bug from static, dynamic, and hybrid perspectives and independently produce evidence-grounded repair strategies. A strategy-guided generation procedure instantiates these strategies as candidate patches and uses validation feedback to refine the most promising strategy.
  We evaluate CT-Repair on 854 Java bugs from Defects4J v3.0. In the mixed-model configuration, CT-Repair correctly repairs 489 bugs. Under a controlled GPT-5.4-mini configuration, it repairs 388 bugs, 19 and 30 more than ReinFix and RepairAgent, respectively. The union of the three evidence perspectives repairs 99 more bugs than the strongest individual perspective. The filtering pipeline also compacts runtime evidence, with execution filtering narrowing the candidate method scope by 94.85% on average and behavior filtering further reducing retained runtime records by 55.97%. These results show that structured runtime evidence and multi-perspective reasoning can improve repair effectiveness without relying solely on a larger patch-generation budget.

</details>


### [197] [Line-Anchored Feedback Cuts Token Costs and Improves Correctness in AI Code Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12713)
*William Franz Lamberti*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Line-anchored feedback (FileMark) reduces token generation cost by 22-58% compared to holistic prompts, while improving correctness by +2 to +7 points for weaker models, especially on larger files.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generated tokens directly drive cost, latency, and energy in GAI code editing; the authors investigate whether the format of feedback can serve as a lever to reduce all three simultaneously.

Method: A paired experiment compares holistic prompts (control) vs. structured, line-anchored FileMark exports (treatment) using a VSCodium extension. Token generation, correctness, and edit-application burden are measured across 7 models on files of varying sizes.

Result: Line anchoring cut generated tokens by 22% (Claude Opus) and 58% (Claude Sonnet), reaching 24-80% on 100+ line files; 4 of 7 models showed significant reduction. Correctness improved +2.0 points pooled, +5-7 points for 3 local models. Removing edit-application burden roughly tripled local-model correctness on large files.

Conclusion: Line-anchored feedback reduces token consumption for stronger models while improving correctness for weaker models, demonstrating the format of feedback as an effective lever on efficiency and accuracy in GAI code editing.

Abstract: Generated tokens are a direct driver of the cost, latency, and energy of generative AI (GAI) code editing. We show the format of feedback is a lever on all three. We compare two deliveries of the same requested changes: a holistic prompt (control) versus the structured, line-anchored export of FileMark (treatment). FileMark is a VSCodium extension for inline comments on any file. In a paired experiment line anchoring cut generated tokens by 22% (Claude Opus) and 58% (Claude Sonnet), reaching 24%-80% on files of 100 lines or more, with four of seven models generating significantly fewer tokens after multiple-testing correction. Correctness rose where models had headroom: +2.0 points pooled and +5 to +7 points for three of five local models. An exploratory experiment in which the harness, not the GAI model, applies function-level patches shows the correctness benefit grows further when the edit-application burden is lifted: local-model correctness on 100+ line files roughly triples under anchoring. Line-anchored feedback reduces what stronger models spend and improves what weaker models get right.

</details>


### [198] [Toward Localizing and Repairing Bias in Transformer Attention Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12863)
*Sigma Jahan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ROBIN is a white-box method that identifies and repairs biased attention heads in transformer LMs by ranking heads via fairness-probe sensitivity and removing a bias subspace from their outputs, achieving better bias reduction and language quality preservation than whole-head zeroing in a pilot study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fairness testing and repair methods operate at input-output or retraining levels, while bias-related behavior can concentrate in a small set of attention heads, yet it's unclear whether these heads can be localized and repaired via targeted inference-time intervention.

Method: ROBIN ranks attention heads using sensitivity to fairness probes and removes a small bias subspace from selected head outputs at inference time.

Result: In a four-model pilot study, ROBIN reduces measured WinoBias gap across all models while preserving language-modeling quality better than whole-head zeroing.

Conclusion: Head-level bias repair should consider not only which heads are selected, but also how selected heads are modified.

Abstract: Transformer language models are increasingly used as software components, yet biased outputs remain difficult to localize and repair inside the model. Existing fairness testing and repair methods largely operate at the input-output or retraining level, while recent work suggests that bias-related behavior can concentrate in a small set of attention heads. This paper studies whether attention heads can be localized and repaired through a targeted inference-time intervention. We introduce ROBIN, a white-box head-level fairness debugging method that ranks attention heads using sensitivity to fairness probes and removes a small bias subspace from selected head outputs. In a four-model pilot study, ROBIN reduces the measured WinoBias gap across all models while preserving language-modeling quality better than whole-head zeroing. These preliminary results suggest that head-level bias repair should consider not only which heads are selected, but also how selected heads are modified.

</details>


### [199] [Deep4ge: DNN Training Trajectories for Fault Detection and Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12868)
*Sigma Jahan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Deep4ge is a benchmark of 14,227 training runs (9,845 faulty, 4,382 correct) from 59 TensorFlow/Keras DNN programs, using 27 source-code transformations across 7 fault categories, with 26 per-epoch features and 4 evaluation metrics, supporting fault detection, diagnosis, and early prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The software engineering community lacks a public, reusable dataset of per-epoch training runs with documented fault history and feature extraction details for deep learning fault detection and diagnosis.

Method: Collected 59 TensorFlow/Keras DNN programs from Stack Overflow, applied 27 source-code transformations covering 7 fault categories to generate faulty variants, and recorded 26 per-epoch features (weights, gradients, activations, accuracy/loss trends, learning rate, hardware use) plus 4 evaluation metrics across 14,227 training runs.

Result: Created a benchmark of 14,227 training runs (9,845 faulty, 4,382 correct) with per-epoch monitoring data, supporting binary fault detection, multi-class fault diagnosis, and early fault prediction from partial runs.

Conclusion: Deep4ge provides a standardized, reusable benchmark for the software engineering community to evaluate fault detection and diagnosis methods for deep learning systems, with publicly released data and fault-injection framework.

Abstract: Deep learning systems often fail due to subtle implementation faults that alter training behavior. Recent work has studied how to detect and diagnose such failures from changes observed across training epochs. However, the software engineering community still lacks a public dataset of per-epoch training runs with documented fault history, feature extraction details, and clear reuse support for fault detection and diagnosis tasks. We present Deep4ge, a controlled benchmark of 14,227 training runs generated from 59 adapted TensorFlow/Keras deep neural network (DNN) programs collected from Stack Overflow. We generated faulty variants using 27 source-code transformations that introduce known faults across seven categories. The dataset contains 9,845 faulty runs and 4,382 correct baseline runs. For each run, we record 4 evaluation metrics and 26 features that measure training behavior at every epoch. These features capture weights, gradients, activations, accuracy and loss trends, learning rate, and hardware use. Deep4ge supports binary fault detection, multi-class fault diagnosis, and early fault prediction from partial training runs. We release the dataset and fault-injection framework at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20337241.

</details>


### [200] [Salesforce Messaging Architecture: Platform Events, Async Sends, and Multi-Tenancy at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12943)
*Devam Gupta*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes building messaging integrations natively inside CRM platforms (instead of using external connectors), treating messages as CRM records, using native transactions, and storing all state as database objects. It evaluates this architecture using a production package deployed across healthcare, sales, field services, and support organizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External messaging connectors create data isolation between messaging platforms and CRM systems, relying on fragile sync jobs, rigid field mappings, and eventual consistency. This leads to unreliable integration, message/record separation, and limited automation capability.

Method: The paper presents an architectural pattern where the entire messaging core is built natively inside the CRM.
