At the strict 0 percentage point threshold on a 5 percentage point budget grid, AppWorld first meets all targets at 15 percent, tau-bench at 25 percent, and SWE-bench Verified at 90 percent; SWE-bench Lite does not meet all targets by 95 percent under the primary coverage rule. Partial-evaluation reports should state how much one agent must outperform another, how tasks are selected, what coverage rule is required, what decision rule is used, and how many comparisons may remain unresolved.

</details>


### [225] [PM-Bench: Evaluating Prospective Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12385)
*Genglin Liu,Saadia Gabriel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PM-Bench, a text-based benchmark inspired by cognitive science to evaluate prospective memory in LLM agents, finding that even the best method (GPT-5.4) only achieves 65.1% F1 score, with no single improvement strategy dominating across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the significant challenge of prospective memory in agentic AI—the ability to execute intentions at specific future cues while handling other ongoing activities.

Method: PM-Bench is a text-based benchmark inspired by the Virtual Week paradigm from cognitive science, simulating a seven-day week where agents must continue ongoing activities while deciding whether any deferred task is due. Eight state-of-the-art LLMs were compared under eight different agent configurations.

Result: PM-Bench is challenging across all settings; the best method (GPT-5.4 agent) reaches only 65.1% F1 score, and no single strategy for improving prospective memory dominates across models.

Conclusion: PM-Bench serves as a controlled testbed for diagnosing prospective memory failures in LLM agents and developing training or inference-time interventions for reliable prospective behavior.

Abstract: A significant challenge in agentic AI is prospective memory: the ability to execute an intention at a specific future cue or state while other activities are ongoing. We introduce PM-Bench, a text-based benchmark for measuring prospective memory capabilities in modern LLM agents. Inspired by the Virtual Week paradigm from cognitive science, PM-Bench evaluates how well LLM agents maintain user intentions, execute delayed intentions, and monitor latent environment changes. Over the course of a simulated seven-day week, agents must continue an ongoing activity while deciding whether any deferred task is due. We compare eight state-of-the-art LLMs on PM-Bench under eight different agent configurations. PM-Bench proves challenging across all settings: the best method, a GPT-5.4 agent, reaches only 65.1\% F1 score under our evaluation. Furthermore, no single strategy for improving prospective memory dominates across models. We release PM-Bench as a controlled testbed for diagnosing these failures and developing training or inference-time interventions that support reliable prospective behavior.

</details>


### [226] [Critic Experience Bank: Self-Evolving Step-Level Confidence Estimation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12397)
*Yaopei Zeng,Congchao Wang,JianHang Chen,Nan Wang,Yurui Chang,Lu Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces EVIDENCE, a self-evolving critic framework for step-level confidence estimation in LLM agents. It uses a hindsight LLM to generate pseudo-labels from past trajectories, stores them in a memory bank, and retrieves similar experiences to improve confidence calibration without training or ground truth labels, achieving best ECE, Brier, and AUC across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents acting in external environments need step-level confidence estimation before executing actions to avoid wasting budget or triggering irreversible side effects. Existing confidence estimators only score responses from prompts and ignore execution consequences (whether similar actions in similar situations actually advanced the task).

Method: EVIDENCE is a self-evolving critic framework: (1) After each trajectory, a hindsight LLM with full execution feedback votes on each step's productivity to generate pseudo-labels. (2) These pseudo-labels populate a memory bank. (3) When a similar step recurs, related productive and unproductive experiences are retrieved into the critic's prompt. No training or ground truth step labels required.

Result: Across three agent benchmarks and three critic backbones, EVIDENCE attains the best calibration (ECE and Brier) and ranking (AUC) in every dataset-critic combination, reducing ECE by up to 54% relative to the strongest training-free baseline.

Conclusion: EVIDENCE demonstrates that leveraging accumulated evidence from past judgments and their observed consequences significantly improves step-level confidence estimation for LLM agents, without requiring training or ground truth labels.

Abstract: LLM agents act in external environments where each action changes the state that later decisions condition on, and where a single wrong step can waste interaction budget or trigger irreversible side effects long before the final failure is observed. Reliable deployment therefore requires \emph{step-level confidence estimation}: a calibrated probability that each proposed action is productive, available \emph{before} the action is executed. Existing LLM confidence estimators are designed to score a response from the given prompt, but agent confidence also depends on execution consequences: whether similar actions in similar situations actually advanced the task after the environment responded. We introduce the \method (\methodshort), a self-evolving critic framework in which an LLM critic accumulates evidence from its own past judgments and their observed consequences. After each trajectory, a hindsight LLM that sees the full execution feedback votes on whether each step was productive. The resulting pseudo-labels populate a memory bank from which related productive and unproductive experiences are retrieved into the critic's prompt whenever a similar step recurs. \methodshort requires no training and uses no ground truth step labels. Across three agent benchmarks and three critic backbones, \methodshort attains the best calibration (ECE and Brier) and ranking (AUC) in every dataset--critic combination, reducing ECE by up to $54\%$ relative to the strongest training-free baseline.

</details>


### [227] [Isolation as a First-Class Principle for LLM-Agent System Safety: Concepts, Taxonomy, Challenges and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12406)
*Huihao Jing,Wenbin Hu,Shaojin Chen,Haochen Shi,Sirui Zhang,Hanyu Yang,Changxuan Fan,Zhongwei Xie,Hongyu Luo,Wun Yu Chan,Wei Fan,Haoran Li,Yangqiu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey proposes isolation as a first-class principle for LLM-agent system safety, organizing agent failures through a boundary-centric taxonomy of five boundaries (user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, system-environment) to explain how failures like prompt injection, tool misuse, and memory poisoning share structural causes and propagate across agent workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current literature on LLM agent safety is fragmented across attack types, applications, and benchmarks, making it difficult to explain why different failures (e.g., prompt injection, tool misuse, memory poisoning) share the same structural cause and how they spread through agent workflows. A unified framework is needed to systematically understand and address these vulnerabilities.

Method: The paper organizes the literature using a boundary-centric taxonomy of five distinct boundaries: user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, and system-environment. It treats isolation (separation of user inputs, tool access, execution channels, inter-agent communication, and environment-originated context) as a first-class principle for LLM-agent system safety.

Result: The taxonomy helps identify where loss of isolation first occurs, how compromise propagates across boundaries, and which defenses are most relevant at each interface. The paper also summarizes cross-boundary failure paths and outlines open challenges.

Conclusion: The paper calls for an "isolation-by-construction" research agenda for future agent systems, emphasizing that safety in LLM agents extends beyond input-output content alignment to encompass system behavior and real-world execution outcomes.

Abstract: The capability of LLM agents to function as the ``brain'' of a system fundamentally expands the scope of analysis beyond a standalone model. Consequently, safety is no longer only about input--output content alignment. It also concerns system behavior and real-world execution outcomes. However, the current literature is fragmented across attack types, applications, and benchmarks. This makes it hard to explain why failures such as prompt injection, tool misuse, and memory poisoning often share the same structural cause, and how they spread through an agent workflow. In this survey, we treat isolation as a first-class principle for LLM-agent system safety. By isolation, we refer to the separation of user inputs, tool access, execution channels, inter-agent communication, and environment-originated context. We organize the literature with a boundary-centric taxonomy of five boundaries: user-agent, agent-tool, agent-execution, agent-agent, and system-environment. This view helps identify where the loss of isolation first occurs, how compromise propagates across boundaries, and which defenses are most relevant at each interface. We also summarize cross-boundary failure paths, discuss open challenges, and outline a research agenda for isolation-by-construction in future agent systems.

</details>


### [228] [Accepted Prefixes Are Not All You Need: A Negative Result on PEFT-Based Block-Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12422)
*Abdurrahman Javat,Allan Kazakov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PEFT-BD uses a LoRA-like adapter as a block-diffusion drafter for same-backbone speculative decoding. While it offers parameter efficiency and avoids tokenizer mismatch, it fails to accelerate inference because the drafter requires a full-backbone pass (same cost as verifier), violating the key condition that the drafter must be substantially cheaper than the verifier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve draft quality while reducing auxiliary parameters and system overhead in speculative decoding, motivating a same-backbone approach with parameter-efficient adapters.

Method: PEFT-BD: a LoRA-like adapter serving as a block-diffusion drafter (BD3LM-style denoising objective) for an autoregressive verifier, sharing the same backbone.

Result: No practical speedup on Qwen3-0.6B. Despite nontrivial accepted prefix lengths, each speculative step requires an adapter-enabled full-backbone draft pass plus an adapter-disabled full-backbone verification pass.

Conclusion: Parameter efficiency does not guarantee compute efficiency. The drafter must be substantially cheaper to execute than the verifier; longer accepted prefixes cannot compensate when draft computation is verifier-scale.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive language model inference by using a cheap drafter to propose multiple future tokens and a target model to verify them. A common design goal is therefore to improve draft quality while reducing auxiliary parameters and systems overhead. We study a negative result for this direction through PEFT-BD, a same-backbone speculative decoding method in which a LoRA-like adapter acts as a block-diffusion drafter for an autoregressive verifier. PEFT-BD is motivated by several attractive properties: it avoids tokenizer mismatch, avoids loading a separate draft model, adds only a small number of trainable parameters, and uses a BD3LM-style denoising objective to propose a block of tokens in parallel. Despite these advantages, PEFT-BD does not yield a practical speedup in our Qwen3-0.6B experiments. Although the method obtains nontrivial accepted prefixes, profiling shows that each speculative step requires an adapter-enabled full-backbone draft pass followed by an adapter-disabled full-backbone verification pass. Thus, the drafter is parameter-efficient but not compute-efficient. Our results isolate a simple but important condition for successful speculative decoding: the drafter must be substantially cheaper to execute than the verifier. Longer accepted prefixes alone cannot compensate when draft computation remains verifier-scale.

</details>


### [229] [EVOQUANT: Self-Evolving Verifier-Guided Strategy Optimization for Robust Quantitative Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12455)
*Jie Mao,Changlun Li,Xiang Li,Qiqi Duan,Jinhui Yuan,Xiang Liu,Yuyu Luo,Jing Tang,Xiaowen Chu,Nan Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EVOQUANT is a self-evolving, verifier-guided framework that uses LLMs to automate quantitative strategy optimization, achieving significant Sharpe ratio improvements (average -0.298 to 0.538) across A-share and Crypto market strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative strategy optimization is still largely manual and costly, requiring domain experts to identify weak signals, tune risk-control rules, and iteratively validate revisions. While LLMs could accelerate this, directly using them to rewrite strategies introduces hallucinated edits, strategy drift, and backtest overfitting.

Method: EVOQUANT uses LLMs to diagnose performance bottlenecks, generates semantically controlled candidate edits, selects the best strategy via a multi-stage verification pipeline, and distills optimization experience into reusable knowledge for continual self-improvement.

Result: Evaluated on seven strategies (four A-share, three Crypto). Average test Sharpe improved from -0.298 to 0.538; best strategy achieved 199% relative improvement. Ablation and stress tests confirmed robustness.

Conclusion: Transforms quantitative strategy optimization from costly manual trial and error into an automated, verifiable iterative paradigm, offering a new path for applying LLMs to financial strategy research.

Abstract: Quantitative strategy optimization remains largely manual, requiring domain experts to identify weak signals, tune risk-control rules, and repeatedly validate iterative revisions. Large language models can accelerate this process, but directly relying on them to rewrite trading strategies often introduces hallucinated edits, strategy drift, and backtest overfitting. We propose EVOQUANT, a self-Evolving Verifier-guided framework for strategy Optimization in Quantitative trading. Our method utilizes LLMs to deeply diagnose performance bottlenecks, generates semantically controlled candidate edits, selects the best strategy through a multi-stage verification pipeline, and distills optimization experience into reusable knowledge for continual self-improvement. We evaluate our method using seven representative strategies: four from the A-share market and three from the Crypto market. Experimental results show that our method significantly improves the Sharpe ratio across all tested strategies: the average test Sharpe increases from -0.298 to 0.538, and the best-performing strategy achieves a 199% relative improvement. Ablation studies and stress tests under stricter conditions further validate the effectiveness and robustness of the framework. Overall, this work transforms quantitative strategy optimization from costly manual trial and error into an automated and verifiable iterative paradigm, offering a new path for applying large language models to financial strategy research.

</details>


### [230] [Do We Really Need Transformers for Global Spatial Information Extraction in Traffic Forecasting?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12462)
*Qihang Zhang,Siyao Zhang,Letao Kang,Wenzhe Liang,Miao Zhang,Zhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether global spatial information in traffic forecasting requires complex attention mechanisms or can be captured by simple global aggregation. Through controlled ablation experiments across six benchmarks, uniform full-range mixing and standard spatial attention perform similarly (0.14% MAE difference), while the former reduces complexity from O(N²) to O(N). Mechanism analysis decomposes spatial attention into a row-uniform global background and non-uniform residual, finding the residual provides marginal, dataset-dependent value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The underlying mechanism of global spatial information modeling in traffic forecasting remains insufficiently investigated, and it is unclear whether such information must be extracted by high-degree-of-freedom adaptive attention or can be captured by a simple global aggregation operator.

Method: Designed a controlled ablation framework that replaces only the spatial mixing module to compare attention-based global interaction with uniform full-range mixing. Decomposed spatial attention into a row-uniform global background and non-uniform residual for mechanism analysis.

Result: Uniform full-range mixing and standard spatial attention each achieve lower MAE on three out of six datasets, with only a 0.14% difference in mean MAE. The uniform method reduces node-scale spatial mixing complexity from O(N²) to O(N). The non-uniform residual in spatial attention shows dataset-dependent marginal value.

Conclusion: Spatial attention should be justified by stable gains beyond a row-uniform global background; the residual component's value is dataset-dependent and marginal overall.

Abstract: Existing traffic forecasting models commonly focus on extracting spatial dependencies, particularly global spatial information, which characterizes the representations obtained through interactions between each individual node and all nodes across the traffic network. However, the underlying mechanism by which such global information is modeled and extracted remains insufficiently investigated. Whether global information must be extracted by high-degree-of-freedom adaptive attention or can be captured by a simple global aggregation operator remains unclear. For this purpose, we design a controlled ablation framework that replaces only the spatial mixing module to test attention-based global interaction. Across six traffic benchmarks, uniform full-range mixing and standard spatial attention each achieve lower MAE on three datasets, with only a 0.14% difference in mean MAE, while the former reduces node-scale spatial mixing complexity from O(N2) to O(N). Mechanism analysis further decomposes spatial attention into a row-uniform global background and a non-uniform residual. The residual shows dataset-dependent marginal value, suggesting that spatial attention should be justified by stable gains beyond a row-uniform global background. The corresponding source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/uuesti/U-Trans

</details>


### [231] [Function-Aware Fill-in-the-Middle as Mid-Training for Coding Agent Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12463)
*Yubo Wang,Jiarong Liang,Yuxuan Zhang,Xuye Liu,Cong Wei,Yuyu Zhang,Ping Nie,Wenhu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training on Python code improves coding agent performance on SWE-Bench by learning the caller-callee conditioning structure of function calls, transferring to non-coding tool-use tasks and mitigating capability erosion from agentic post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents must integrate external tool returns into ongoing reasoning, but standard left-to-right pretraining on code only exposes forward-direction conditioning. The paper identifies that the action-observation-continuation loop is isomorphic to a function call site (caller binds arguments → callee returns value → downstream code consumes it), and exploits this structure at internet scale via self-supervised learning.

Method: Function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training: a self-supervised objective masking functions selected via program dependency graph analysis and a complexity-inferability double criterion. Mid-trained Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B/14B) and Qwen3-8B on a 2.6B-token decontaminated corpus from 968 GitHub repositories, followed by existing agentic post-training pipelines.

Result: SWE-Bench-Verified improvements: +2.8/+3.0 (7B/14B) and +3.2 (Qwen3-8B); SWE-Bench-Lite gains: +3.7/+4.0/+5.4. Improvements hold across two post-training pipelines (R2E-Gym, SWE-Smith) and non-Qwen2.5 base. Mid-training also mitigates capability erosion from agentic post-training on non-agent coding (LiveCodeBench) and non-coding tool-use (tau-bench, BFCL).

Conclusion: Function-aware FIM mid-training injects a useful inductive bias about caller-callee conditioning that persists through agentic post-training. Even though the corpus is Python-only, the function-call inductive bias transfers to non-coding tool-use benchmarks and preserves non-agent coding capabilities otherwise eroded by agentic fine-tuning.

Abstract: Coding agents must integrate external tool returns into ongoing reasoning - a capability that standard left-to-right pretraining on code exposes only in its forward direction. We observe that the action-observation-continuation loop of a coding agent is structurally isomorphic to a function call site, where a caller binds arguments, a callee returns a value computed elsewhere, and downstream code consumes that value. This conditioning structure exists at internet scale in ordinary code. We exploit it through function-aware fill-in-the-middle (FIM) mid-training: a self-supervised objective that masks functions selected via program dependency graph analysis and a complexity-inferability double criterion. We mid-train Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B/14B) and Qwen3-8B on a 2.6B-token decontaminated corpus drawn from 968 GitHub repositories, then apply existing agentic post-training pipelines. Mid-training improves SWE-Bench-Verified by +2.8/+3.0 at 7B/14B and by +3.2 on Qwen3-8B; SWE-Bench-Lite gains are +3.7/+4.0/+5.4 on the same models. The improvement holds across two post-training pipelines (R2E-Gym, SWE-Smith) and on a non-Qwen2.5 base (Qwen3-8B with SWE-Lego). Beyond in-domain gains, mid-training also mitigates the capability erosion that agentic post-training otherwise inflicts on non-agent coding (e.g., LiveCodeBench) and non-coding tool-use benchmarks (tau-bench, BFCL): although the mid-training corpus contains Python code only, the function-call inductive bias survives post-training and yields consistent gains.

</details>


### [232] [From Observation to Insight: Mechanistic World Models and the Quest for Autonomous Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12474)
*Ingmar Posner,Anson Lei,Bernhard Schölkopf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that scientific discovery requires knowledge organization around reusable explanatory mechanisms, not just predictive mappings. It introduces Mechanistic World Models as a paradigm for mechanism-centric representation, computation, and learning, outlining design principles and computational requirements for autonomous scientific discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI excels at prediction but fails at scientific discovery because it focuses on predictive mappings rather than uncovering reusable explanatory mechanisms that generate observations. The paper seeks to bridge this gap by reorienting AI toward mechanistic understanding.

Method: The authors draw on philosophy of science to derive computational requirements for discovery, identify design principles and inductive pressures for explanatory knowledge, formalize the anatomy of mechanism-centric world models, and synthesize insights from mechanistic interpretability, causal representation learning, equation discovery, and modular architectures.

Result: A unified conceptual framework—Mechanistic World Models—that integrates complementary ingredients from diverse research directions while highlighting their current lack of a unified foundation.

Conclusion: Mechanistic World Models provide a conceptual foundation and computational blueprint for moving AI beyond predictive forecasting toward autonomous scientific discovery by centering reusable mechanisms in representation, computation, and learning.

Abstract: Recent advances in foundation models have transformed AI for Science, enabling remarkably accurate predictive performance across domains ranging from protein folding to weather forecasting. Yet prediction alone does not constitute scientific discovery. Scientific understanding depends on uncovering the reusable explanatory mechanisms that generate observations, whereas contemporary machine learning remains fundamentally organised around predictive mappings rather than explanatory structure. In this paper, we argue that scientific discovery is fundamentally a problem of knowledge organisation. To this end, we introduce Mechanistic World Models, a new design paradigm that places reusable mechanisms at the centre of representation, computation and learning. Drawing on insights from the philosophy of science, we derive the computational capabilities required for discovery, identify the design principles and inductive pressures that encourage explanatory knowledge to emerge, and formalise the anatomy of a mechanism-centric world model. Finally, we show how diverse research directions including mechanistic interpretability, causal representation learning, equation discovery and modular architectures capture complementary ingredients of this paradigm while lacking a unified framework. We propose Mechanistic World Models as a conceptual foundation and computational blueprint for moving AI beyond predictive forecasting towards autonomous scientific discovery.

</details>


### [233] [TRACE: An Operational Reasoning Schema for Auditable Agentic Commitments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12480)
*Edward Y. Chang,Emily J. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a typed, versioned schema for recording reasoning traces with a discipline of no durable state change without a record, arguing that reasoning is not inherent in language models and providing formal structures to bridge that gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning is absent as formal machinery in language models (autoregressive association, chain-of-thought/RL inherit limits); we need a schema to externalize, audit, and gate decisions based on explicit reasoning records.

Method: Defines TraceRecord schema with causal specialization, eight-stage reference writer, gate-first measurement regime, TRACE-Bench protocol, and consumer mechanisms (memory admission, plan gating, temporal regret, verdict reuse) under a record-consumer contract.

Result: Two illustrative examples: a music-lessons argument traced through association/intervention/prescription; a flood search-and-rescue vignette where world model confidence is contradicted by its own scores, leading to deferred commitment, append-only revision, and branch clearing.

Conclusion: Contribution is the schema and its contractual interface, not empirical performance; closed-loop evaluation is left to future work.

Abstract: This paper defines TRACE (Typed Reasoning And Commitment Evidence): a typed, versioned schema for recording reasoning traces, a reference procedure for writing records against it, and one operating discipline, no durable state change without a record. The paper argues in three layers that reasoning is not in the language model: the autoregressive mechanism natively computes association; chain-of-thought and reinforcement learning inherit its limits; and the formal constructs of reasoning theory, from Socratic procedure to Pearl's ladder, are absent as machinery. The schema answers the absence with fields and tests: the TraceRecord and its causal specialization, an eight-stage reference writer, a gate-first measurement regime, the TRACE-Bench protocol, and the consumers, memory admission, plan gating, temporal regret, and verdict reuse, whose more auditable decisions are the measure of the record. A record-consumer contract states what a record guarantees and what a consumer must honor in return, making the schema an operational interface rather than a passive document. Two worked examples run in the main text: a music-lessons argument traced from sentence to typed verdict, separating association, intervention, and prescription; and a flood search-and-rescue vignette in which a predictive world model reports confident plan success that its own support and out-of-distribution scores contradict, so the record defers the commitment, requests a bounded observation, revises append-only, and clears a different branch. The vignette is illustrative, not empirical; closed-loop evaluation is left to future work, so the contribution is the schema and its contract, not a performance claim. Appendices carry the full schema, writer algorithms and cost model, clinical and policy illustrations, the benchmark protocol, convergence metrics, and usage scenarios.

</details>


### [234] [The Model Knows Your Project, Not You: Measuring Recognition in LLMs with NameRank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12520)
*Bojie Li,Noah Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NameRank measures how well frontier models recognize specific entities (people, tools, artifacts) from parametric memory. Testing 4,685 entities across 36 models, it finds recognition attaches to named artifacts (methods, papers, tools) rather than credentials, titles, or authorship rosters. Olympic credentials rank below working researchers, but Nobel/Turing/Fields laureates saturate the panel. Tools outrank their creators, and artifact rosters score near zero. Bibliometrics don't predict; institutional density and peak news salience matter more.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Citations explain only ~1/3 of why a model recognizes a researcher. The paper aims to understand what drives the residual — what makes an entity "knowable" from a model's parametric weights alone, independent of retrieval or context.

Method: NameRank: a [0,1] recognition score across 4,685 entities in 54 cohorts, probed with one open-ended question per entity across 36 models. A binary verdict from an independent judge checks if the model stated a specific, non-guessable fact about the entity. Synthetic-null entities establish a floor. Probes include credentials, artifacts, authorship, bibliometrics, institutional density, and news salience.

Result: Recognition is paid to named, indexable artifacts, not credentials. Olympic credentials sit below working-researcher baseline; Nobel/Turing/Fields laureates saturate. Tools out-rank creators. Named contributors to celebrated artifacts (e.g., model report authors) earn almost nothing. No bibliometric predicts well; top-density institutions outperform peers; recognition loads on peak news salience, not persistence.

Conclusion: Parametric recognition tracks named artifacts and peak salience, not credentials or authorship credit. A self-report probe shows models read a corpus prior, not their own knowledge, suggesting introspection is unreliable as ground truth.

Abstract: What a frontier model recalls about a person or tool from its own weights -- before any retrieval step -- often shapes the first description a human sees, making that parametric corpus presence a measurement problem. Citations explain about a third of whether a model recognizes a researcher; we target the residual and build NameRank, a [0,1] recognition score: each of 4,685 entities in 54 cohorts is probed with one open-ended question across 36 models, and an independent judge returns a binary verdict against a curated gold -- did the model state a specific, non-guessable fact about this exact entity? -- so hallucination, context echo, and guesses earn nothing. Synthetic-null entities hold the floor near zero, and verdicts track the entity, not the model. One thesis organizes the findings: recognition is paid to named, indexable artifacts, not to credentials or titles. Every Olympic-style credential sits below a working-researcher baseline, because no named artifact ships with the medal, yet the ranking inverts at the marquee tier, where Nobel, Turing, and Fields laureates saturate the panel. For independent creators the tool out-ranks its maker, and the credential that does propagate is a named method or awarded paper. Being one of many named contributors to a celebrated artifact, by contrast, earns almost nothing -- the authors listed on a flagship model report or system card sit near the recognition floor -- because recognition attaches to the artifact's own distinctive name, not to the roster behind it. No bibliometric predicts recognition well; top-density institutions out-recognize peers at matched citations; and on 258 news events recognition loads on peak salience, not persistence. A self-report probe shows introspection reads a corpus prior, not its own knowledge.

</details>


### [235] [Evidence-Grounded AI for Musculoskeletal Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12527)
*Wenjie Li,Yujie Zhang,Fanrui Zhang,Haoran Sun,Renhao Yang,Junjun He,Weiran Huang,Yuanfeng Ji,Chenrun Wang,Kailing Wang,Hongcheng Gao,Kaipeng Zhang,Hanyu Wang,Angela Lin Wang,Xingqi He,Yilin Huang,Shiyi Yao,Lilong Wang,Yankai Jiang,Yirong Chen,Chenglong Ma,Jiyao Liu,Ming Hu,Gen Li,Yidong Xu,Chengyu Zhuang,Jiawei Liu,Yin Zhang,Lequan Yu,Lu Chen,Yinpeng Dong,Lei Liu,Carlos Gutierrez Sanroman,Yu Qiao,Weijie Ma,Xiaosong Wang,Lei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OrthoPilot, a clinical AI system powered by a large language model, integrates hospital data with external knowledge to support continuous musculoskeletal management. It outperformed 81 orthopaedic physicians and multiple other intelligent systems across diagnostic reasoning, decision-making, and management planning, improved full-chain management success by 10.6% in a prospective study of 1,870 cases, and increased cumulative cases per bed by 9.7% in an 8-month deployment involving 8,240 inpatients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Musculoskeletal diseases require longitudinal management, but patient evidence is fragmented across visits, departments, and hospital systems, limiting individualized, evidence-based care.

Method: OrthoPilot is an LLM-powered clinical AI that autonomously retrieves real-time imaging, laboratory, pathology, and order data, converting evolving patient states into evidence-based decisions from admission to rehabilitation. It was evaluated via a specialist-validated benchmark (1,000 disease codes), a reader study against 81 orthopaedic physicians, external validation across 60 clinical centres, a prospective study (1,870 complex cases), and an 8-month randomised deployment (8,240 inpatients).

Result: OrthoPilot surpassed experts with 25 years of experience in diagnostic reasoning, clinical decision-making, and management planning; outperformed all evaluated intelligent systems across 60 external clinical centres; increased full-chain management success by 10.6%; increased cumulative cases per bed by 9.7%; and improved patient-reported access to health information.

Conclusion: These results move clinical AI from predicting isolated events toward executing longitudinal management across complete musculoskeletal care pathways.

Abstract: Musculoskeletal diseases are among the leading causes of disability worldwide and create the greatest global need for rehabilitation. Because recovery, remodelling and degeneration often unfold over months to years, musculoskeletal care requires longitudinal management that repeatedly integrates evolving patient evidence, external medical knowledge and stage-specific functional goals. In routine practice, this evidence is fragmented across visits, departments and hospital systems, limiting individualized, evidence-based care. Here we report OrthoPilot, a clinical artificial intelligence system powered by a large language model that integrates hospital data streams with authoritative external knowledge for continuous musculoskeletal management. OrthoPilot autonomously retrieves real-time imaging, laboratory, pathology and order data and converts evolving patient states into evidence-based decisions from admission diagnosis to rehabilitation planning. We established a specialist-validated benchmark from real-world electronic health records spanning 1,000 disease codes. In a reader study across the complete care pathway, OrthoPilot was compared with 81 orthopaedic physicians and surpassed experts with 25 years of experience in diagnostic reasoning, clinical decision-making and management planning. It also outperformed all evaluated intelligent systems across 60 external clinical centres. In a prospective study of 1,870 complex cases, OrthoPilot increased full-chain management success by 10.6%. During an 8-month randomised deployment involving 8,240 inpatients, it increased cumulative cases per bed by 9.7% and improved patient-reported access to health information. These results move clinical AI from predicting isolated events toward executing longitudinal management across complete musculoskeletal care pathways.

</details>


### [236] [Vertical Standardisation for High-Risk AI Systems under the EU AI Act: A Domain-Specific Framework for Algorithmic Hiring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12588)
*Anna Gatzioura,Vrettos Moulos,Nina Baranowska*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes domain-specific standardisation recommendations for AI-based recruitment systems to comply with the EU AI Act, focusing on lifecycle discrimination risks, fairness-aware data governance, explainability, human oversight, and post-deployment monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The European AI Act mandates compliance for high-risk AI systems (including algorithmic hiring), but no European AI standards fully cover the challenges of algorithmic hiring yet, necessitating concrete, domain-specific standardisation recommendations.

Method: The authors map the AI Act's requirements (risk management, data quality, logging, transparency, human oversight, accuracy) to standardisation recommendations for ranking-based recruitment systems, drawing from the FINDHR project but keeping recommendations independent of its technical artefacts.

Result: A vertical, domain-specific framework for algorithmic hiring standardisation is produced, covering lifecycle discrimination risks, fairness-aware data governance, explainability, human oversight, and post-deployment monitoring.

Conclusion: Unlike existing horizontal approaches, this paper contributes a concrete, domain-specific standardisation pathway for AI recruitment systems to align with the EU AI Act, applicable beyond the FINDHR project's specific tools.

Abstract: According to the recent European legislation, high-risk AI systems will have to adapt in order to comply with requirements related to specific areas, like risk management, data quality and governance, logging and traceability, technical documentation, transparency, human oversight, and accuracy, as outlined in the European Artificial Intelligence (AI) Act. As the standardisation process for AI is expected to remain iterative and, so far, there are no European standards on AI fully covering the challenges of algorithmic hiring, we propose specific standardisation-oriented recommendations related to the relevant AI areas specified by the European Commission. For each of these areas, we set the context by describing the requirements that AI systems in high-risk domains, and especially in recruitment, should fulfil, as well as the activities that should be carried out to ensure their appropriate use and desired performance, in line with the requirements deriving from the AI Act. Unlike existing horizontal approaches to AI governance and standardisation, this paper contributes a vertical, domain-specific framework for algorithmic hiring, and especially ranking-based recruitment systems, by mapping the requirements of the AI Act to concrete standardisation recommendations, focusing on lifecycle discrimination risks, fairness-aware data governance, explainability, human oversight, and post-deployment monitoring in recruitment systems. Even though our recommendations were informed by the outcomes of the European project FINDHR, they are not tied to the project's technical artefacts and could be implemented using alternative methods, tools, or governance mechanisms.

</details>


### [237] [Agentic Service-Oriented Computing: A Manifesto for the Next Frontier of Service-Oriented Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12619)
*Amin Beheshti,Rong N. Chang,Boualem Benatallah,Fabio Casati,Schahram Dustdar,Geoffrey Fox,Quan Z. Sheng,Yan Wang,Jian Yang,Albert Zomaya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Agentic Service-Oriented Computing (ASOC) as a research area that applies established Service-Oriented Computing principles—composition, interoperability, governance, security, trust—to engineering LLM-powered autonomous agents as dependable, service-based systems, articulating six foundational principles and a five-dimensional research agenda.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered agents are being built ad hoc without the engineering rigor required for dependable enterprise and societal deployment, despite facing challenges (composition, interoperability, QoS, governance, security, trust) that the Service-Oriented Computing community has studied for over two decades.

Method: The paper proposes ASOC as a framework: engineering agents as services, orchestrating services through agents, and governing agent-service ecosystems. It defines six foundational principles and organizes a five-dimensional research agenda.

Result: Articulation of six foundational principles (harness-ability, composability, lifecycle engineering, trustworthiness by design, goal-driven orchestration, observability/accountability) and a five-dimensional research agenda spanning agentic services foundations, composition/orchestration, governance/observability, security/trust, and evaluation/certification/Agentic QoS.

Conclusion: The Services Computing community is well-positioned to provide the conceptual and engineering spine for ASOC, transforming agentic AI from fragmented demonstrations into dependable, service-based systems worthy of trust.

Abstract: The rapid emergence of LLM-powered autonomous and semi-autonomous agents is reshaping software systems from static, request-response components into goal-directed, adaptive, and tool-using computational actors. As these agents move from isolated cognitive prototypes into complex distributed workflows, they confront challenges that the Service-Oriented Computing community has studied for more than two decades: composition, interoperability, quality of service, lifecycle management, governance, security, and trust. Yet much of today's agentic AI ecosystem is developing these foundations ad hoc, without the engineering rigour required for dependable enterprise and societal deployment. This paper introduces Agentic Service-Oriented Computing (ASOC) as a new research and practice area concerned with engineering agents as services, orchestrating services through autonomous and semi-autonomous agents, and governing ecosystems of agents and services under constraints of trust, cybersecurity, compliance, performance, and accountability. We articulate six foundational principles of ASOC (harness-ability, composability, lifecycle engineering, trustworthiness by design, goal-driven orchestration, and observability/accountability) and organise a five-dimensional research agenda spanning: (i) agentic services foundations and lifecycle engineering; (ii) composition, orchestration, and interoperability; (iii) governance, observability, and accountability; (iv) security, trust, and risk management; and (v) evaluation, certification, and Agentic QoS. We argue that the Services Computing community is especially well positioned to provide the conceptual and engineering spine for this emerging field, transforming agentic AI from fragmented demonstrations into dependable, service-based systems worthy of human and organisational trust.

</details>


### [238] [Atomic Units of X: The Compression Layer of Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12634)
*Sachin Dev Duggal,Pradyumna Swarnalatha Ramanna,Alexandros Vassiliades*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a theoretical framework (Compression Calculus) where intelligence is understood as compression through atomic decomposition and compositional reuse across abstraction layers, arguing that current AI systems operate at suboptimal representation levels and should move toward concept-level atomic structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unifying theoretical framework that explains how scalable intelligence emerges across cognitive, biological, computational, and organizational systems through atomic compression and compositional reuse, and to address the suboptimal representation levels in current AI systems.

Method: The paper draws on evidence from multiple disciplines (cognitive science, information theory, evolutionary biology, software engineering, medicine, legal reasoning, education, music, AI) to develop the Compression Calculus — a formal framework comparing surface-level vs. atomic representations — and introduces the Compounding Cascade thesis describing multiplicative efficiency gains across abstraction layers.

Result: A formal framework (Compression Calculus) for comparing representation types and measuring compression gains, plus the Compounding Cascade thesis showing multiplicative (not additive) efficiency gains across abstraction layers. The framework positions LLMs as dynamic fusion engines rather than complete knowledge architectures.

Conclusion: Intelligence is best understood as compression through compositional abstraction, providing a foundation for designing self-evolving knowledge systems that discover, refine, and compose new primitives over time, with implications for expertise, knowledge representation, explainable AI, and adaptive intelligent systems architecture.

Abstract: This paper proposes a theoretical framework for understanding intelligence as a process of atomic compression and compositional reuse. We argue that cognitive, biological, computational, and organizational systems achieve scalable intelligence by decomposing complex phenomena into reusable atomic units that can be recombined into higher-order structures. Drawing on evidence from cognitive science, information theory, evolutionary biology, software engineering, medicine, legal reasoning, education, music, and artificial intelligence, the paper develops the concept of atomic units as fundamental compression layers that support efficiency, transfer, interpretability, and evolvability. The central contribution is the Compression Calculus, a formal framework for comparing surface-level representations with atomic representations and for describing how compression gains compound across abstraction layers. We introduce the Compounding Cascade thesis, according to which each additional layer of abstraction multiplicatively increases representational efficiency rather than merely adding incremental savings. The paper further argues that contemporary AI systems often operate at suboptimal levels of representation, relying on token-level processing or document-level retrieval rather than stable, concept-level atomic structures. In this view, large language models are best understood not as complete knowledge architectures, but as dynamic fusion engines capable of navigating, sequencing, and recombining atomic units. The framework provides a foundation for designing self-evolving knowledge systems that can discover, refine, and compose new primitives over time. By reframing intelligence as compression through compositional abstraction, the paper offers a unifying perspective on expertise, knowledge representation, explainable AI, and the future architecture of adaptive intelligent systems.

</details>


### [239] [A Learning-Rate-Gated Failure of GRPO in a Small Language and Vision-Language Model Web Agent: A Controlled Null and Its Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12640)
*Chengguang Gan,Zhixi Cai,Yunhao Liang,Hanjun Wei,Shiwen Ni,Qinghao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRPO fine-tuning of small (4B-8B) web agents does not improve performance over a strong supervised baseline unless there is substantial headroom (i.e., the sampled policy already outperforms greedy decoding). When improvement is absent, GRPO can degrade (moderate LR) or collapse (high LR) the agent, with distinct mechanistic signatures — degradation localizes to attention/MLP blocks, collapse is distributed, and embedding changes are causally inert. Effective rank tracks capability only at 4B, not 8B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (GRPO) adds genuine skill to small (4B-8B) language and vision-language web agents beyond what the supervised baseline already exhibits.

Method: A controlled grid of 18 runs varying learning rate, KL weight, seed, initialization, and clipping, with paired testing, 25 evaluation seeds, 6 training seeds, both text and Set-of-Marks observations, at 4B and 8B scales. Also a positive control experiment where headroom exists (reward reachable by sampling). Mechanistic analysis using grafting and effective rank measurements.

Result: No configuration credibly improves a strong supervised baseline. Moderate-to-high LRs make text-track performance worse. Under conditions with headroom, success rate rises by 22 points. Degradation localizes to attention/MLP blocks; collapse is distributed; embedding changes are causally inert. Effective rank tracks capability only at 4B.

Conclusion: GRPO helps only when the sampled policy already outperforms the greedy one (headroom exists). Otherwise, it degrades or collapses the agent, with scale-dependent mechanistic differences.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) in particular, is now run routinely on a supervised checkpoint in the hope of producing a stronger agent. We ask whether it adds skill to a small language and vision-language model web agent at the 4B to 8B scale, or whether it mostly reshapes behavior the supervised model already has. Across a control grid of 18 runs that varies learning rate, KL weight, seed, initialization, and clipping, no configuration credibly improves the success rate of a strong supervised baseline on tasks the agent has largely mastered. On the text track, moderate to high learning rates make it credibly worse. The null holds under paired testing, 25 evaluation seeds, 6 training seeds, changes to the recipe, both text and Set-of-Marks screenshot observations, and scaling the backbone to 8B; the credible harm is a text-track finding and is only nominal under Set-of-Marks. To show that the null reflects the setting and not a broken pipeline, we run the identical harness, reward, and recipe on tasks whose reward is reachable by sampling, and there the success rate rises by 22 points with a paired interval that excludes zero. GRPO therefore helps only when there is headroom to climb, meaning the sampled policy already succeeds more often than the greedy one. We then explain the failure. A middle learning rate degrades the agent and a high one collapses it, and the two regimes form a double dissociation: grafting localizes the degrade regime to the attention and MLP blocks, while the collapse regime cannot be traced to any single group, and the embedding change that dominates the weight movement is causally inert. At 4B, effective rank in the late layers tracks capability in both directions; at 8B the two come apart. This coupling is specific to the smaller model, so we report it as scale-dependent.

</details>


### [240] [Internet of Agentic Things: Networked AI Agents for Closed-Loop IoT Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12662)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Internet of Agentic Things (IoAT), a unified architectural framework integrating agentic AI, IoT, cyber-physical systems, Physical AI, edge computing, and digital twins. It formulates IoAT as a coupled workflow-control problem with nested strategic/tactical decision-making using hylomorphic dynamic programming, using smart-building orchestration as a use case, and discusses challenges in safety, security, governance, resilience, and trustworthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of a unified closed-loop orchestration framework that integrates agentic AI with IoT and cyber-physical systems, enabling autonomous perception, reasoning, coordination, and actuation across distributed environments.

Method: The paper formalizes IoAT as a coupled workflow-control problem with nested strategic and tactical decision-making using a hylomorphic dynamic programming framework that links agentic planning with physical execution. The architecture consists of cloud, edge/fog, and physical IoT layers connected through autonomous AI agents.

Result: A formalized architectural framework (IoAT) with a hylomorphic dynamic programming approach to couple agentic planning with physical execution, demonstrated via a smart-building orchestration use case.

Conclusion: IoAT provides a unified orchestration framework for integrating agentic AI with IoT/CPS, but further research is needed to address key challenges in safety, security, governance, resilience, and trustworthy deployment.

Abstract: The paper introduces the Internet of Agentic Things (IoAT), an architectural framework that integrates agentic AI, IoT, cyber-physical systems, Physical AI, edge computing, and digital twins into a unified closed-loop orchestration framework. The proposed architecture consists of cloud, edge/fog, and physical IoT layers connected through autonomous AI agents that perceive, reason, coordinate, and actuate across distributed cyber-physical environments. The paper formalizes IoAT as a coupled workflow-control problem with nested strategic and tactical decision making using a hylomorphic dynamic programming framework that links agentic planning with physical execution. Smart-building orchestration is presented as a representative use case, and key research challenges related to safety, security, governance, resilience, and trustworthy deployment are discussed.

</details>


### [241] [Win by Silence: Deletion Non-Monotonicity, Autonomous Exploitation, and Typed-State Gating in LLM Plan Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12986)
*Aleh Manchuliantsau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Plan evaluators can inadvertently reward strategic plans for omitting details. This paper proves that score-improving omissions exist in expected-value scorers for LLM-generated venture routes, demonstrates a concrete exploit (deleting interior transitions while preserving downstream value), and proposes PCSC/GATE as a defense that detects and neutralizes post-hoc omission splices by acting as a deterministic search-shaping constraint rather than a post-hoc filter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plan evaluators can reward strategic plans for becoming less explicit, creating an omission incentive where a plan scores better only because it omits necessary work. The paper aims to study and address this failure mode in staged expected-value scorers for LLM-generated venture routes.

Method: The paper formulates Proposition 1 (Δ_k = (∏_{i<k} p_i)[c_k + (1-p_k)R_{k+1}]) for score change from deleting an interior transition while retargeting its predecessor. It conducts a frozen 26-route cohort analysis validating all 57 admissible deletions against the analytic identity. It tests a score-seeking optimizer (not told the exploit mechanism) on route restructuring. It applies GATE (the proposed defense) to silence scored routes, observe revisions, and measure covered improvement. An adaptive compiler-aware co-author tests registry-provenance boundary conditions across v1/v1.5 variants.

Result: All 57 admissible deletions matched the analytic identity and threshold sign; every route had ≥1 score-improving deletion. A score-seeking optimizer found baseline-beating uncovered structures in 21/26 routes. GATE refused score release for 26/26 silenced routes with 0/26 honest suspensions; 47/54 next revisions repaired to a covered structure; strict covered improvement rose from 1/26 to 13/26. Obligation-channel evasions remained 6/6 across all four v1/v1.5 conditions; delta-indexed cost floors reduced beat-honest routes from 6/6 to 3/6 and fundability-by-silence from 5/6 to 0/6 without establishing semantic completeness.

Conclusion: If a plan scores better only because it omits necessary work, the plan did not improve; the evaluation created an omission incentive. PCSC detects and neutralizes post-hoc omission splices over model-mediated typed-state records. GATE acts as a deterministic search-shaping constraint (not merely a post-hoc filter) in the cooperative setting tested, though it does not verify semantic completeness or real-world quality of arbitrary LLM-generated strategies.

Abstract: Plan evaluators can reward a strategic plan for becoming less explicit. This paper studies that failure in a staged expected-value scorer for LLM-generated venture routes. Proposition 1 gives the score change from deleting an interior transition while retargeting its predecessor and retaining downstream value: Delta_k = (prod_{i<k} p_i)[c_k + (1 - p_k)R_{k+1}]. On a frozen 26-route cohort, all 57 admissible deletions matched the analytic identity and threshold sign, and every route had at least one score-improving deletion. A score-seeking optimizer, allowed to restructure routes but not told the exploit mechanism, found baseline-beating uncovered structures in 21/26 routes. GATE refused score release for 26/26 silenced routes with 0/26 honest suspensions; after refusal, 47/54 next revisions repaired to a covered structure, and strict covered improvement rose from 1/26 to 13/26. An adaptive compiler-aware co-author exposed the registry-provenance boundary: obligation-channel evasions remained 6/6 across all four v1/v1.5 conditions, while delta-indexed cost floors reduced beat-honest routes from 6/6 to 3/6 and fundability-by-silence from 5/6 to 0/6 without establishing semantic completeness. If a plan scores better only because it omits necessary work, the plan did not improve; the evaluation created an omission incentive. PCSC detects and neutralizes post-hoc omission splices over model-mediated typed-state records. In the cooperative setting tested, GATE acts as a deterministic search-shaping constraint, not merely a post-hoc filter. It does not verify the semantic completeness or real-world quality of arbitrary LLM-generated strategies.

</details>


### [242] [MaxSAT-Based Feedback for Guiding Vision-Language Models in Sudoku](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12711)
*Pedro Orvalho,Guillem Alenyà,Felip Manyà*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a neuro-symbolic approach that integrates a Maximum Satisfiability (MaxSAT) oracle to validate and refine VLM-generated puzzle solutions, improving logical consistency and solving rates on Sudoku tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs lack explicit mechanisms for enforcing logical consistency in structured reasoning tasks, often generating assignments that violate underlying constraints.

Method: A neuro-symbolic framework where VLM-generated candidate placements are encoded as soft clauses in a partial MaxSAT formulation, with Sudoku constraints as hard clauses. The MaxSAT solver identifies the largest mutually consistent subset of assignments, and inconsistencies are translated into structured textual and visual feedback for iterative refinement.

Result: MaxSAT-based feedback improves logical consistency and increases the number of solved Sudoku instances across multiple open-source and closed-access VLMs, especially in full-board refinement mode.

Conclusion: Symbolic optimisation via MaxSAT can enhance the reliability of vision-language reasoning in constraint-satisfaction tasks.

Abstract: Vision--Language Models (VLMs) have recently demonstrated promising performance on structured visual reasoning tasks, including grid-based puzzles. However, despite strong perceptual capabilities, these models lack explicit mechanisms for enforcing logical consistency and frequently generate assignments that violate underlying constraints. In this paper, we propose a neuro-symbolic approach that integrates formal constraint reasoning into the VLM solving process via a Maximum Satisfiability (MaxSAT) oracle. Rather than computing solutions directly, the symbolic component acts as a consistency validator and refinement engine. Candidate placements generated by the VLM are encoded as soft clauses in a partial MaxSAT formulation, while Sudoku constraints remain hard clauses. When inconsistencies arise, the MaxSAT solver identifies a largest mutually consistent subset of assignments, which is then translated into structured textual and visual feedback to guide subsequent refinements. We evaluate our approach on a Sudoku dataset across multiple open-source and closed-access VLMs. Results show that MaxSAT-based feedback improves logical consistency and increases the number of solved instances, particularly in full-board refinement mode. These findings demonstrate that symbolic optimisation can enhance the reliability of vision-language reasoning.

</details>


### [243] [Do AI Agents Know When a Task Is Simple? Toward Complexity-Aware Reasoning and Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13034)
*Junjie Yin,Xinyu Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies that LLM agents over-consume context and compute by not estimating task difficulty first. It formalizes minimum-sufficient execution and proposes E3 (Estimate, Execute, Expand), which cuts cost by 85%, tokens by 91%, and inspected files by 92% while matching 100% success on a deterministic benchmark, and outperforms baselines on a live gpt-4o agent editing real code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents automatically over-read files and dependencies for simple tasks (e.g., one-line edits become full codebase audits) because they lack task-aware execution-scope estimation—the ability to judge a task's actual difficulty and information requirements before committing resources.

Method: The authors formalize minimum-sufficient execution and the Agent Cognitive Redundancy Ratio (ACRR), then propose E3: the agent estimates an initial operating point, executes a minimum viable path, and expands scope only when verification fails. They evaluate on MSE-Bench (121 deterministic edits in a simulator) and LLM-Case (live gpt-4o editing a real open-source library with pytest oracle).

Result: E3 matches the strongest baseline's 100% success on MSE-Bench while cutting cost by 85%, tokens by 91%, and inspected files by 92%; beats a strong adaptive retrieval baseline by 16%. On live gpt-4o, it is the leanest and fastest policy at comparable success; the only shortfall was a provider rate-limit, not a wrong edit.

Conclusion: Task-aware execution-scope estimation is a critical missing capability in LLM agents; E3 demonstrates that minimum-sufficient execution drastically reduces redundancy without sacrificing accuracy, positioning this as a step toward engineering-grounded AI (EGAI) where agent effort aligns with the engineering reality of the task.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate multi-step engineering and informatics workflows, yet they rarely ask how much effort a task actually requires. They often follow a maximum-context-first strategy--re-reading files and dependencies they have already seen--turning a one-line edit into a small code-base audit. We argue the missing capability is task-aware execution-scope estimation: judging a task's difficulty, the information it truly needs, and the shortest reliable path before committing budget. We formalize minimum-sufficient execution and the Agent Cognitive Redundancy Ratio (ACRR), and propose E3 (Estimate, Execute, Expand): the agent estimates an initial operating point, executes a minimum viable path, and expands scope only when verification fails. On MSE-Bench--a deterministic benchmark of 121 edits in a capability-controlled simulator--E3 matches the strongest baseline's 100% success while cutting cost by 85%, tokens by 91%, and inspected files by 92%, and further beats a strong adaptive retrieval baseline by 16%; the gains survive held-out instruction wording and essentially every cost weighting. A companion real-model harness (LLM-Case) corroborates the effect on a live gpt-4o agent editing a real open-source library, with every candidate patch graded by actually running the project's real pytest suite against a measured oracle: the over-reading is milder but real, and E3 is the leanest and fastest policy at comparable task success--its one shortfall a provider rate-limit, not a wrong edit. We frame this as a controlled probe of execution redundancy, not a measurement of any deployed agent, and position task-aware execution as a step toward engineering-grounded AI (EGAI)--agents whose effort is anchored in the engineering reality of the task. We release the framework and benchmark.

</details>


### [244] [LLMs Can See the Smoke but not the Fire: Evaluating Abductive Reasoning with Elenchos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12733)
*Julius Steiglechner,Lucas Mahler,Gabriele Lohmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can detect behavioral changes in mutated formal systems but struggle to identify the specific latent mutations causing those changes, revealing a detection-attribution dissociation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To measure and understand the capacity of LLMs for abductive inference—inferring latent hypotheses that explain observed behavior—which remains poorly understood despite LLMs' strengths in pattern recognition and text generation.

Method: Introduces Elenchos, a generative evaluation framework that treats abductive reasoning as a structural inverse problem: given a reference formal system (e.g., lambda-calculus) and a potentially mutated counterpart, agents must detect whether a mutation occurred and infer the specific rule modifications causing behavioral differences.

Result: Frontier and mid-tier LLMs show a consistent detection-attribution dissociation: they can detect system alterations but fail to identify latent mutations; performance drops substantially under interacting mutations; preliminary evidence shows diminishing returns from increased inference-time reasoning.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit a fundamental limitation in abductive reasoning, particularly attribution under complex mutation scenarios, with limited benefit from additional reasoning compute—suggesting a structural bottleneck that warrants further investigation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) excel at pattern recognition and text generation, but their capacity for abductive inference - inferring latent hypotheses that explain observed behavior - remains poorly understood. Here, we introduce Elenchos (named after the Socratic method of cross-examination), a generative evaluation framework that measures abductive reasoning as a structural inverse problem. Given a reference formal system, such as the lambda-calculus, and a potentially mutated counterpart, agents must determine whether a mutation has occurred and infer the rule modifications responsible for the resulting behavioral differences. Evaluating frontier and mid-tier LLMs reveals a consistent detection-attribution dissociation: models often recognize that a system has been altered but struggle to identify the latent mutations causing the observed discrepancies. Performance degrades substantially under interacting mutations, where models frequently recover only a subset of the underlying mutations. Preliminary evidence also suggests diminishing returns from increased inference-time reasoning, with only modest improvements under larger reasoning budgets, though this finding requires further validation.

</details>


### [245] [Tracing Agentic Failure from the Flow of Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12747)
*Samuel Yeh,Yiwen Zhu,Shaleen Deep,Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OAT is a lightweight, unsupervised method for failure attribution in LLM-based agentic systems that trains only on successful trajectories using neural controlled differential equations, achieving 200-5000× speedup over prompting-based methods with +20%/+7% F1 improvements in in-domain/out-of-distribution settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing failure attribution methods for LLM agentic systems rely either on expensive prompting-based pipelines or require costly step-level error annotations on failure trajectories, which are difficult to scale. A practical model should be lightweight and trainable without step-level supervision on failure data.

Method: OAT casts failure attribution as one-class learning using neural controlled differential equations (NCDEs) to model the dynamical pattern of successful trajectories in latent space. It trains exclusively on successful trajectories. At inference, each step in a failure trajectory gets an anomaly score based on deviation from learned successful dynamics, forming a set of identified error steps.

Result: Trained on only 100 successful trajectories, OAT is 200-5000× faster than prompting-based baselines and consistently outperforms them with +20% F1 on in-domain datasets and +7% F1 on out-of-distribution datasets.

Conclusion: OAT demonstrates a promising and efficient direction for diagnosing agentic system failures through unsupervised, lightweight anomaly detection without requiring costly failure trajectory annotations.

Abstract: Failure attribution for LLM-based agentic systems, i.e., identifying which steps in a failure trajectory caused the task to fail, is critical for debugging and improving these systems. Existing approaches either rely on prompting-based pipelines, which are computationally expensive, or require post-training on failure trajectories with step-level error annotations, which are costly to collect and difficult to scale. We argue that a practical failure attribution model should be lightweight and trainable without step-level supervision on failure data. To this end, we address unsupervised failure attribution, i.e., training exclusively on successful trajectories and identifying error steps at inference time given a failure trajectory. We propose OAT, which casts this problem as one-class learning with neural controlled differential equations, modeling the dynamical pattern of successful trajectories in latent space. At inference time, each step in a failure trajectory is assigned an anomaly score based on its deviation from the dynamics learned on successful trajectories, which is then used to form a set of error steps. With training on only 100 successful trajectories, experiments show that OAT is 200--5000 $\times$ faster than prompting-based baselines, and, at the same time, consistently outperforms them in both in-domain and out-of-distribution datasets with +20% and +7% F1 scores, respectively, demonstrating that OAT is a promising and efficient direction for diagnosing agentic system failures.

</details>


### [246] [Accuracy and Normalized Accuracy under Length Bias: Analysis, Guidelines, and a Bayesian Alternative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12767)
*Koen Oostermeijer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multiple-choice benchmarks using conditional log-probability scoring suffer from length bias. Standard scoring penalizes longer answers, while length-normalized scoring over-corrects. The paper introduces Bayesian accuracy, which uses an explicit prior over answer length to remove linear length effects, consistently reducing length bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Length bias in likelihood-based multiple-choice evaluation leads to unfair scoring: standard log-probability scoring penalizes longer answers, while length-normalized scoring over-corrects, favoring longer answers. A principled solution is needed.

Method: The authors analyze standard and length-normalized scoring rules, characterizing when each is appropriate and how length biases depend on the distribution of completion lengths. They propose Bayesian accuracy, which computes the posterior probability of each candidate under an explicit prior over answer length, removing linear length effects without additional forward passes.

Result: Bayesian accuracy consistently exhibits lower empirical length bias than both standard and length-normalized accuracy across benchmarks and few-shot settings, while requiring no additional forward passes.

Conclusion: Bayesian accuracy serves as a drop-in replacement for likelihood-based multiple-choice evaluation that effectively mitigates length bias without over-correcting.

Abstract: Multiple-choice benchmarks that rank candidate completions by conditional log-probability suffer from a length bias: because log-probabilities sum over tokens, longer answers tend to be penalized relative to shorter ones in practice. A common mitigation is to normalize scores by completion length, but we show empirically that this heuristic frequently over-corrects, introducing a bias toward longer answers instead. We first analyze these scoring rules, characterizing when standard and length-normalized accuracy are appropriate and how their length biases depend on the distribution of completion lengths. Motivated by this analysis, we introduce \emph{Bayesian accuracy}, a scoring rule that computes the posterior probability of each candidate under an explicit prior over answer length, thereby removing linear length effects. Bayesian accuracy is a drop-in replacement for likelihood-based multiple-choice evaluation, requires no additional forward passes, and consistently exhibits lower empirical length bias than both standard and length-normalized accuracy across benchmarks and few-shot settings.

</details>


### [247] [Who Grades the Grader? Co-Evolving Evaluation Metrics and Skills for Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12790)
*Xing Zhang,Guanghui Wang,Yanwei Cui,Ziyuan Li,Wei Qiu,Bing Zhu,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Double Ratchet, a co-evolutionary system that jointly evolves a transparent, inspectable evaluation metric alongside a skill-learning agent loop, enabling self-improvement even when no reliable ground-truth metric exists. The approach preserves 88–110% of the lift achieved with an ideal metric, and includes safety mechanisms (anchor discipline, outer audits) to prevent metric collapse and gaming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agent systems require a reliable evaluation metric to improve, but such metrics are often unavailable in real-world applications. The paper addresses this fundamental gap by showing metrics themselves can be evolved rather than assumed.

Method: The metric loop searches compositions of small drawback detectors under a full evolutionary lifecycle, trained against a ten-item anchored reference set, regularized by consensus over unlabeled outputs, and audited via a held-out anchor. Double Ratchet co-evolves this metric with a lifecycle-managed skill loop across code generation (MBPP+), enterprise text-to-SQL (Spider 2.0-Snow), and reference-free report generation.

Result: Double Ratchet retains 88–110% of the held-out lift achieved by the same skill loop with ground truth or best available rubric. Removing anchor guards collapses the metric into a vacuous detector; independent judges caught gaming of the report rubric, and task-aware judges preferred evolved outputs over baselines in 77% of decided pairs.

Conclusion: The failure-expecting architecture of co-evolving metrics with skills, anchored by reference sets and audited externally, is the right default wherever no reliable automatic verifier exists.

Abstract: Self-evolving agent systems improve by creating, revising, and retiring their own skills, but every such loop rests on a hidden assumption: a reliable evaluation metric already exists. In many real applications it does not. We make three claims. First, metrics can be \emph{evolved}: our metric loop searches compositions of small drawback detectors under a full evolutionary lifecycle, trained to agree with a ten-item anchored reference set, regularized by consensus over unlabeled outputs, and audited against a held-out anchor it never reads, yielding a transparent, inspectable metric rather than an opaque judge. Second, since no metric exists to beat, the yardstick is recovering what an accurate metric would have enabled, and \emph{Double Ratchet}, our co-evolution of the metric with a lifecycle-managed skill loop, does so: across code generation (MBPP+), enterprise text-to-SQL (Spider~2.0-Snow), and reference-free report generation, it retains 88--110\% of the held-out lift achieved by the same skill loop driven by ground truth or the best available rubric. Third, safety comes from anchor discipline plus outer audits: removing anchor guards collapses the metric into a vacuous detector while removing the lifecycle does not; and when evolved skills gamed the report rubric, an independent judge caught it, one detector repaired it, and a task-aware judge then preferred the evolved outputs over the pre-evolution baseline in 77\% of decided pairs. We argue this failure-expecting architecture is the right default wherever no reliable automatic verifier exists.

</details>


### [248] [Visual Access Boundaries in Vision-Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12815)
*Hiroto Osaka,Shohei Taniguchi,Gouki Minegishi,Kai Yamashita,Masahiro Suzuki,Yutaka Matsuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Chain-of-Thought prompting in Vision-Language Models does not primarily improve performance by extending direct image-token access during reasoning, but rather by extending language-side computation over image-derived hidden-state information. CoT gains are constrained by perceptual readout reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what is extended when VLMs generate longer reasoning traces during Chain-of-Thought prompting—specifically whether CoT requires continued access to image tokens or operates over visual information already made available earlier in the forward pass.

Method: Introduce Visual Access Sweep, a causal intervention that masks attention from generated-token queries to image-token keys across layer depth and generation time, defining the Visual Access Boundary (VAB) as the minimal access region preserving task accuracy. Experiments conducted on Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3 models (six configurations) comparing no-CoT direct answering and CoT prompting.

Result: Both no-CoT and CoT prompting exhibit finite VABs. In Qwen2.5-VL-32B and InternVL3 (14B, 38B), CoT's VAB layer differs from no-CoT boundary by at most two layers despite much longer generations.
